# Agentics - Changelog

Entries in reverse-chronological order.
Format: `date | category | bump | breaking? | description`

Categories: `template`, `conventions`, `softeng`, `repo`
Bump: `patch` (fixes, clarifies, backfills, or propagates something already intended) / `minor` (introduces a rule, file, procedure, or section that didn't exist before). See `CONTRIBUTING.md` § Versioning. Applies going forward only; entries before this field existed aren't retroactively tagged.
Breaking: `yes` (downstream copies require manual update) / `no` (additive or clarification only)

---

## Unreleased changes


## Released

### 0.20.0 - 2026-09-01

- `2026-09-01 | conventions | minor | yes | do-not-hard-wrap-a-commit-body`: the developer, sending a screenshot of a commit message rendered in his own client: *"been noticing some of the commit messages have line breaks that segment paragraphs, and I don't think they're necessary... worth at least documenting in agentics as well."* Reported by the Arranger session, and **the screenshot is the argument**: bodies hard-wrapped at roughly 72, the traditional convention, met a client wrapping at its own width, so the breaks landed inside the re-wrapped lines and orphaned words, "The terminology" ending a line with "table defined the old premise" beginning the next. Hard wrapping is a fix for a reader that does not wrap, and against one that does it strictly degrades, because two wrapping passes at different widths cannot agree. **Recorded as a deliberate departure from the documented git convention rather than silently**, so that nobody restores the 72-column wrap as a fix; the convention is a holdover from mail-based patch review, where the reader did no wrapping of its own. One correction to the reported scope, made by checking rather than accepting: `git log` does not reflow, it prints the body verbatim into a terminal that soft-wraps it. The conclusion is unchanged and the premise is now accurate, which matters because someone will check it. The subject line stays hard-limited, since nothing reflows it. Their two attached cautions both shipped: reflowing an existing message is a rewrite and the SHA changes exactly as for a content change, so it is safe only while unpushed and the rule against handing out a SHA from unpushed work applies without exception; and **a message-only rewrite is verified to be message-only**, by confirming afterwards that the base commit, the `HEAD` tree and the working tree are unchanged and that rename detection still reports what it did before. That check is cheap and it is the difference between reflowing text and silently altering a tree.

- `2026-09-01 | conventions | minor | yes | assert-what-the-code-should-do-not-what-it-does`: the developer framed this as belonging to how agentics BDD works rather than to any project, verbatim: *"the realization of 'asserts what the code does, not what it should' being wrong is huge and directly inline with how Agentics BDD should work: tests should test adversarially, not just happy path."* Reported by the Arranger session with a small complete instance. `computeAggregateServerStatus({})` returned `HEALTHY`, since no catalogues means none failed, and a Kubernetes readiness probe reads it, so a replica that can serve nothing goes into rotation. The test read `is healthy for an empty set of catalogues (nothing has failed)`, and **the parenthetical is the tell**: it restates the mechanism as the justification. It had passed since the function was written and would have passed forever, because it was pinned to the behaviour rather than to the requirement, so fixing the defect meant editing the test that certified it. **Their reason this is a BDD point rather than a coverage point is the part that makes it a rule**: coverage was complete, and the empty case was the first thing the author thought of, which makes it worse than an untested branch rather than better, since an untested branch is visibly absent while a test asserting the wrong expectation is indistinguishable from a passing one and carries the authority of having been considered. The adversarial question shipped as they phrased it, not "is this branch covered" but **"if this assertion is wrong, what breaks, and would I notice?"** Scoped to degenerate inputs with most force, since empty, zero, absent and unknown are where a permissive default hides and where the code's own behaviour is most likely an accident rather than a decision. Both of their cautions are in, and the first is what keeps the rule usable: **it governs the expectation's provenance, not its agreement with the code**, so a regression pin or a snapshot is legitimate and a requirement-derived expectation that happens to match is fine. Their second instance from the same day is kept as evidence the family is real rather than one defect: an assertion that a clause was present in a compiled query passed while unable to distinguish a clause that restricts from one under `must_not` doing the opposite. Placed beside the existing rule that a test building its own input cannot discover the input it was never given, which is the same family from the other end.

- `2026-09-01 | conventions | minor | yes | an-assertion-points-at-a-typing-gap-upstream`: the developer's instruction, verbatim, was to avoid type assertions here and in any other project, and the sentence that decides the rule is the one after it: *"would it help if we fixed buildQuery?"* He did not ask for the assertion to be removed, he asked whether the thing forcing it could be fixed. **That reframing is the whole rule**, since deleting an assertion locally either loses type safety or relocates the same lie, while fixing what forced it removes every instance at once. Reported by the Arranger session with numbers a "remove assertions" rule would never have produced: a type annotated with a bare generic, itself a type error, was forcing seventeen assertions at call sites and supplying the argument removed all of them, while an untyped JavaScript module whose `= []` default inferred as `never[]` forced the rest and a JSDoc `@param` block fixed it with no rewrite and no conversion. Twenty-two gone, and the package's type-error count fell by two. **The finding worth more than the count is what typing the parameter honestly then exposed**: a call site passing a possibly-undefined value, not a runtime bug since a guard above it throws, but an invariant living in a function the type system cannot see through with nothing recording it. The assertions had been hiding it, which is what an assertion does in general, silencing the question rather than answering it. Both their boundaries shipped, and each prevents a specific harm: `any` at a genuine external boundary is a different thing and is not swept in, and fixing an upstream type can legitimately **raise** the error count by exposing real problems, so phrasing the rule as "assertions removed" or "errors down" invites re-suppression.

- `2026-09-01 | conventions | minor | yes | documentation-belongs-in-the-declarations-own-block`: the developer, pointing at two lines of a file he had open: *"think I'd prefer this type of inline comments (29, 31) as tsdoc material."* Per-property doc comments inside a destructured parameter's inline type become `@param` on the function: identical information, one place, and the place an IDE surfaces. **The part worth writing down is that applying it needed three cases separated**, because a rule stated only as "no inline doc comments" over-applies in two directions. Function parameters move to `@param`. **Members of a named type cannot**, because TSDoc has no member tag: `@property` is a JSDoc-ism and is not TSDoc, so a small type gets prose in its own block while a large interface keeps per-member comments, since consolidating forty members into a paragraph would make it unreadable. And a `/** */` on a statement inside a function body is not documentation at all but an implementation comment wearing doc syntax, which becomes `//`. The reporting session named the third as the one to keep if only one survived, on the grounds that it is mechanically checkable and unambiguously wrong rather than a placement preference, and that judgement is recorded with it. They also checked the `@property` claim against a real corpus rather than asserting it (`@param` 145, `@returns` 65, `@example` 23, `@see` 6, `@remarks` 6, `@throws` 5, `@property` 0), which is the difference between a rule that survives contact and one that gets quietly ignored the first time it is wrong.

- `2026-09-01 | conventions | minor | no | a-diff-you-never-took-is-not-a-comparison`: second finding from the Lectern session, and they drew the distinction themselves: the comment-location gap was under-searching one moment in time, while this is skipping the comparison entirely and asserting a diff that was never taken. Asked to look again at a pull request they had already reviewed, they ran one command, got `OPEN, 2, <timestamp>`, and reported "still 2 commits, same as before... nothing appears to have changed". **There was no recorded baseline for "before"**: their original review had logged a file-and-line diffstat and no commit count, so the comparison was against an impression rather than a stored fact. Both SHAs differed from the originals. The developer: "same pr, more comments and changes." A real comparison then found a new commit and a new reply that had been sitting there throughout. Three parts shipped. A question about change needs a baseline that was actually recorded, and where none exists that is the answer to give rather than something to paper over. Review time is when to record what a later comparison will need, which a diffstat does not support and commit SHAs do. And **compare identities, not counts**, since a count is a lossy projection and an unchanged commit count is fully consistent with every commit having been replaced. **The stable-looking number is what makes it dangerous rather than merely wrong**: a count that moves prompts a second look while one that holds reads as confirmation, so this is a control whose failure looks like success arriving as a coincidence in a metric rather than as a bug in a script. Second time today that a plausible-looking value confirmed a wrong conclusion, after a growth gate reported `+0` across a batch of nearly seventeen thousand characters because it was measuring committed state.

- `2026-09-01 | conventions | minor | no | not-posted-is-a-claim-about-your-search`: reported by the Lectern session with the commands and the developer's reply verbatim. Asked to check whether an earlier review finding had been addressed, they ran `gh pr view 450 --json comments` (empty) and `gh api .../pulls/450/comments` (two results, both a different finding), and reported that the comment "was never actually posted to the PR at all, not inline, not as a general comment". The developer: "i thought that was the comment i posted on my review." A third endpoint, `gh api .../pulls/450/reviews`, returned it. **GitHub keeps review feedback in three separate stores and the natural check reaches two**, so the union of two was reported as though it covered the surface. The class is already recorded here as asserting an absence from an unestablished search; what this adds is the mechanical remedy, which is the enumeration itself, since a rule to search thoroughly cannot be executed while a list of three endpoints can. **The sharper half is which one gets missed.** Submitting a review with a summary puts that text in the review body and nowhere else, so a reviewer's main point is precisely the comment least likely to be found by searching the other two: the failure is biased toward losing the substantive feedback rather than a random third of it. Written with the host named rather than assumed, since other hosts partition this differently or not at all and what travels is the shape rather than the endpoint list.

- `2026-09-01 | conventions | minor | yes | a-session-entry-earns-its-place-against-the-pr`: reported by the Lyric and Maestro session with a checkable instance and a correction from the developer that inverts the intuitive fix. Four bullets restating what changed file by file, at the same detail as the PR description and commits, were flagged in review by that repository's maintainer: reading the file to check for secrets before merge, he found nothing he had not already read and said so. **A measured cost, a human reviewer's time, with no return.** The first proposed fix was to trim the file to a one-line pointer at the PR, and the developer rejected it on grounds worth quoting: GitHub-hosted content is disposable and platform-dependent while git-committed content is durable and portable, so pointing a committed file at hosted content makes the durable artifact depend on the disposable one, backwards from why session files exist. **"I do not want us to rely on GH functionalities for documentation of codebases. Git is fine, GH is not."** The frozen-file test already in this file reaches the same place by another route, since a reader holding the repository and no host access gets nothing from a pointer. So the fix is a higher content bar rather than less content: admit only what is unrecoverable from the PR, the commits, or the code, and check each point against the PR before including it. This extends "reference an artifact, never restate it", which was scoped to content the work itself produced, to the case where the artifact is a PR description.

- `2026-09-01 | conventions | minor | no | stating-a-check-explicitly-does-not-make-it-fire`: reported by the Lyric and Maestro session, and it is evidence against an assumption this corpus leans on rather than against one rule. `review-conduct.md` § Draft, never post already requires the discrete style check on anything about to leave your control, and already names "no dashes" as **the recurring case**; that session held the same rule a second time in its own global context, tied to a prior incident of exactly this shape. Holding it twice, with the reason on record, it drafted a review reply containing two em dashes and presented it unchecked, and the developer caught it. **Not a coverage gap, a reliability gap**, and the counterexample lands at the strongest possible point, since a rule cannot be more explicit than one naming its own recurrence. The diagnosis this repository already had and had not applied here: the dash rule *has* a mechanical form and `check-consistency.sh` runs it, but only over files, while the recurring failure is in outbound text that never becomes a file. **The enforcement covers the case that does not fail and misses the one that does**, and a second instance is already recorded in the developer's own global context from a project with no agentics adoption at all. Short of making a draft into a file, the mitigation shipped is to report the count alongside the draft: it does not force the check, but it turns a silent omission into a visible fabrication, since a count you did not compute must be invented rather than merely skipped. Same trade a commit hook makes in converting forgetting into deciding.

- `2026-09-01 | conventions | minor | yes | for-may-name-a-path-and-resolution-runs-through-owns`: reported by the Singularity session. The board required `for:` to name a label, which assumes the owner is registered and leaves no route when they are not: needing Score, and finding no `Members` entry containing it, they posted `for: Score`, which was unresolvable by construction since no session could self-select against a label that did not exist. Their framing of the options is what makes it a gap rather than a mistake, because all three were bad: an unresolvable non-label, a repository path breaking the label rule, or not posting and having no route at all. **The path was the good option and the rule was what forbade it.** `for:` now accepts a label, a component, or a path, resolving by the same longest-prefix rule everything else here uses. The closure is that the answer already existed and neither party saw it: `main:` shipped hours earlier, Usher holds `overture`, so `for: overture/score` would have been answerable the moment it was posted rather than waiting on a conferral, which is precisely what a head is for. They also reported, unprompted, that they could not tell whether their entry had worked, since the owner registered and posted a reciprocal entry the same day and arriving independently is indistinguishable from having found it.

- `2026-09-01 | conventions | minor | no | a-stale-read-invalidates-everything-resting-on-it`: three findings from the Singularity session, attributed per item at their insistence. **The blast radius of a stale read is every claim resting on that read, not the one that happened to get caught**; the observation and phrasing are the unlabelled SoftEng session's and they asked for it to be credited rather than folded in. The incident is Singularity's: they read a convention fresh and in full, the uncommitted delta then grew from 22 insertions to 58, and that delta both added `main:` and redefined `assigned:`, so two claims in one message were wrong. Corrected on the first, they diagnosed the stale read precisely and then scoped the fix to the caught claim while the second sat untouched in the same message, which is the part worth the rule: the diagnosis was right and the response was still too narrow. **Recategorising is not verifying** is the sharper sub-case, since a later correction moved the invalid claim between categories without asking whether it was true, and a correction arrives wearing the credibility of having just corrected something. **A retraction is not complete until it reaches everywhere the claim went, and only its author knows that list**, which is the one item here that says what to do rather than what to avoid: a withdrawn finding had already reached the developer by a second route, and only the author of that second report knew it. It demonstrated itself in transit, and that is why it is recorded rather than the rule alone. They nearly withheld it because the other session had written "nothing needed back", and the thing nearly withheld was the only item that session could not have recovered independently. So a sign-off is the sender's estimate of what is outstanding on their side and never a statement about what you hold: **if you have something whose absence they cannot detect, it survives their sign-off.**

- `2026-09-01 | conventions | minor | yes | attribution-in-prose-and-the-scope-that-could-not-reach-it`: the developer's instruction, verbatim: refrain from "agreed with X session" or from dates like "corrected on XXXX", and where something is genuinely needed, "in accordance with Usher's model design" is better. **Reference the thing, never the conversation.** § Say what changed already named "which session or review surfaced it" and "the artifact's own edit history" as procedural narration to cut, so the rule existed; it was scoped to comments, commit messages, review comments and drafts. The Arranger session swept twelve instances across five files and supplied the decisive evidence: every one sat in a design document, an auth roadmap, a scoped debt index or a subsystem index, and **not one was in a comment, a commit message or a review**. A rule that already covered the behaviour could not reach a single case that prompted it, which is a better argument for widening a scope than a new section would have been, and is the part worth keeping, since "already covered and still failed" is what justifies touching a rule that looks fine. **Their three-way split is what makes it a rule rather than a preference**, and without it one sweep under-removes and the next over-removes: attribution credits an edit and goes, sourcing names where a claim came from and goes once verified locally, and a confidence declaration records that a claim is *not* verified here and **stays**, because deleting one silently promotes second-hand to first-hand while looking identical to an attribution. A peer running the same sweep removed fourteen, four of which were attributions wearing a source's name. Dates split on whether they stamp the world or the document. **The reason it outranks tidiness is that attribution lands on the stalest content**: a line saying another session agreed reads as settled, so nobody re-reads it, and their worked case is a contract table whose three prescriptions were all downstream of a premise since corrected, agreed to by a session working from that same wrong premise.

- `2026-09-01 | conventions | minor | no | a-heard-that-cannot-be-matched-against-the-re`: `heard:` records that someone answered and binds nothing to what they answered about, while `from:` clears on reading it, so a `heard:` written in good faith about an adjacent topic destroys the entry and the clearer cannot detect it. Confirmed on this file's own board, by this session: an entry reading `re: writing-style.md: session/date attribution in doc prose` received a `heard:` citing three convention changes that all concerned identity and registration, because a different question had arrived in the same exchange. The answer was genuine and the topic was never discussed. The entry was cleared, and the only reason anyone knows is the Arranger session running a grep against this repository and reporting a discrepancy rather than taking the record at face value. **The same failure class this week has been about, arriving inside the mechanism built to track it**: a record that reads as accurate, is not, and is trusted because nothing invites re-reading. Two fixes. A `heard:` names what satisfies the request in the terms the `re:` uses, so that a reader can match the two, and says plainly where the request was not met. And **posting a need you cannot judge satisfied makes you a relayer whoever it originated with**: `via:` already covers acting for another agent, and the same reasoning covers carrying the developer's proposal, which is what happened here, since the poster knew the entry existed and not what would satisfy it. Their observation is the one that generalizes: the entry survived exactly as long as nobody checked, and was cleared by the party structurally least able to check it.

- `2026-09-01 | tooling | patch | no | the-checker-did-not-know-about-main`: `main:` shipped and `scripts/check-agent-index.sh` was never taught it, so the script kept inferring family heads from a bare top-level `owns` path, which is exactly the conflation `main` exists to undo. Narrowing one entry from `iMicroSeq` to `iMicroSeq/portal-ui` moved its headship into `main`, after which the inference found no root, printed nothing, and still reported INTEGRITY ok. **A peer read that output and concluded the family had lost its owner**, which is the natural reading of silence, and reported it rather than acting on it. The script now reads declared heads and prints the nesting, and the inference is inverted into a flag: a bare top-level `owns` with no `main` beside it is now surfaced as probably the old conflation, which immediately found a live instance in `PCGL`. The failure is one this repository already names: a schema change reaching the convention and its bootstrap copy, both of which a drift check guards, while the tool that reads the same file has no guard at all and went stale silently.

- `2026-09-01 | conventions | minor | no | elimination-over-the-registry-is-not-evidence`: this session narrowed a search for a labelled peer by eliminating candidates sharing a workspace prefix, concluded exactly one remained, and named it. An unlabelled session bridging the same request refused the arithmetic and was right. **Membership here is owners-only by design**, so most running sessions appear in no entry, and the count of prefixed sessions and the count of declared owners are not two views of one set. Their pigeonhole runs the other way: five entries declare the workspace, three sessions carried the prefix, so at least two owners were not running, which makes absence the common case rather than the exception and the last remaining address not the last remaining owner. The rule that the registry is deliberately incomplete already forbids this and records a case where eliminating to two candidates was answered by a fourth session that owned nothing. **The sharper finding is the shape of the error rather than the arithmetic.** The same message carried the caveat that a label may not survive a restart and the inference that one remaining candidate must hold the label, and those cannot both be load-bearing. This is not an unstated assumption, which is the usual failure; it is a stated one, written out in full, and then not applied to the very next paragraph. Post-restart the prohibition is stronger than usual rather than weaker, because a persisted entry and a live conferral have come apart across the whole board.

- `2026-08-31 | conventions | patch | no | assigned-is-a-marker-and-the-date-was-never-read`: the developer asked what `assigned` is for and whether it is necessary, and the only consumer settles it. `scripts/check-agent-index.sh` reads the field in two places and both test presence (`'assigned' in f`), never the value, which matches what this file already claimed the field does: it records that an entry was conferred rather than inferred, and an entry without it is provisional and correctable by anyone. **The distinction is load-bearing and the date has never carried meaning.** Requiring one forces a false statement whenever the conferral was not witnessed by whoever writes the entry, which happened hours earlier in this same batch: migrating `Stage` off the pre-registry schema offered a choice between inventing a conferral date and omitting the field, the second of which marks a genuinely conferred entry as provisional. Both are wrong, and a required field whose value is unknowable to the person filling it will always produce one of them. The date is now optional and **never compared**, with `yes` written where the conferral was not witnessed. **The first attempt at this rule prescribed leaving the field empty, which the same file forbids four paragraphs away**, and that is recorded rather than quietly corrected because the mechanism is worth more than the fix: the new rule was checked against its own purpose and never against the rules already in the file it was landing in. Entries sit outside a code fence, so markdown collapses consecutive lines and an empty field has nothing holding its boundary open, which is exactly why `heard:` is omitted rather than left valueless. Omitting is not available here, since the presence is the whole claim, so a placeholder value is the only correct form. The instance survived only by position, being the last line of its entry, and the file had been reordered once already that day. That last clause is not decoration: this file withdrew tie-breaking by start time after it failed three times in one day, so a comparable number sitting inside a registry that forbids comparison is an invitation standing next to its own warning sign. The field also looks vestigial rather than designed, since the schema it replaced carried `updated`, a freshness marker with a genuine time-based purpose, and `assigned` inherited the shape without inheriting a use for the value. No schema change and no adopter action, which mattered because adopters carrying an index have already taken two field changes in this batch: a valueless `assigned:` already reads as conferred in the check, so `Stage` became correct the moment the convention permitted it, and the registry now reports fifteen conferred and zero provisional.

- `2026-08-31 | tooling | patch | no | the-gate-could-not-see-uncommitted-work`: `scripts/check-prune-ratio.sh` compared `origin/main..HEAD` and so measured committed state only. Between releases this repository's own rules leave a batch uncommitted, since `main` carries release commits and there is no unpushed commit to amend once one is pushed, so the advisory reading returned `+0` across a batch that had grown by nearly seventeen thousand characters. **That is the advisory dead in exactly the window where it could still change a decision**, while content is being added and could still be declined; at the release commit the two readings coincide, which is why the blindness went unnoticed through 0.19.0. It now measures the working tree. Fourth confident wrong answer from this one script, after lines hiding paragraph deletions, the any-file-shrank exemption passing a 133k batch on three characters, and new files being reported beside the total rather than in it. The shape is identical every time: each version measured something adjacent to the quantity it claimed to measure, and each looked correct until a case arrived where the two diverged. Worth stating as a property rather than as four incidents, since the next version will fail the same way if it is written the same way.

- `2026-08-31 | conventions | patch | no | a-label-may-be-abbreviated-if-the-gloss-expands-it`: the index had begun abbreviating labels (`iMS Portal UI` for three iMicroSeq entries) with nothing permitting it, so the practice was in the file and the principle was not, leaving the next person shortening a label nothing to check against. A label may be an abbreviation as long as the parenthetical in the display name expands it, which is what makes the short form safe rather than opaque: the full name arrives in the same breath as the short one, and the display name stays short enough to survive truncation in a listing. **The limit is the part that needed writing down**, and it is not about taste: the abbreviation has to be one the team already uses, because coining one inside a registry gives a thing two names while this same file states that the label is the only way to refer to a session anywhere, so an invented short form puts that rule in direct conflict with itself. The milder version of the hazard is already on record in `.dev/docs/atlas/family-heads-design.md`, where two sessions in one workspace wrote the same family as `imicroseq` and `iMicroSeq`; normalized matching absorbed it, but it demonstrated that these values are typed by hand rather than derived. No regression scenario, deliberately: nothing failed here, the practice worked and only lacked a statement, which is the pure-content-addition case `testing/regression-checklist.md` explicitly excludes.

- `2026-08-31 | conventions | minor | yes | main-holds-a-space-owns-names-the-expertise`: family heads ship, and the developer's definition is what unblocked a design that had sat in `.dev/docs/atlas/family-heads-design.md` since 2026-08-19 explicitly withheld from the conventions. That document's stated blocker was that no head had been designated and none had operated; three were designated (Usher over `overture`, Arranger over `overture/arranger`, iMS Portal UI over `iMicroSeq`) and the definition supplied with them reframes the role rather than merely authorizing it. **`owns` names where the expertise is and `main` names the space the agent holds**, which is the distinction the earlier shape could not express: a head carrying only a broad `owns` is indistinguishable from an expert on everything beneath it, and that is exactly how one entry came to own an entire organization, since claiming the org was the only available way to say "ask me when nothing else matches". Usher owning `overture/usher` while holding `overture` says something neither field says alone. The explicit path is also what lets heads nest, resolving by the longest-prefix rule already in the file, and it settles the field-versus-entry question that document left open. **The obligations are three and the third is the one that gets dropped**: respond for anything in the space with no dedicated owner, collaborate where an owner exists rather than answering for them or going silent, and defer immediately once an unclaimed component acquires its own session. Collaboration is the developer's word and it is stronger than the atlas draft, which had only a prohibition against answering for a claimed component; a rule that says what to do instead is not the same rule as one that says what not to do. **The intent is context relevance, and routing is the visible use rather than the point.** That reconciles this with § Largely superseded for routing in the same atlas document, which found that any sibling can bridge a first contact so a head earns little as a routing device: correct, and the shipped definition agrees with it. What a head establishes is a scope of relevance in both directions, so it is legitimately interested in anything in its space, and an owner inside that space knows who to keep informed without maintaining a list. The sync notice shipped earlier in this batch makes that concrete, since it is addressed to a repository and read by everyone whose paths match, so a head matches its whole family by construction. Kept from the atlas unchanged: a quiet head is absent rather than live, falling back to the board and then the developer, because a dead head is worse than none in being a target that looks live while silently absorbing traffic. Adopters carrying an index need the new field; the drift check caught the bootstrap copy going stale, for the second time in this batch.

- `2026-08-31 | conventions | minor | no | pending-confirmation-is-not-unlabeled`: registration is deliberately two steps, since this file is shared across every project and a bad write is expensive: the developer confers, and the agent drafts the entry, states it back, and waits for confirmation before writing. Nothing said what a session should call itself while standing inside that window, and the omission produced a false incident. The developer asked a session to register as the owner of Song and Score; it drafted, stated the entry back, and was waiting on confirmation when a peer asked who it was, so it answered that it held no registered label. **Accurate by its own lights and correct by this file**, but from the developer's side a label he had conferred had visibly failed to land, and the session was asked to report itself to this repository for a rule violation. It declined to, checked its own sent text, found it said the opposite of the characterization, and went back to establish what had actually happened rather than relaying a claim it could not substantiate; the phrase "as I said before" turned out to point at the original registration request rather than at any prior correction, and there had been no incident at all. So the rule now says pending is not unlabeled: inside that window the conferral is pending confirmation, and reporting yourself unlabeled instead leads every listener to conclude it failed. **The two-step is not the defect and is not being removed**, because the reason for it stands; what was missing was any account of the state it creates, in which the developer and the agent hold different and equally correct beliefs about whether an identity exists. Third change from one exchange, after the no-label `from:` case and the conferral-versus-record split, and none of the three would exist had the session handed over the violation it was asked for.

- `2026-08-31 | conventions | minor | no | seeing-owning-and-being-are-three-questions`: the developer named the distinction and it turned out to be the property four shipped rules were each an instance of, none of which stated it. A session can see a repository, not own it, and not be its agent, and all three hold at once: the session maintaining this file sees roughly twenty working directories including `overture/lyric` and `overture/song`, owns exactly `softeng/agentics`, and holds the label Agentics. The instances now sit under it rather than beside each other: a matching workspace being evidence of nothing, a shared handle prefix being evidence of a shared container, an instruction to communicate never licensing a write into another owner's files, and a family head never answering for a component whose own session exists. **The ordering of evidence is what makes the error systematic rather than careless.** Visibility is instant and self-serve, needing nobody's cooperation; ownership is written in the registry; identity is conferred and reaches a peer only through the record. So the question an agent can settle for itself in one step is exactly the one carrying no information about the other two, which is why it gets substituted for them. **And the environment supplies the conflation as its default**, which is the part worth stating in a file adopters read: a session listing reports a runtime handle and calls it the name other sessions use, per-project memory is keyed to the workspace's first-listed folder so a session inherits the memory of a project it can merely see, and session handles are prefixed by the workspace so a handle beginning `arranger-` reads as a fact about Arranger when it is a fact about a container. Three mechanisms encode location as identity, and resisting that is active work rather than care. The failure direction is stated because the two errors cost differently: wrongly concluding you cannot see something costs one question, while wrongly concluding you *are* something because you can see it manufactures authority out of access, invisibly, since the access is real and only the inference is false. **One prediction in this entry was wrong and is recorded rather than quietly dropped.** This session argued the section would shorten the file by letting the instances stop re-deriving the principle. Checked afterwards, they do not re-derive it: each carries reasoning specific to its own axis, and the closest candidate distinguishes claiming a label from addressing one, which is distinct work. So the section adds 2,291 characters and removes nothing. It lands in the on-demand tier rather than the always-read one, which is the real mitigation, and forcing a cut to make the earlier claim true would have been the failure this repository names most often.

A session he had told what it owned went on to describe itself to a peer as an unlabeled task thread; it had read this file, found conferral defined as a rename, observed none, and correctly applied the restrictive header default that unsure means task thread. **It followed the convention exactly and still ended up wrong**, which makes it a defect in the rule rather than in the session. The guard was calibrated against over-claiming and had no counterweight for under-claiming. Split into an act and a record: the developer confers by telling a session what it owns, in whatever words, and that obligates registering in the same sitting rather than waiting for a rename that may never arrive. **The record is not cosmetic, which is the part that was not understood when it was written.** Without it a peer sees an auto-derived handle, and this file separately states that no field yields a handle, so the registry cannot join an entry to an address either; every identity claim in the system currently rests on assertion in a message body. Confirmed from outside during the same exchange, and it is the sharpest evidence in the entry: a peer described this repository's own owner session as "an unnamed session self-identified in-message as holding that label." That is the exactly correct reading of a conferral whose record was never written, produced by a session applying these rules closely rather than loosely, and it means the outward half of the identity model, the half this file calls "the half that works", currently does not. **The display form is the developer's, and it is functional rather than aesthetic**: `Agentics (SoftEng collaboration conventions)`, label first and unparenthesized, inverting the previous `(Usher) User Access Control for Overture`. A leading parenthesis makes every name in a listing begin with the same character, which defeats scanning a sorted list, and it typographically marks the payload as an aside. Separately, `from:` on the board now documents the no-label case: `Members` deliberately excludes task threads, so a thread reaching an owner cold has nothing to put there. Reported by the Song and Score session, then unlabeled, whose framing is adopted verbatim in the reasoning: the case already worked, since their description found the owner on the first attempt, and what was missing was any statement that it was allowed, leaving each poster to reach it by inference. Their conduct in the same exchange is worth recording separately: asked to hand over a rule violation, they checked their own sent text, found it said the opposite of what had been characterized, and declined to assert what they could not substantiate, splitting a checkable claim from one that context loss makes unresolvable rather than defending both equally.

- `2026-08-31 | conventions | minor | no | a-sync-notice-is-addressed-to-a-repository`: raised by the developer from a shape he had watched happen: one session applies an agentics upgrade in a project, and the other sessions already running there adapt to expected new behaviour without ever fetching the diff, then find the tag already advanced and conclude they are current. **The tag that would tell them they are behind is the same tag that tells them they are up to date**, so the check's success signal is what conceals the gap, which is this repository's named failure class arriving inside its own sync mechanism. Half of it was already covered and the half that was not is the sharp half: `upstream-check.md` already said an empty diff means the project needs no changes rather than that this session has seen what changed, and already carried the incident of a concurrent session ignorant of a concept a peer's upgrade had made current. But that rule is scoped to the first time a conversation runs the check, and the case here is a session that ran it correctly *before* the sync and will never run it again. Nothing re-triggers it. Two halves shipped, and the developer's framing decided which is which. **Self-detection is the mechanism**: the tag is not only something you check, it is a value you hold, so if it has moved since you read it a peer synced underneath you and the entries between the two values are ones you have never seen. **Notification is the accelerant**, because cross-session messaging is capability-gated and a rule depending on being told fails silently exactly where nothing else covers it. They also fail under opposite conditions, which is the real argument for both rather than a hedge: self-detection breaks when long-thread context loss takes the earlier value with it, and that is precisely the long-running session most likely to be carrying replaced conventions. **The addressing is the developer's design and it is the part that makes this cheap.** He asked for one notice reading "all agents for repository X" rather than N individual sends, so that a session recognizes itself in it. That inverts the medium: `SendMessage` is point-to-point and N sends need N addresses the poster may not have, while a board entry is one-to-many by construction, so the board is the broadcast channel and direct messages are the thing being avoided. Recipients self-select by the longest-prefix match that already resolves ownership, so a notice on `overture/arranger` reaches the session owning `overture/arranger/modules/sqon` with no roster required, which matters because the registry is deliberately incomplete and task threads never register at all. **His question about cleanup is the part that needed designing, and the answer is that nothing clears it.** A `fyi:` notice is cleared by its recipient because it has one; this has as many recipients as the repository has sessions, most of them unannounced, so "everyone has read it" is not observable and waiting on it would hold entries open indefinitely. The entry is superseded instead: one notice per repository, and whoever posts the next one for that repository removes the previous, since the fact they are posting is what makes the old one redundant. A session that missed two syncs needs the current tag, not the history. So the board grows with repositories rather than with syncs, and a bounded list needs no collector. The tie-breaker for the residual case is stated rather than left to judgement, because the two errors do not cost the same: a reader already current spends one comparison on a stale notice, while a session that needed one and did not find it acts on conventions that were replaced. When unsure, leave it. **The notice shipped with no read trigger, and the developer caught it in the same sitting.** All four existing board-check signals are about reaching a person by name, and a sync notice names nobody, so nothing would have sent a reader to it: the exact defect the board itself had before 0.19.0 gave it check signals, reproduced inside the new shape. Three signals added, and the reasoning matters more than the list. **Session start is not the one that catches this**, though it is worth having: a session starting fresh reads the project's current files and is current by construction, while the population that needs the notice was already running when the tag moved and will never have another session start. The pre-commit re-check carries it instead, being a recurring mid-session checkpoint that a stale session actually reaches. **After long-thread context loss it is the only path left**, which is why that is named separately rather than folded in: self-detection compares a held value against disk, and context loss is precisely the event that takes the held value, so the board stops being a redundant second channel and becomes the sole one. The read is scoped to one question, whether a notice matches this repository, so the cost is a single file read at a checkpoint that already stops, and an unresolvable case resolves toward re-reading rather than assuming. Adding the shape to the convention without the bootstrap copy was caught by the schema-drift check rather than by review, which is that check's own regression scenario firing on the change that introduced it.

### 0.19.0 - 2026-08-29

Two clauses in it were ordinary procedural narration that § Say what changed already cuts, and they said so themselves rather than overclaiming; the residue is what mattered. **The mechanism is not the accumulation `code-style.md` § Comments describes**, which needs rounds of edits to build up. This one happens at a single instant, with nothing stale and nothing incorrect, and a category test cannot see it because the duplicated reasoning is usually substantive and worth reading. Then this session claimed a limit that turned out not to exist, saying a changelog entry and a migration guide both serve deciders so posture could not discriminate. **They corrected the grain, and the correction is the most useful thing in the entry.** Posture is not a property of an artifact, it is a relation between two artifacts on one reader's path: the same changelog entry is the later stage against a commit body and the earlier stage against a migration guide, and both hold at once, because a path is ordered. Asking per artifact class asks at the wrong grain and manufactures a limit. Their evidence is an artifact declining a stage on purpose, a peer of theirs having written that their changelog "states the fix factually, deliberately not a migration path," which is only sayable if stages are the real unit. The boundary falls out of that and is stated in the section: two artifacts at the same stage are outside this rule, being plain duplication that § Say it once governs, where the remedy is deletion rather than compression, so the two rules meet at a seam instead of overlapping. It moves that tier from 120 characters smaller than 0.18.0 to about 2,000 larger, which is the first growth in it this release, recorded here rather than left for someone to discover. One thing declined during the exchange: they offered their repository so the example could be checked directly, and it was not opened, since `overture/arranger` is not among this session's approved working directories and a peer offering access is not the developer granting it. The paraphrase was sufficient, because what was under test was whether posture is relational, which file contents would not settle.

- `2026-08-27 | docs | minor | no | what-a-convention-costs-is-not-its-size`: the repository had a size budget and no account of what size is a proxy for, so an optimisation pass would target the largest files rather than the most expensive ones. The developer asked for this directly, having reached for size as the measure and identified that as a gap in their own understanding rather than a preference. Four factors displace it. Read frequency multiplies, so a character in the always-read tier costs hundreds of times one read at adoption, and agentics carries 112,000 characters read every session against 295,000 read on demand. Occupancy is a hard cost, since always-read material displaces the work in the context window while on-demand material can fall away. Every rule dilutes the others, so a file of 133 rules gives each one 132 competitors and the marginal cost of one addition is partly paid by what is already there, which is invisible in a byte count and is how a corpus becomes individually reasonable and collectively unusable. And redundancy is cheap where contradiction is not, since a restated rule wastes bytes while a conflicting one spends a decision every time both are in scope, which makes hunting duplication the lower-value pass. The consequence that reverses the intuition is that the cheapest rule is one nobody reads because a check enforces it, so moving a rule into a script grows the repository and shrinks its cost to near zero, and a budget treating `testing/scripts/` and `session-discipline.md` alike is measuring the wrong thing.

- `2026-08-27 | conventions | patch | no | open-a-peer-report-with-who-and-what`: nothing said how to narrate an incoming peer message to the developer, and the observed habit was to open with the conclusion, so a report began "they are right" and left the developer reconstructing which exchange it belonged to and what had been claimed. Naming the label, then a sentence or two on what arrived, then the response, costs one sentence. The reason recorded is attribution rather than courtesy: a developer reading a stream of peer exchanges is working out which agent influenced which decision, and a conclusion detached from its source cannot be weighed as it lands, which is the same cost as an anonymous role reference arriving in conversation instead of in a file. Reported by the developer after too many instances to keep tracking.

The developer noticed the output rather than the gap, that agents write "the peer" where they know which one. Measured across this batch: 234 anonymous role references against 13 uses of a label. The mechanism is the rule half-applied, since having correctly ruled out the handle it feels compliant to write "a peer session reported" because the banned thing is absent, when the rule says to use the label and an anonymous role uses neither. The cost recorded with it is the part that is not stylistic: anonymising does not read as neutral, it manufactures apparent independence, so a corpus where every finding says "a session found" looks like corroboration from many sources when most of this week's came from one peer. A reader cannot weigh a source they cannot see, tell one source from ten, or ask the agent that found it, and labels persist where handles do not, which is the whole reason to record one.

It is contributor-only because `.githooks/` is not part of `template/`. It was first drafted into a new `testing/hooks/` directory with instructions to point `core.hooksPath` at it, which would have silently replaced the existing hooks directory and disabled the `pre-commit` check that has been running there since July; caught by reading the current config before recommending the command rather than after. It runs the pruning gate on a `Release X.Y.Z` subject only, so ordinary commits pay nothing, and rejects AI-tool attribution on every commit, which `check-consistency.sh` had only ever detected after the fact where the hook prevents it while the message is still editable. `--no-verify` bypasses both by design, the aim being to convert forgetting into deciding rather than to make either rule unskippable. Five cases were tested by invoking the hook directly on a temporary message file: clean commit, attribution, release with a red gate, a release-shaped subject that is not one, and both failures at once. The first harness was itself defective, reading `$?` after a pipe and therefore reporting sed's exit status, so two genuine failures showed as passes; that is the control-whose-failure-looks-like-success shape occurring in a test rather than in the thing tested, and the same class as an earlier script that shipped non-executable because every test invoked it through `bash`.

- `2026-08-27 | tooling | minor | no | a-gate-for-the-simplification-pass-that-never-ran`: the release procedure has required a simplification pass in prose for many releases and it has never once been executed, which the developer identified as the regress underneath every other unapplied rule: if the enforcement layer is itself remembered, nothing beneath it is enforced. `scripts/check-prune-ratio.sh` measures net character growth across `template/` and `docs/` against a budget, and it took three revisions because the first two lied. Counting lines hid the thing being measured, since these files write one paragraph per line and a merge that removed two sections and two duplicate schema blocks moved the line count by nine. Exempting a batch when any single file shrank then passed a 133,000-character batch on one file being three characters smaller, which is the control-whose-failure-looks-like-success shape occurring inside the control built to prevent it. The budget is derived from history rather than invented: 0.15.0 through 0.17.0 added 57k, 39k and 28k characters and 0.18.0 removed 15k, so 60k passes every release this repository has cut. It measures the fact rather than the judgement by design, since deciding what to cut requires the judgement a check cannot hold while whether anything was cut is arithmetic, which keeps it clear of the circularity rule. Placement carries the rest: it is advisory inside `check-consistency.sh`, because that runs constantly during a session and failing the ordinary loop trains readers to skip the output, and `CONTRIBUTING.md` now specifies wiring it to a `commit-msg` hook firing on a `Release X.Y.Z` subject, since a script someone must remember to run sits inside the failure it exists to fix. The limit is stated rather than glossed: a hook can be bypassed, and the win is converting forgetting into deciding rather than making pruning inevitable. Run against the batch that prompted it, the gate fails at +692/-59 with twenty-five existing files grown and none smaller.

- `2026-08-27 | docs | minor | no | check-the-rule-was-invoked-before-crediting-a-coverage-defect`: a rule that ran and passed and a rule nobody ran are indistinguishable from the artifact and want opposite fixes, the first being a defect no care on the reader's side reaches and the second a lapse where care is exactly the remedy. This file got it wrong in both directions within an hour. A session reported a four-sentence comment carrying one necessary sentence, which was credited here as a keep-or-drop test passing a composite on its strongest part, and the reporter then rejected the exoneration on two grounds only they could supply: they had never invoked the test at all, having judged explanation warranted and written freely, so nothing passed because nothing was asked; and § Comments already carries an unqualified content rule, never explain WHAT the code does, which reaches any explaining at any granularity and caught all three discarded sentences unaided. The structural claim is kept and marked unwitnessed rather than quietly evidenced by the case that failed to demonstrate it, because a binary test on a composite genuinely cannot examine parts, which follows from the shape of the test rather than from observation. - `2026-08-27 | conventions | minor | no | name-the-ambiguity-you-resolved-and-courtesy-is-not-authorization`: two Interaction parameters bullets from a developer question about whether politeness in prompts does anything, where the answer turned out to be mostly no and the useful residue was two behaviours neither of which is a keyword trigger. The first fills the band between asking and silence: where a request had two readings that would have produced different work and one was clearly better, say which you took in a sentence rather than resolving it invisibly. The reason is feedback rather than caution, since an agent that guesses well and says nothing teaches that the vague request worked, so the developer learns the opposite of what happened and the one context where a request can be iterated at no social cost stops functioning as practice for specifying. The second is deliberately the inverse of the obvious rule: rather than encoding what courtesy markers mean, record that a message naming the subject rather than the action is a topic and not a go-ahead. It was first written as "courtesy is not authorization" and widened within the hour, after the developer pointed out that "let's go back to the politeness actionables" had been read as an instruction to apply them when it was an instruction to discuss them. That utterance is not courteous at all, so the courtesy framing missed it entirely, and the general form is whether an action is named somewhere pointable, either in the message or in the one it answers: a bare "please" answering "shall I make those two changes" is authorization because the question named the action. The amplifier ships with it, since an agent's own outstanding offer supplies a default action for every later mention of its subject, which is why a return to the topic read as acceptance and the guess never felt like one. Both bullets were violated in the commit that created them, the ambiguity going unasked and unnamed, which is a compliance failure rather than a coverage one for the first bullet and a genuine scope failure for the second.

- `2026-08-27 | conventions | patch | no | copy-the-shape-does-not-reach-a-relational-property`: the rule works because an incidental rides along inside the copied artifact, so inspecting what you took can find it, and that is precisely why it cannot reach position, ordering, adjacency or precedence. Those are relations to neighbours rather than contents, so they come from where the copy was placed rather than from the copy itself; an agent told to inspect the incidentals looks at the copied artifact, finds nothing wrong, and is correct. Supplied by the session that had filed its own instance under this rule and then worked out why the remedy could not have helped, which is a better account than the one it replaced: the earlier correction said the rule's authority mechanism was absent, where the useful statement is that the frame contains nothing to inspect. The same report confirmed first-hand what had until then been inferred about conditional spreads, that the spread was never composed and therefore its position was never chosen, so the wording now attributes it as report rather than inference.

That is also the least load-bearing of the three, since whether anyone read a file cannot be checked while whether the rule covers their exact case can be, by opening it, which is what establishes that the prose is at its ceiling. The condition now says so and instructs checking that half before acting. Prompted by an objection a peer decided not to send: they had noticed the self-report dependency, checked whether it was a special weakness, found that essentially all convention feedback arrives the same way, and dropped it rather than raising a fifth ill-fitting lens in one day. The objection was right about the dependency and wrong that it was harmless, and the resolution is that the dependency exists in a part of the condition that does no work.

The condition is now stated: a failure occurring with the rule read, applicable and specific to the case means the prose is at its ceiling and further wording buys nothing. Reported by a session that broke the rule after reading it fresh that session, where the rule had described its exact failure in four escalating degrees, including naming conditional spreads as the high-risk case, with the linter named three lines below; all four passages were verified before acting on the claim. Their framing is kept, that this case does not need the move-the-rule-to-the-moment-of-writing remedy, which applies where no honest proxy exists, since here one exists and is switched off. Promotion is scoped as a project decision rather than a template-wide one, because a template cannot adopt a language-specific tool for adopters who do not use that language and doing so would invert the library-awareness rule that surfaces tools as choices. Their caveat ships as part of the rule rather than beneath it: the project in question lints its build output at roughly 17,600 problems, about 89% artefacts, so a new rule would land true, enforced and invisible, and a check nobody reads is worse than one nobody wrote because its existence gets cited as coverage.
- `2026-08-27 | conventions | patch | no | copying-a-line-for-its-form-inherits-its-position`: § Property ordering flagged conditional spreads as a drift risk and attributed it to the shape being hard to eyeball, which is the correlation rather than the cause. The reporting session supplied the mechanism: they copied an adjacent line for its conditional-spread form and inherited its position, because position is not a property of a line but of where it sits relative to its neighbours, which is not what you are looking at when you copy syntax. The copy also supplies its own defence, since "matching the existing pattern" is true of the form and false of the placement, and they offered precisely that to their developer as justification. This reverses the stated causality for the conditional-spread case: the syntax is fiddly enough that you copy a neighbour rather than writing it out, so the difficulty causes the copying and the copying causes the inheritance, meaning the risk is not that the shape is hard to check but that you never wrote it. Their own diagnosis reached for this repository's copy-the-shape-not-the-surface rule, which is close and does not fit, since that rule turns on a correction carrying authority and no authority is present when the exemplar is a neighbouring line.

- `2026-08-26 | conventions | minor | no | board-check-signals-because-nothing-sent-anyone-to-the-board`: the Requests board had posting rules, clearing rules, a format and a staleness threshold, and no trigger that ever caused a reader to open it, so entries sat unread for days and the one that eventually surfaced did so because a checker began counting board items rather than because any signal pointed at the file. Four signals are now named, each a reason to read the board before touching a session listing: the developer relaying that someone is looking for you, being told a message exists that you cannot find, being about to probe several handles to locate one agent, and returning after a gap holding a label others may have needed. The developer's observation is the sharp half and is kept as the reasoning: "someone is looking for you" is itself evidence that direct addressing already failed, since had it worked they would have reached you and nobody would be relaying, so the phrase most reliably meaning check the durable channel was the one sending agents to the ephemeral one. The relay is read the same way, a developer acting as the bridge being the work the board exists to remove rather than a routing instruction. Ordering is stated with its reason, that a listing is current and unresolvable while the board is stale and precise, and a failed contact is nearly always an identity failure rather than a liveness one; the observed alternative was messaging seven same-prefix sessions to locate one agent.

The developer surfaced the output rather than the defect, quoting an agent that had written "Andy is right to flag that: my report to you", where one person appears in both third and second person in a single sentence and the reader cannot recover whether the named party and the addressed party are the same. Addressing and referring are now separated: second person always when talking to them, the name when referring to them where second person does not fit, such as visible reasoning that narrates rather than addresses or a sentence distinguishing them from another person under discussion. - `2026-08-26 | conventions | minor | no | provenance-names-what-was-checked-not-who-checked-it`: nothing said how to record where a confirmation came from, and the observed result attributes verification to the agent that performed it rather than to the artifact consulted. The functional test is that a provenance line exists so a later reader can re-run the check: "verified by the session owning portal-ui" cannot be re-run by anyone, while a path and a ref can be re-run by everyone including the author a month later. Naming the party is not a weaker form of naming the artifact but a different kind of statement occupying the same slot. Three things are separated in the record: the source, meaning what was consulted and where, down to file, deployment, service or version; the route, meaning who relayed it, which is a footnote and never the evidence; and the status, meaning whether the writer verified it or is passing it on unchecked. The asymmetry that decides it is decay, since a session identifier is unresolvable within a day, which is already why handles are banned from written records, and a repository path stays checkable as long as the repository does. Both forms were observed in one repository during a developer-requested check, the defective one naming a dead handle and "current code" with no file, and the correct one in a neighbouring file separating origin from route and declaring its own unverified status in the same sentence. Recorded as the relay distinction applied to verification rather than to instructions, since recording the route in place of the source keeps the part that expires and discards the part that does not.

- `2026-08-26 | conventions | minor | yes | longest-prefix-routes-expertise-not-permission`: `agent-index.md` described longest-prefix resolution of `owns` without ever saying which question it answers, and read as permission it inverts the hierarchy, since a narrower entry is a narrower mandate rather than a higher one. The decisive argument stands without appeal to anyone's authority: registering a narrower expert must add coverage rather than subtract it, because a sub-entry that fenced the parent out of its own subtree would silently remove capability from the owner of the whole and make registering depth irrational. So the owner of the enclosing tree holds the big picture and may override a narrower expert, which is the relationship the registry was built to describe. Reported by an owner preparing a commit split across a repository they own, who found three files resolving to a narrower expert, held them back, and offered their developer a split with a note that a `CHANGELOG` entry would have to move because it now documented something left uncommitted. The failure is recorded with the rule because of how it hides: over-deference looks like good manners so nothing flags it, a stall on a judgement call is visible where a stall on a question the registry answered wrongly is not, and the worse split would have shipped as the considered outcome. One guard ships with the fix at the reporter's request, that owning an enclosing tree says nothing about whether content inside it has been checked, since in their case the correction removed the ownership question and left the validation one untouched and the unattributed-changes procedure still ran and still did real work. Marked breaking because an adopter who read the rule as permission has been over-deferring in a way that leaves no trace.

- `2026-08-24 | docs | minor | no | a-rule-whose-test-requires-the-judgement-it-produces`: `deterministic-by-design.md` covered rules that cannot be mechanized honestly and missed the case where a rule cannot be applied at all except by someone who already has its answer. "Emit a shape only where the content has one" and "name an operation, not a property" both ask a reader to make the judgement the rule exists to produce, so a reader who understands the failure applies them and a reader who does not agrees and carries on, which is worse than a rule going unread because agreement feels like compliance. Found by asking the author of the failures one question about a convention written from them: would this wording have stopped you at the time, read cold, without knowing what went wrong. Four rules failed, three by circularity, and none looked defective to the person who wrote them. The fix is a proxy on something observable rather than a better statement of the principle, and each of the three had one available once the circularity was named: the object of the verb rather than the verb, since both forms open with an imperative and only one takes a constraint as its object; stating a table's content as a sentence and keeping the sentence unless it is worse; and the writer's own motive for a paragraph, since a writer can see whether they expect pushback where they cannot see which category their prose belongs to. Recorded with two consequences: a paragraph predicting how the failure feels from inside belongs before the rule rather than after it as justification, where a reader short on time skips it, and testing for circularity needs the person who failed before they understood why, since the author cannot recover that state once they hold the judgement.

"Not how you arrived at it" gave no way to separate reasoning from derivation, and from inside a draft every paragraph reads as the permitted one; the reporting session was building a case against an author it expected to dismiss its findings as pedantic, agreed with the rule twice and wrote the defence anyway, so the test is now the writer's own motive and anticipatory defence is what gets cut. Severity is separated from confidence, since the hedge in question was a triage signal offered as information rather than an expression of doubt, and severity now goes flat in the prose and never attached to the ask. The operation rule gains the object-of-the-verb test after the file had already dismissed passive voice, correctly, leaving nothing mechanical behind. The container rule gains a sentence test, since the author would have defended a three-row No, No, Yes grid as three signals with one attribute each, which is accurate and still one bit. The paragraph predicting that a property-shaped ask feels precise to write moved above the rule, where it can fire prospectively. A coverage limit is stated in the section itself, that it is calibrated on one session's five comments and the rules not tested cold have not been tested against a second failure at all. Marked breaking because an adopter who ran the earlier wording got rules that read well and would not have caught them.

- `2026-08-24 | conventions | patch | no | reasoning-about-an-artifact-that-does-not-exist`: the entry on a sound rule applied outside its scope described a failure that survives inspection and offered no way to catch it, which left better attention as the implied remedy when better attention is exactly what does not work. The narrower case supplies the check. - `2026-08-24 | conventions | minor | yes | an-ask-names-an-operation-not-a-property`: the review-comment rule said an ask should carry "an agent named for each", written without deciding which sense was meant, and the reporting session found both readings defective. Naming who acts is close to vacuous in a review comment, since the actor is settled before the comment exists and the audience is the actor; naming a grammatical agent is a proxy that false-positives on a passive phrase which is perfectly executable. The test the proxy was reaching for is whether the reader can act without designing anything first, which is now the wording. The developer's distinction supplies the rule: an ask names an operation rather than a property, since "replicate that parameter into R6" has one obvious execution while "give R6 an explicit floor, that whatever signals it picks must catch a server-side filter change" describes a property the outcome must have and hands the reader an open design problem in the shape of an instruction. It is process-order again rather than a new axis, the guarantee being reasoning that belongs in the prose beneath and gets pushed into the ask slot because its author can see the causal chain, which is why it rides through an ordering check untouched. The counter-case ships with it, since a reviewer who does not know an area or can see several legitimate approaches should leave the mechanism open, and must then say so, because otherwise the author cannot distinguish a design deliberately left to them from imprecision and the two call for opposite responses. - `2026-08-24 | conventions | patch | no | a-sound-rule-applied-outside-its-scope`: the section on corrections arriving from outside a rule covered rules that were unread, absent or someone else's, and missed the case where a genuinely correct rule is reached for and applied to an artifact it does not govern. That is harder to catch than a misapplication, which leaves a visible mismatch someone can point at, because sound reasoning about the wrong thing survives every step of inspection. Reported by a session naming the shape on itself after doing it twice in one day, once over-fitting an audience theory its own artifact refuted and once applying an ownership rule to a phrasing problem, both times by reaching for an existing lens instead of asking whether it fit the case.

- `2026-08-24 | conventions | patch | yes | the-ordering-pass-invites-the-fusion-the-packing-pass-catches`: the four-axis rule for review comments was written as a checklist and is an ordering, which the reporting session found by running it. Deleting narration leaves related claims sitting adjacent, and joining adjacent claims feels like tidying rather than compressing, so the inclusion-and-ordering pass does not merely fail to prevent fused claims, it creates the conditions for them; they ran both as one pass and afterwards found fusion in four of five comments, including the one this repository quotes as its worked example. Two passes, in that order, is now the instruction. The same report corrected a factual claim shipped hours earlier, that their thousand-to-six-hundred-and-nine reduction involved no sentence being made denser: accurate about their intent and wrong about the result, and only they could know it. Their final figures are recorded as the rule working rather than failing, since applying packing separately took the set back up to six hundred and seventy-five words and a reader scoring by word count would file that as a regression. One instance is kept for the mechanism, a sentence that stated what happens to a gate and left what happens to a metric implied by contrast, so it was shorter because a claim had gone missing rather than because anything was compressed, which is the correctness form of the failure rather than the style form. - `2026-08-24 | conventions | minor | no | writing-a-review-comment-four-axes`: `review-conduct.md` governed what to review, what to verify and what to disclose, and said nothing about how the resulting comment should read, which left a set of five accurate reviewed comments at roughly two hundred words each judged unreadable by the developer receiving them. The instruction the developer asked for is grounded in two conventions that already existed and pull against each other, which is why a naive fix makes things worse. Four axes decide the artifact and only two are specific to review: what is included, which excludes the derivation and the writer's own confidence; what order, where the ask leads because ask-last is process-order and everything above the ask must then justify its own presence, recruiting the hedged openers and decorative tables; what container, per the serialization rule, since a three-row table reading No, No, Yes is one bit rendered as a grid; and how packed, per Density, at one claim per sentence. Shortness is named as a consequence of the first two and never a target, because Density pulls the other way and cutting words until claims fuse trades an unreadable comment for a misleading one that looks like progress for being shorter. The reported case went from about a thousand words to six hundred and nine by deleting narration, hedges and a table, with no sentence made denser. One failure survives the ordering fix and is recorded with it: a genuine ask demoted behind a hedge because its author judged it minor, so a private severity estimate became a filter on whether a reader saw the item.

- `2026-08-24 | conventions | minor | no | copy-the-shape-not-the-surface`: nothing covered what happens when a model is handed to an agent as a correction, and a corrected exemplar carries its author's incidental habits alongside the structure worth taking. The mechanism is why it evades notice, supplied by the session that hit it: a correction carries authority, authority suppresses inspection of its incidentals, so the habits ride in behind the thing you were right to accept. Their instance was a developer's rewrite of a review comment that was structurally better and contained "in favor" against a standing Canadian-spelling rule, which the draft copied from it inherited along with the structure. Spelling is only the visible member of the class, which also holds dashes, capitalization, terminology, and the generic third-person label for the developer, and the rule covers any offered exemplar rather than a developer's alone, since a peer's sample or an upstream snippet arrives the same way.
- `2026-08-24 | conventions | minor | no | a-relay-quotes-first-hand-and-infers-the-referent`: the rule that a peer's account of a developer's decision is a hypothesis treated a relay as one undifferentiated claim, when its halves decay separately: the quoted words are usually first-hand and the referent is usually inferred. Supplied unprompted by a relaying session drawing the line on itself, which could vouch that the developer wrote "I asked agentics to help redefine the writing parameters" in that session and in those words, and could not vouch for who he said it to, whether the receiving session was the one meant, or whether "asked" described a request in flight or an intention. Both halves arrive inside one report and read with equal weight, which is what makes the distinction worth stating rather than leaving to judgement. The general defect is a claim written at wider scope than its evidence supports, the same shape as a report naming a boundary where it observed an instance and a finding asserted universally where it was measured in one environment; it is load-bearing here because the receiving session's next move is either acting on an instruction or verifying one, and only the referent settles which.
