- `2026-08-24 | conventions | patch | no | the-strongest-instance-of-not-auditing-yourself`: the section arguing that corrections arrive from outside a rule carried instances where the rule was unread, absent, or held by someone else, which left the weaker reading available that better attention would have caught them. A session supplied one that closes it: they misrouted a message by reading a handle prefix as a label, when "prefixes encode workspaces, not labels" was written in a registry entry they had authored themselves, and they had told another peer minutes earlier in that same session that a launch artefact is not evidence of identity. Authorship, recency and articulation were all present and none was sufficient, so the claim is structural rather than a matter of care.
- `2026-08-24 | conventions | minor | no | narrating-your-own-confidence-is-process-order`: `entry-formats.md` treated recording the process as recording a derivation, which misses the case where the thing being narrated is the writer's own certainty rather than their reasoning, and that case does not look like a derivation so it survives the rule. Reported by a session auditing its own review comments, which found a genuine third request demoted to "One detail if the list is being edited anyway" because it had calibrated the item as minor and buried it accordingly; the fix was promoting it to the ask and stating the severity flat underneath. The reason it matters more than a style point is that the hedge does not soften the ask, it hides it: a reader scanning for what is wanted skips a sentence that opens by disclaiming its own importance, so the writer's private severity estimate has become a filter on whether the item is seen at all.

- `2026-08-24 | conventions | patch | no | a-relayed-request-belongs-to-whoever-holds-the-need`: the board rule that the poster clears an entry carried an unstated assumption that poster and needer are the same session, which holds when a session posts for itself and fails on a relay: the poster cannot know when the other party is satisfied, and that party cannot clear a line they did not write. Reported by a session on the receiving end of it, after a peer bridged for them when their own contact went dead, so the entry named the bridge while the need belonged elsewhere. The rule itself is unchanged and only its subject moves, since knowing satisfaction was always the reasoning rather than owning the line: `from:` now names whoever holds the need and clears it, and a session posting for someone else adds `via:` with its own label and never clears. `check-agent-index.sh` rejects a `via:` with no `from:`, since that combination leaves nobody able to close the entry.
- `2026-08-24 | conventions | patch | no | a-board-timestamp-is-read-from-a-clock`: `posted:` is the only staleness signal the board has, and the rule to surface anything open after a day is computed from it, so a guessed value qualifies an entry for escalation the moment it is written. Observed on a live entry posted minutes earlier carrying a date two days old with a zeroed time. The zeroed time is the part that makes it worse than an obviously missing value, because it is recognizable as a placeholder by a reader who looks and the rule is applied by readers who do not, which is the same shape as a check whose failure looks like success. `check-agent-index.sh` now notes a midnight timestamp rather than failing on it, since a genuine midnight post exists and a noisy check trains readers to skip its output; the same zeroed-time pattern stays valid only where a historical record is migrated and its real time is genuinely unknown.

- `2026-08-24 | conventions | minor | no | a-board-notice-for-a-change-already-made`: the write-only-your-own-entry rule was silent on the case where a developer directs an edit to another agent's entry, so the change arrived as an unexplained diff and the owner had to spend a round trip reconstructing who made it and why. The board gains a second shape for it, `fyi:` beside `for:`, reporting something already done and wanting no reply, with the entry declaring that rather than leaving the reader to infer it. Three properties follow. The recipient clears a notice, inverting the request rule for the same reason it exists, since a poster clears a request knowing the need was met and a recipient clears a notice knowing they have read it. A notice carries no `heard:`, which also removes the collision a request has when the answer arrives fast enough that writing `heard:` and clearing touch the same lines. And `by:` names the authority rather than a justification, because a reader cannot otherwise separate a directed edit from an agent overreaching; "unreachable" is explicitly not an authority, which is the trap the maintaining session walked into earlier the same day, treating an inability to ask as permission when the developer's instruction was the actual basis. An entry field was considered and rejected, since it needs parser support in every reader and splits "things to tell you" across two places when one exists; the board is also counted by `check-agent-index.sh`, which is the only push either shape has, nothing notifying anyone that a board entry exists at all. The payload boundary is unchanged and load-bearing: `re:` still names a thing and `by:` names an authority, keeping a handshake board from becoming an instruction channel, demonstrated the same afternoon when a payload-free entry reached its owner and they asked who was calling instead of acting on it.

- `2026-08-24 | conventions | patch | no | a-ref-is-an-address-and-no-more-durable-than-a-handle`: the rule banning runtime handles from stored fields said nothing about the short bracketed identifier a listing prints beside a handle, so recording one looked compliant while defeating the point. It is an address for telling two identically-named rows apart in the moment, not a session identifier. Observed rather than argued: this session kept its whole conversation across an editor extension restart and returned with a new handle and a new ref, both values recorded twenty minutes apart. The consequence is asymmetric and worth stating, since a matching ref remains weak evidence that two observations are the same process while a differing ref is evidence of nothing at all. A peer had inferred session continuity the day before from a ref that matched across four days, which was sound reasoning from a premise that no longer holds, and the inference is recorded with the correction rather than quietly dropped.
- `2026-08-24 | conventions | minor | no | subscribe-to-idle-rather-than-polling-a-listing`: the file forbade polling a session listing in a loop and offered no alternative, which leaves an agent needing to wait with nothing sanctioned to do. Two things now fill that. A listing shows presence rather than availability, since rows carry a mode and a start time instead of a busy or idle state, so readiness cannot be read off one however carefully; and where the capability exists a send can register a one-shot notice for when a peer next goes idle or exits, with no message attached, costing that peer nothing. Verified end to end on 2026-08-24 with an empty subscription that returned success and delivered its notice when the peer finished. The caveat ships beside it rather than below it, because the call reports success whether or not it will ever deliver: its own confirmation notes that a permission-class mismatch downgrades the notice to a local log entry, and a caller cannot distinguish "not idle yet" from "never arriving". That is the control-whose-failure-looks-like-success shape appearing in the tooling rather than in a check written here, so a subscription is an optimization on waiting and never a precondition for proceeding.

- `2026-08-24 | conventions | minor | no | prose-is-a-serialization-and-the-reader-reverses-it`: `writing-style.md` treated visual structure as a presentation preference triggered by subject matter, which missed why it matters and scoped it wrongly. The developer supplied the reason: a person's inner voice reports on layers operating in understanding rather than in words, so verbalization is a byproduct of their thinking, while for a language model the visible token stream is the thinking. Writer and reader are not running one operation in opposite directions, so prose is native output on one side and a lossy import format on the other, and content with a shape gets flattened into a line on the way out for the reader to rebuild on the way in. Good writing does not avoid that and only makes it pleasant. The test is therefore whether content has structure other than a line, with prose the correct fit for an argument, a causal chain or a single supported claim rather than a fallback. Scoping moves from topic to item count, since the topic trigger named design decisions and trade-offs and let findings, status and change lists through, the observed cost being a developer repeatedly asking for a list after long answers across sessions and projects. The trigger is mechanical because fluency is free to produce and costly to consume, so judgement left to the writer is judgement exercised by the party not paying for it. Recorded as distinct from Density, which asks how much is packed into a sentence rather than whether a sentence is the right container, the two failing independently.

- `2026-08-24 | fix | patch | no | self-visibility-of-a-handle-is-tooling-dependent`: `agent-index.md` stated flatly that a session never sees its own display name, which was true when written and is not now: a session listing in Claude Code on 2026-08-24 reports the caller's own handle, where a build the day before omitted it and left this session dependent on a peer to learn its own address. The correction is narrow on purpose. A handle is an address and says nothing about which label was conferred, so the tooling closed the address gap and left the identity gap exactly where it was, and the contact ladder rests on the second. Recorded with its date and tool because the claim is environment-specific and was asserted as universal, which is the failure `documentation.md` describes; found by a routine listing rather than by testing, which is the argument for rechecking a capability claim instead of inheriting it.

- `2026-08-24 | conventions | minor | no | a-question-is-not-a-work-order`: nothing in Interaction parameters distinguished a request for information from an instruction to act, so "how expensive would it be" could be answered by building the thing. The asymmetry settles which way to lean: reading an actionable request as a question costs one round trip, while reading a question as a request costs unrequested work that may need undoing, so a question gets answered and an implied action gets described rather than performed. The ambiguity is named explicitly rather than left to judgement, since "can you X" is literally a capability question and conventionally a request, and the instruction is to ask when the two readings produce different work. This sits beside the existing sanity-check rule, which handles the inverse case of a yes/no-shaped question that is really an invitation to scrutinize. Directed by the developer, which is also what distinguishes it from a peer proposal: the same section was left untouched earlier in the day when a peer argued convincingly for a change to it.
- `2026-08-24 | conventions | minor | no | report-the-outcome-not-the-routing`: `agent-index.md` already said a runtime handle is an address rather than a name and forbade using one in anything reported to the developer, and the maintaining session broke that rule repeatedly in a single day's reports while actively editing the file containing it. The loophole is identifiable: when the work itself concerns addressing, a handle feels like subject matter rather than plumbing, and that is precisely when it is least useful to anyone outside the exchange. So the rule now leads with the reporting case. An identity correction between two handles is a fact about transport, and it reduces without loss to whether the message reached the right agent and whether anything needs resending, which is the whole report. The test added is whether a handle in a sentence would still mean anything to a reader who never saw the listing it came from. Reported by the developer quoting an agent's message to him in which both readings of the correction named addresses and neither named anything he could act on.

- `2026-08-24 | conventions | minor | no | where-the-residue-goes-and-why-its-address-is-not-writable`: the remediation rules named no home for detail that cannot be public, and the obvious answer conflates two artifacts needing different ones. A live unfixed vulnerability belongs in a security advisory on the affected repository, because an advisory drafts privately, carries a private fork for the fix, and publishes deliberately when the fix ships, producing the operator notice as an output; a private repository produces nothing at the end, so used for a live hole it is a drawer, and a drawer is how something stays undisclosed for years by default rather than by decision. Design substance that cannot be public is the real gap and a private organization repository fills it, with three qualifications: count the collaborators first, since the candidate that prompted this had twenty-three and "private" reads as need-to-know while meaning private from the internet; keep writing prescriptively inside it, since remotes change and forks outlive the decision, and writing freely there moves the map behind a login rather than not drawing it; and expect staleness, since `.dev/` works partly because files sit beside the code and a remote atlas has neither that nor a session-start read. The guard is what makes it recommendable at all: an adopter told to record detail in their organization's private atlas will name it in a committed `AGENTS.md`, and the aggregate across adopters is a public map of where organizations keep unfixed vulnerabilities, each step locally reasonable. So the rule carries both halves, have one and keep its location out of every committed file, which is the committed-paths constraint rotated: that rule forbids a resolved path because it will not work for anyone else, this forbids a resolvable location because it should not, and an organization repository URL is neither machine-specific nor user-specific so the existing wording does not catch it. The reporting session recommended both a committed `.dev/` pointer and no committed location, which cannot both hold; resolved by splitting existence from address, since a committed note that an atlas exists fixes staleness while only the global context carries where it is.

- `2026-08-24 | conventions | minor | yes | prescriptive-is-safe-diagnostic-is-the-disclosure`: `security.md` told an agent to record that security work happened rather than what the weakness is, which conflicts with the standing instruction to document design reasoning and makes a design record unwritable rather than thinner, since an enforcement seam cannot be justified without describing how the current one fails. The split that resolves it is direction rather than topic: "compose the filter at this boundary so every read path inherits it" carries the whole design intent, while "these three read paths do not compose it" is a map, and only one direction is exploitable. Reported by a session that had already used the distinction to redirect a peer about to publish a gotcha-style note in a README, with a worked rewrite where a table enumerating which empty encodings fail open became "a filter must carry at least one leaf clause; an empty combination is not a restriction". The same session then found the limit of its own rule, which is recorded alongside it: mitigation guidance for a hole still open is inherently diagnostic, because "set this flag" is not actionable without "because the default does not restrict", so protective value and disclosure are one sentence and no phrasing separates them. The answer to an unpublishable stopgap is closing the hole faster rather than better wording, and if a hole outlives the ability to warn about it the only remaining channel is direct contact with operators, which is never an agent's call. Marked breaking because the previous phrasing forbade content this now permits, so an adopter's existing security notes may need rereading rather than only new ones.
- `2026-08-24 | conventions | minor | no | remediation-once-a-vulnerability-is-already-published`: the undisclosed-vulnerability rule was entirely preventive, so it covered every situation except the one an adopter reading it late is actually in, and its own observation that history survives the edit removing it is precisely why the remaining moves all have to be forward ones. Four are now named. Read and count the material before choosing a response, since a session that had pushed an access-control audit to a public repository first reported 128 mechanism-bearing lines from a keyword grep and then found 11 payload literals on reading them, of which about four were genuinely disclosive: the first number justifies drastic action and the second does not. Do not rewrite public history, because a force-push reaches neither forks, existing clones, local caches, nor dangling commits still fetchable through the hosting API, while reliably signalling that the range was worth hiding, and a wrong inventory leaves the map in place after paying the full cost. Work in the order close the hole, tell the developer, stop, then tidy the description, which is close to the reverse of what the incident makes an agent want to do. Stopping is a real step rather than the absence of one, since whether known downstream deployments get notified involves people and timing an agent cannot see. Most of an artifact normally survives in place once rewritten prescriptively, so relocating all of it is over-scoping.
- `2026-08-24 | conventions | minor | no | a-memory-description-is-a-broadcast-not-a-label`: nothing treated an agent's own memory as a publication surface, and its index is the most reliably read text in the system, since an always-loaded index reaches every session sharing that path regardless of what they are working on. A session confirmed the failure rather than predicting it, finding a memory file in its workspace whose `description` stated a fail-open mechanism outright, indexed into the file every session there loads. The fix is cheap once seen, since a prescriptive description costs nothing and stays useful; recognizing the field as a publication surface is the part that had not happened. The general form is recorded with it, that moving something out of version control is not a security control: a global agent-context file has no access control beyond the filesystem, keeps no history, and is loaded into every session on the machine including ones in unrelated projects, so its read exposure is broader than a private repository rather than narrower, which is the opposite of how "off git" reads. Surfaced while evaluating whether a shared off-repository reference file should exist at all, and it applies to the memory layer that already exists either way.

- `2026-08-23 | conventions | minor | no | naming-the-person-you-are-talking-to`: `writing-style.md` restricted the generic third-person label for the developer and was titled with it, so every lookup of the rule reprinted the habit the rule exists to break, and the body used the phrase inside the sentence forbidding it. The section is retitled and rewritten to avoid it except where quoting. Reported by a session that had read the file that morning, quoted it while working, and then used the phrase all day until the developer asked twice who was meant, which establishes this as a wording problem rather than a discoverability one. The mechanism it named is that an agent's operating context uses the label constantly at a priority no repository file competes with, so a convention forbidding a phrase the surrounding context repeats cannot win by assertion. Three gaps are now filled. A shared checked-in instruction file takes "the developer", since a name is wrong where the file belongs to no one person and second person is awkward, and nothing said so previously. Where "user" is a term in the project's own subject matter, as in authorization or identity work, it is reserved for that meaning, because "let the user decide" in such a repository parses as a claim about the domain and was found inherited near-verbatim from this template into an authorization service. And the convention now states that only the persisted half is checked, listed for review rather than failed since describing the person to a third party is legitimate, while the conversational half that actually broke has no mechanism at all. Twelve instances are fixed across nine template files, several worse than the ones reported: `session-discipline.md` carried "never commit without explicit user instruction: the user handles all git work themselves" in the file every adopter reads at session start, and `convention-levels.md` carried a bare "let the user decide", the reported domain collision already shipped. Correcting `AGENTS.md` § Initialization also stranded a verbatim quote of that line in `AGENTS.overture.md`, which is worse than the phrasing it preserved, so quotes were repaired alongside their sources. Two are deliberately kept, one meaning the software's own users and one being the rule quoting the phrase it bans. The remaining six sat in § Interaction parameters, mirrored in `global-context/personal-preferences.md` and required by the drift check to match the root `AGENTS.md` that governs the maintaining session, so they were held for explicit developer instruction rather than changed on a peer's proposal, and then made on that instruction across all three files at once. The reporter noted this is their third instance of auditing an artifact without auditing the author, having reported the collision and committed it in speech in the same message.

- `2026-08-23 | fix | patch | no | the-density-splitter-merged-across-closing-emphasis`: a peer reported that a sentence-length check merges bullet lists into one enormous sentence, since list items carry no terminal punctuation, and warned this repository's check had the same defect. It did not, which testing settled in one command rather than by reading the code, because the maximum was 68 words and a merged list cannot produce that ceiling. Sweeping the class rather than the instance found a real one anyway: the splitter breaks on a full stop followed by whitespace, so a sentence ending in bold emphasis ran into the next one, `...resolvable.** Agentics...` measuring as a single 42-word sentence. That is the same false-merge class arriving through different punctuation, and it is the case `definition-of-done.md` makes in the abstract, that a report names an instance and never a boundary. Emphasis markers are now stripped before splitting, which corrected three merges across the corpus and moved the shipped figure to 86 of 572; `writing-style.md` now says the number tracks the measure as well as the corpus, so a reader running the check and seeing a different total knows which of the two moved. Found because two sentences added to `README.md` in the same change flagged in the check's own output, which is the density rule catching its own author inside the file it governs.

- `2026-08-23 | conventions | minor | no | workspace-names-a-container-and-compares-case-folded`: the registry's `workspace:` field decides peer status, and peer status is what licenses guessing a recipient during an introduction, so two spellings of one workspace split it and each half stops reading as peers of the other. This was live rather than hypothetical, with `Arranger` and `arranger` naming one workspace whose members were already messaging each other, and `SoftEng` and `softeng` doing the same in another. `check-agent-index.sh` exited clean on both, which is the failure shape this repo already names, a control whose failure looks like success. The fix folds case for comparison and reports the variants as a note rather than an error, deciding no casing, because an earlier attempt to enforce one shipped on relayed developer intent and flagged the correct entry. Two things are now recorded in `agent-index.md`: the field names a container rather than a team or a product, so a workspace called `SoftEng` does not contradict a team written `softeng`, and the casing follows whatever the container is actually called. The provenance is worth keeping as well, since the same peer relayed the developer's answer twice and it was held as uncorroborated both times before being confirmed directly, repetition by one source not being a second source.
- `2026-08-23 | docs | patch | no | the-sync-marker-sha-is-advisory`: `README.md` told adopters to stamp `synced: <commit-sha>` and said nothing about the hash's lifetime, which left a stamp matching no commit looking like a bad copy or a rewritten history. It is the expected end state here. Agentics amends its release commit until the release is cut, so one set of changes appeared under four hashes in a single day during 0.19.0's development, and a peer noticed the same subject line under three of them before this was written down. The marker is kept as it stands rather than dropped or deferred to release time, since the SHA still records which commit was copied even once it stops resolving, but both the root and global-context copies now say it is advisory and that the version number is the half surviving an amend and the half `upstream-check.md` compares.

- `2026-08-23 | docs | minor | no | report-the-extreme-next-to-the-count`: a check that reports a count cannot be audited from that count, because a measurement artifact appears in the extreme value and leaves the count looking ordinary. Two independent implementations of the same sentence-length measure demonstrate it: one merged entire bullet lists into a single 255-word "sentence", since list items carry no terminal punctuation for a splitter to break on, and this repository's split on lines before sentences and topped out at 68 words. The counts are indistinguishable and the maxima are not, because nobody writes a 255-word sentence. The placement half matters as much as the practice, since a figure travels without the output it came from: `writing-style.md` already published "worst at 68" beside its count, that clause is the disproof, and it still failed to reach a peer who quoted the bare number and warned this check carried their bug. So the extreme goes in the summary line, which is the part that gets quoted, and the density check now prints it there. Recorded alongside the method lesson the same peer drew, that they had found their own 255-word case by printing the worst item and reading it and then did not run that one command against the claim they made about someone else's code, so having a technique is not the same as turning it on yourself and only the second needs a trigger.

- `2026-08-23 | conventions | minor | no | an-exchange-ends-when-it-stops-changing-the-work`: `agent-index.md` covered finding a peer, addressing one, and deciding whether a message was yours, and said nothing about how long an exchange should run, which left one round looking like a completed interaction from both ends. It is not, in two symmetric ways: a sender who treats an acknowledgement as completion and a receiver who treats a reply as completion have both mistaken the transaction for the work. The argument for continuing is not thoroughness but availability, since each round changes the artifact and the next round examines the changed one, so a later finding is about a state an earlier round created and could not have been reported at the start. The Density convention is the worked case, at four rounds between two sessions, where the mechanical check written in round one is what exposed its own path-scoped file list as the wrong boundary in round two, and lifting one-claim-per-sentence out of scope in round three is what left it binding only where the check cannot measure, which round four found. Stopping early therefore forfeits findings that do not exist yet rather than forfeiting polish. The stopping condition is a round that changes nothing rather than a round where both sides agree, since agreement arrives early and an exchange closing on consensus with no artifact changed refined nothing; an acknowledgement and a restatement are both non-rounds and should be silence instead. The bare condition does not terminate on its own, though, which the peer found by ranking their own five rounds as monotonically decreasing: a motivated participant always has something further to say, and diminishing returns are not zero returns. The half that makes it observable is whose judgement it is, since whether a round changed anything is a fact about the recipient's own artifacts while whether it was worth sending is a prediction by the party with an interest in continuing, so each round states what it changed and the receiver is the one who calls the end. Two caveats attach against the developer's "not a wasteful endless back and forth". The sense of being finished is unreliable near the end, evidenced by one clean instance where a message ended "Nothing else from me" and the next carried a scoping correction, a new rule and a retraction; an earlier draft claimed two and was corrected, having counted a message that raised a gap before closing, which is ordinary, and an availability offer that left the door open. And rating your own round is the same error one level up, since the round its author called close to the floor corrected a shipped count, produced the dating rule that a late correction belongs to when it arrives rather than backfilled onto the message it revises, and found the termination defect itself.

- `2026-08-23 | docs | minor | no | when-no-honest-proxy-exists-move-the-rule-to-the-write-moment`: `docs/deterministic-by-design.md` argued for mechanizing checks and had no guidance for rules that cannot be mechanized honestly, which left "ship a weak proxy" as the apparent default. It is not: a noisy check is not a weak version of a good one, because once a reader learns most of its hits are false they stop reading all of them, including the true ones, and the damage spreads to the other checks in the same run by association. So when no proxy is trustworthy, placement substitutes for measurement, reading order being a real enforcement mechanism: a rule that binds while composing an entry belongs in the file an agent opens to write one rather than in a general style file read once at session start, which is the reasoning that split `entry-formats.md` out of `session-discipline.md`. The worked case is one-claim-per-sentence, which length cannot detect; a peer session enumerated the candidate proxies, rejected all of them on false-positive grounds rather than shipping the least bad, and the rule moved to the file read at the keystroke instead. One requirement attaches: say in the convention which you chose and why, because a reader meeting an unchecked rule should be able to tell that the absence of a check was a decision rather than something nobody got to.

- `2026-08-23 | conventions | minor | no | scope-a-new-convention-to-the-artifact-class-that-produced-it`: self-reported by a peer session after overreaching twice in one exchange, and it is the most transferable thing to come out of that thread. A rule arrives attached to the thing that went wrong, and generalizing it to everything it could plausibly touch feels like thoroughness while being how a rule ends up governing artifacts nobody checked it against, where it is sometimes actively wrong rather than merely unnecessary. Their two instances are the illustrations: a density rule drafted from one over-compressed document was written to cover all prose including agent-facing instruction files, where compression is the goal and unpacking would raise every adopter's per-session cost; and a claim about grouped registry labels was widened from "this clause carries real content when present" to "this is what justifies such labels existing", which would have imposed a registration requirement nobody asked for. The tell is theirs and is what makes the rule checkable: the correction arrives from the person who raised the original problem rather than from anything inside the rule, because a rule cannot notice its own overreach and reads as more complete the further it extends. So the check is external and is a question about evidence, which artifacts this was actually observed on, with everything else a candidate that gets tested before it gets governed.

- `2026-08-23 | conventions | patch | no | omit-heard-while-unanswered`: a board entry rendered as though its fields were merged, and the cause was not the writer. Requests entries sit outside a code fence, so markdown collapses consecutive lines into one paragraph, and a field left valueless has nothing holding its boundary open: the following line runs into it. The schema block showed `heard:` with a placeholder and said nothing about the unanswered case, so a poster following the template exactly produced the broken render. Fixed by omitting the line entirely while an entry is unanswered, stated in both the convention and the bootstrap copy. Reported by the session whose own entry rendered that way, which also noted it had skipped running `check-agent-index.sh` beforehand, and that the script does not cover Requests in any case.

- `2026-08-23 | conventions | minor | no | write-so-the-first-read-is-enough`: drafted by the Usher session with the developer, after he read a design audit and wanted to ask "can you please dumb that down for me?" and identified that needing to ask is itself the defect. The reframe is what makes it shippable, and it is theirs: this is not a reading-level problem, since he understood every concept and is more expert in the domain than the document required. The problem is how much work the writing made him do to reach what the writer already knew, which puts the fault entirely on the writer and lets the rule be stated without implying anything about any reader. Their diagnosis of the mechanism is the part worth quoting: finishing a piece of work means holding all of it at once, so prose becomes an index into that structure rather than a description of it, with names standing in for findings and pointers for arguments, and a single sentence carrying a claim plus its qualifications because the writer can see them together. The reader has none of that structure and rebuilds it sentence by sentence. **You compress once; every reader decompresses again.** The headline is the test rather than any bullet: if a sentence has to be read twice, the sentence is wrong, not the reader. Seven practices follow, with their three before-and-afters kept verbatim as worked examples since each is checkable, being shorter with nothing lost. Two additions beyond the draft. They warned the rule is unusually easy to agree with and ignore, because while writing, density feels like precision, so `check-consistency.sh` now lists prose sentences over thirty words on every run as review output that never fails the build, with the comment stating that length is not the defect but is where a doubled claim usually hides, the re-read test being unmechanizable. And the rule was measured against the file it would live in before shipping: **74 of 753 prose sentences in the conventions and docs were over thirty words**, worst at fifty-nine, a number now stated inside the section itself, because shipping a density rule while quietly failing it is the first thing the rule forbids. Their overlap question is decided in the file: the narrower global rule about a pitch leading with a concrete example stays as the sharper case rather than folding in, since it governs a decision needing an anchor rather than ordering within prose. One boundary added: this does not apply to `.dev/sessions/` logs, deliberately terse for an oriented reader, where the fix for density is fewer entries rather than longer ones. **Then scoped twice more, and the second time changed the shape of the rule.** The developer scoped it to human-facing content only, on the ground that a person pays the decompression cost in time and comprehension while an agent does not, since dense prose is cheaper to read and `context-economy.md` exists to reduce exactly those bytes. Applying it to `conventions/` would inflate files every adopter loads every session, so the reader who gains nothing pays the whole cost; one convention here grew from 18KB to 41KB in a day, which is the direction this rule would push all of them. The check was pointed at precisely the wrong corpus and is repointed to human-facing files, and the measurement cited in the section is corrected from 74 of 753 to **15 of 203**, since the original number described agent-facing prose the rule no longer governs. The Usher session then argued the exemption was slightly too wide, and was right in a way that survived the rescoping: the section carries two separable obligations. Reader-orientation practices are genuinely about decompression and stay human-facing, but **one claim per sentence is not a density rule at all**, so it is lifted out of scope and applies everywhere including agent-facing text. Its cost is accuracy rather than comprehension: density makes a reader work harder, while a merged claim makes them read something nobody asserted. It is also free, which is what defeats the inflation argument, since splitting adds a full stop rather than words and usually shortens the line. Their example, from their own session log, is kept: "Superseded, and this is the one that matters: empty combinations are no longer fail-secure" packs a status change, a priority judgement and a finding into one sentence, and a reader skimming for what changed receives the priority claim for free. It matters most where prose is thinnest, because nothing surrounding a terse entry contradicts the smuggled half, and session logs are the sharpest case since they are what a future reader consults to find out what was decided, so a merged claim there becomes a decision nobody made. **The scope was then wrong twice more, and the third correction invalidated both the boundary and the check enforcing it.** The split had been drawn by path, and the document that prompted the entire rule was a roadmap document under the reporting session's own `.dev/docs/`, no file in this repository, which a path rule places on the exempt side; the check was a hardcoded file list that skipped `.dev/` altogether, so the originating artifact could never have been measured by it. Replaced with a three-row classification on the question of who reads the file to decide something: human-facing docs and chat where density applies, agent-facing instruction files where context economy wins, and working documents both parties read where the human rule applies. The asymmetry justifies the third row: an agent reading a longer document spends context budget, which has slack and can be recovered by summarizing, while a person reading a condensed one pays a cost they cannot delegate. `.dev/` deliberately spans two rows, with the roadmap on the human side and session logs condensed, which is why a directory test cannot work. Correcting the scope moved the measurement from 15 of 203 to **87 of 564**, and the direction is the finding rather than the figure: every path-based boundary excluded the densest prose in the repository, which is `.dev/docs/` and the roadmap, and which is exactly where a person goes to decide something. A second gap closed at the same time: exempting agent-facing docs from Density had left no replacement rule, so the exemption read as no rule at all. It now carries one, **condense freely but never to a pointer that has to be reconstructed**, with the reporting session's own incident as the instance: a reference table named two query operators without describing what either emitted, so the names described each other, the reader reached for the wrong one, and had it shipped an authorization filter would have returned records it was written to withhold. The framing kept is theirs, that the human failure mode is frustration while the agent failure mode is confident misreading, which is worse and quieter. A final exchange then found the gap left by lifting one-claim-per-sentence out of scope: it now applies uniquely in the files the density check cannot measure, since the proxy is sentence length and a fused sentence can be short, theirs being twelve words. So the only writing rule still binding in condensed files was the one with no mechanism at all, which is the "easy to agree with and ignore" failure arriving by a different route. They declined to invent a check and listed the proxies they rejected, a colon followed by a judgement clause, the strings "and this is" or "which is the", and a sentence carrying both a status and a priority word, on the ground that each has enough false positives to train a reader to skip the output. The fix is placement rather than measurement: the rule is restated in `entry-formats.md` § One entry states one thing, phrased for the artifact and sitting in the file an agent opens while composing an entry rather than in a style file read once at session start, which is the same reasoning that split `entry-formats.md` out of `session-discipline.md` originally. Their fused-sentence example is kept as the worked instance, since it shows a status change, a finding and an editorial ranking in one twelve-word line, where a reader skimming for what changed records the writer's ranking as established fact.

- `2026-08-21 | conventions | minor | no | dev-was-never-scanned-for-environment-assumptions`: the developer corrected a claim made in this session that `~/.claude/` is "identical on every machine". It is identical on every machine running Claude Code, which is a subset, and the whole reason this template writes `(for Claude: X; for other agents: Y)` everywhere; the developer then added that it also assumes a Mac-or-Unix home layout. So the assumption stack is three deep, counting GNU-versus-BSD tooling in scripts, and each layer is invisible to whoever wrote it because it was true where they were standing. Measuring it found something larger than the session-log issue that started the thread: **the agent-neutrality check scans `template/` only and had never looked at `.dev/` at all**, where 19 files carry 74 Claude-specific references. The guard existed, was good, and had a blind spot exactly the size of the directory that also travels to other users. Fixed by extending that check to `.dev/`, reported by file rather than by line since the judgment is per file, and kept as review output rather than a failure because environment-specific findings are legitimate there. `documentation.md` now carries the rule that resolves them: a finding tied to one tool, OS, or shell declares its environment once in a header rather than hedging every line, because per-line qualification of an empirical observation is dishonest as well as unreadable, rewriting it into neutral language claims a generality nobody verified, while a header naming the agent, platform and date keeps the finding exactly as strong as its evidence. Explicitly the opposite trade from the template's own prose, where neutral framing is correct because those files instruct every reader, whereas an atlas page reports what happened somewhere specific and the honest form is to name the somewhere.

- `2026-08-21 | conventions | minor | yes | sweep-backwards-when-a-convention-change-invalidates-content`: developer-established, after a sweep found that the new session-file portability test invalidated content in seventeen `.dev/` files across sessions, roadmap and atlas. Until now a convention change applied only forward, which leaves the existing corpus asserting whatever the old rule allowed and no way for a reader to tell which entries predate the change. So a convention change names a rule and not a point in time, and the existing files are its unswept instance set, which is the same shape as `definition-of-done.md` § A report names an instance along a different axis. The sweep is mechanical and reports by file and category, since a grep for the newly forbidden pattern is the proposal's evidence and a memory of where such content might be is not. **This creates a deliberate exception to session-file immutability**, narrow and stated as such: immutability protects a record from casual revision and was never meant to preserve content that should not have been written, and a frozen file asserting something the conventions now forbid is worse than an edited one because nothing can correct it and its readers cannot tell. Nothing happens silently: the agent brings what it proposes to remove, from which files, and why the new rule invalidates it, because a diff without reasoning is not a proposal, the developer cannot check the judgment behind it, and some of what a sweep surfaces turns out legitimate on inspection, so a partial answer is the expected one. The granularity is the developer's, file by file for anything contentious or one approval for a uniform batch. **Extended the same day after the first real run, which found the section assumed removal was the operation.** It is one of four. Sorting the matches gave: a genuine defect, which is removed or rewritten; the project's own subject matter, since a convention about a mechanism discusses that mechanism and a project defining a rule is the one place the apparent violation is the content, which is left alone; true-but-environment-specific content asserted as universal, where the defect is the missing scope rather than the content, so it is disclosed with a header rather than cut; and content someone already marked at the time, which is left alone and whose marking is the pattern worth spreading. A fifth operation covers a dead identifier carrying evidence: generalize rather than delete, since an empirical finding names the instance that produced it and the identifier goes stale while the finding stays true, so cutting the sentence removes the basis for something true, which is the falsification boundary running the other way. Also added, because it is the number that would have caused the damage: a pattern count is the size of the search and not of the problem, measured at 86 occurrences across 17 files reducing to one genuine fix in two, so the count opens the investigation and never authorizes the edit, and if the matched lines are too many to read then the proposal is too large for one pass rather than the reading being skippable. One boundary is drawn explicitly because the failure would be quiet: removing what misleads is cleanup, while rewriting a past entry so the file reads as though the new convention was always followed, adding reasoning nobody had at the time, or adjusting a recorded decision to match current thinking, is falsification. The test is whether the file, after the edit, claims anything untrue about what happened; where it would, the honest form is removal plus a note in the current session's file saying a past entry was pruned and why. `breaking: yes`: adopters treating closed session files as strictly immutable now have a defined path for retroactive removal, and one that requires consultation rather than permitting silent edits.

- `2026-08-21 | conventions | minor | no | a-session-file-is-committed-so-machine-local-state-does-not-belong`: raised by the developer after noticing session logs full of operations that neither persist in the repository nor exist on anyone else's machine: inter-agent messaging, memory writes, agent-index state. The existing session-file rules do not catch these, and the reason is precise: they ask what *kind* of thing an entry is, while this asks where the thing *lives*. "Coordinating with another session is process, not effect" covers one case by accident, but writing to project memory, registering in a cross-session index, and reaching a peer are all genuinely effects, so they pass the process-versus-effect test and fail anyway, being effects that exist nowhere a reader can look. The developer's framing of the harm is what makes this its own test rather than a tidiness preference: it is **not noise**, because another developer's agent reads a committed log as project fact and will reasonably infer that the peers, memories and registries mentioned exist for it too, then act on an environment it does not have. An absent line costs nothing; a line asserting machine-local state as project state hands a false premise to a reader with no way to check it. So the second test is that a session file's readers share the repository and nothing else, and an entry belongs only if what it describes exists in the repository. Recorded with the concrete failing categories, and with the observation that each of them already has its own record somewhere machine-local, so logging it here duplicates a local fact into a portable file. Verified live rather than asserted: four of this repo's own session files carry such content.

  Swept on 2026-08-21 with the developer's approval, and the result was partial exactly as `upgrading-adoption.md` § Sweep backwards predicts. **The atlas was the real defect and is fixed:** `interagent-communication-findings.md` and `agent-index-design.md` now open with a scope header naming the agent, the platform and the date, and stating plainly that the plumbing described is not a portable claim, and five dead runtime handles were generalized to the role each session played rather than deleted, because the handles were the evidence distinguishing which session exhibited which behaviour. One was a format example and had its value anonymized instead, since the shape was the point. **`roadmap.md` needed nothing**: all four coordination mentions turned out to be provenance for a finding or description of the convention's own mechanism, which is this project's subject matter. **The session logs needed almost nothing**: twelve of eighteen outside-repo references were already marked `(outside this repo)` or as personal, which is the disclosure the new rule asks for, and the six unmarked ones are design work on agentics' own agent-index convention rather than assertions of machine-local state. So the count that opened the sweep, seventeen files and eighty-six occurrences, overstated the defect by roughly an order of magnitude, and the honest report of that is worth more than a cleanup that matched the approved scope.

- `2026-08-21 | conventions | minor | no | a-report-names-an-instance-not-a-boundary`: reported by one session and routed by another that owned the affected repository, with the placement question raised explicitly rather than decided unilaterally. Fixing a reported SQL injection, user input spliced into raw SQL from a request parameter, that session grepped its package for the same call shape before calling the fix done and found a second, independent instance in a different file, fed by a value read back out of a submitter's own stored record. Same pattern, nobody had reported it, and its stored second-order reach made it **more severe than the one that was reported**. The generalization: when the defect you were handed is an instance of a pattern, the report tells you where someone happened to look and nothing about where else the shape lives, so treating the report's scope as the fix's scope is how a sibling instance survives a commit that says "fixed", with the commit message itself doing the damage by reading as the class being handled. Two points added beyond the report. Expect the unreported instance to be the worse one rather than treating that as bad luck, since reports arrive from wherever attention fell and not in severity order. And do the sweep mechanically rather than from recollection, because a memory of where else this is done is precisely what the first instance already disproved. On the placement question they raised: `definition-of-done.md`, not `security.md`. The mechanism has nothing to do with security, and filing it under vulnerability handling would stop it reaching the missing null check or the unconverted timezone, which is the same narrow-filing failure two other findings hit this release. `security.md` carries a pointer, since a vulnerability fix is where the cost of skipping the sweep is highest.

- `2026-08-21 | conventions | minor | no | write-only-your-own-entry-does-not-prevent-lost-updates`: reported by a session whose migrated entry was silently reverted to the pre-migration schema after it had written and verified it, most plausibly by another session writing back a whole file it had read before the migration. It noticed only because a file-change diff happened to surface the revert, having had no reason to re-read. The rule "write only your own entry" turns out to describe intent and to protect nothing, since a session can respect it completely and still discard every change made by anyone else since its own read: the unit of writing is the file and not the entry. That makes it another instance of the class named in `docs/deterministic-by-design.md`, the writer's operation succeeding and looking correct while the loss is visible to nobody at the time, with the additional property that the victim is a different session from the one that caused it. Writing is now three steps: re-read immediately before writing rather than from a read taken earlier in the session; replace your own entry's lines in place rather than rewriting the file from a held copy, so concurrent changes outside your entry survive; and re-read afterwards to confirm your entry is present and no entries were lost. The third is the one that matters, because narrowing the race does not close it and the check is what converts a silent loss into a visible one. On discovering a loss, restore your own entry and tell the sessions whose entries vanished rather than restoring theirs from your copy, which is how a stale read propagates.

- `2026-08-21 | conventions | minor | no | a-check-implements-a-rule-and-is-never-a-source-of-one`: a session read the agent-index convention fresh in the morning, read `check-agent-index.sh` later, took the script as authoritative where the two disagreed, and asserted to two other sessions a casing rule the convention has never contained. The re-read trigger fired and was obeyed; its own summary of the gap is exact and is kept as the rule's framing: **a freshness rule governs when to read the convention, not which artifact wins when a script and a convention disagree.** Nothing anywhere had said. Now stated in `docs/deterministic-by-design.md` and in the convention's own trigger section: the convention is the source and a check is an implementation of it, so when they disagree the convention wins and the check is a defect rather than a stricter reading, and a rule cited to a peer is cited from the convention and never from a script. Where the passage cannot be found, that is the finding, since either the rule is undocumented or the check is wrong and both want resolving before anyone acts. Two points added beyond the report. A check enforcing something undocumented is worse than an undocumented rule, because an unwritten convention announces its own softness the moment someone states it aloud, while a check states it with machine authority nobody granted, in the imperative, at the moment of action, to a reader with no easy way to tell whether prose stands behind it. And the damage runs in a specific direction: an imperative flag recruits the reader into changing correct data, which is what nearly happened here, since the flagged entry was right and "fixing" it would have detached another session's carve-out into a separate root. The originating defect, a lowercase rule enforced on second-hand developer intent, had already been reverted about forty minutes before the report arrived; the reporter also corrected its own earlier retraction, having first said the developer's decision overrode the tool and then returning to say it did not need to, because the tool was enforcing something never written down.

- `2026-08-21 | tooling | patch | no | a-shebang-without-the-executable-bit`: `scripts/check-agent-index.sh` shipped mode 644 with a `#!/usr/bin/env bash` line, so `./scripts/check-agent-index.sh` returned permission denied while the pre-existing script in the repo was already 755. Reported by the developer hitting it. The cause is small and worth naming because it is the fixture-shape rule from this same release turned on its author: every local test invoked the script as `bash scripts/...`, the form already written into the README, so the invocation a person would actually reach for was the one never exercised. A test that constructs its own input proves the code handles the shape it already expected, and documenting an invocation then testing only that invocation is the same closed loop. Fixed by the mode change, which git records, and the README now shows direct invocation since the shebang promises it. A consistency check flags any file under `scripts/` or `testing/scripts/` that carries a shebang without the executable bit, negative-tested by clearing the bit and confirming the flag, then restoring.

- `2026-08-20 | docs | minor | no | a-control-whose-failure-looks-like-success`: two sessions independently reported the checker defect below, and the second supplied the generalization neither the first report nor the fix contained: it is the credential-hook shape from 0.15.0 recurring, a control whose failure is indistinguishable from success sitting in the artifact whose whole job is to be checkable. That makes three instances in this template's own history, none predicted and each found by someone using the thing rather than reviewing it: a credential hook that read tool parameters under key names the harness never sends and so inspected nothing while every commit passed it; an announcement that records nothing, confirms nothing, and leaves a session unable to distinguish "never announced" from "announced, missed me"; and a registry checker that compared paths exactly, split one ownership tree in two on a casing difference, and then asserted the tree was intact under a heading a reader opens to verify precisely that. Three of one shape is a class rather than a run of bad luck, so `docs/deterministic-by-design.md` now carries it as a named hazard of mechanising a check, with the reason it is expensive rather than merely annoying: a control that errors is self-announcing and gets fixed, while one that silently passes accumulates trust in proportion to how long it has been wrong, and that trust is the damage, since nobody re-derives by hand what a green check already answered. Three practices go with it, ordered by how often they have caught something here: make the check fail on purpose and confirm both the flag and a non-zero exit, since all three instances passed the happy-path test; ask what the check silently assumes and whether that assumption is itself checkable, since exact comparison assumes normalised input and a hook assumes it receives the fields it reads; and suppress derived claims when any input is malformed rather than computing them anyway, because a count derived from bad data is not a smaller truth but a confident falsehood appearing exactly where someone went looking for certainty.
- `2026-08-20 | tooling | patch | no | check-agent-index-printed-a-confident-false-claim`: found by a peer verifying at source while answering an unrelated question, and it is worse than a missing check. `check-agent-index.sh` compared `owns` paths with exact string equality and matched prefixes with a plain `startswith`, so a case difference silently produced two unrelated roots: `iMicroSeq` and `imicroseq/submission-service` yielded no duplicate error, no prefix match, and no nesting. The part nobody had predicted is that the family-root section then counted carve-outs with the same case-sensitive test, so it printed that zero entries carve out of `iMicroSeq` and that everything beneath resolves to one label. **A confident false statement, in the exact section a reader opens to verify what they cannot verify by reading**, which is strictly worse than staying quiet and is the same shape as the announcement gap: a wrong answer wearing the appearance of a checked one. Fixed by case-folding every comparison, so casing is preserved for display and never decides structure. That is the second fix: the first rejected non-lowercase paths as a hard error, on a reported developer decision that turned out to be an over-application the reporting session retracted within the hour. `owns` names the project and app family, so `iMicroSeq` is correct and lowercase belongs to directories and URLs. The wrong fix flagged the correct entry as an error and suppressed the tree because of it, which is the same defect inverted: a false alarm on good data rather than a false all-clear on bad. The lesson is not about casing. **A peer's account of a developer's decision is a hypothesis and not the decision**, which another session had modelled hours earlier by declining to treat this session's reading of developer intent as settled and going to ask instead; this session then shipped a hard error on second-hand intent, and relayed it to a third party as fact. The family-root section is additionally suppressed entirely whenever any path error is present, because every count in it rests on exact comparison and a malformed path makes all of them unreliable. Verified both directions: mixed case flags and suppresses the claim at exit 1, clean lowercase prints the nesting and the correct carve-out count at exit 0. The same pass fixed a grammar defect in that section's own output. The reporter also caught a false statement this session had made to it: told that the live index header now carried only fail-safe defaults, when in fact only the Requests header had been rewritten and the Members header still described the peer-supplied runtime handle, the sibling listing-diff, and the prefix-fallback routing that `0.18.0` withdrew. The live header is now replaced; the lesson is that fixing one half of a file and reporting the file as fixed is the same overclaim as any other unverified summary.

- `2026-08-20 | conventions | minor | no | derive-and-read-your-own-projects-memory-every-session`: reported by an adopting session, and it is the third and previously unstated consequence of memory being keyed to a resolved working directory. Two were already recorded: in a multi-root workspace every session resolves to the first-listed folder, so the wrong project's memory loads, and it becomes the default write target. The third is different in kind. That session created and used the correct directory for its own project and found **nothing ever loads it**, because the harness assigns only the shared one, so it arrives only when the session remembers it exists. Their framing is what decided the fix's placement and is kept verbatim in the file: memory exists so a fact survives a session that has forgotten it, so a memory directory that only works when the session remembers it exists has no property a stray file in the repo lacks. The first two are misrouting; this one is the mechanism silently ceasing to be one. The asymmetry decides the phrasing rather than merely describing the problem, since the wrong context arrives automatically and the right context takes deliberate effort, so every lapse fails toward another project's facts, and there is no symptom to dispatch on: nothing looks wrong. They also stated the obvious repair and its collision rather than routing around it, pointing at the memory path from `AGENTS.md`, which is forbidden because a resolved path encodes one machine's home directory and clone location. Stating the collision is what made the third option visible, because it is precisely between a **resolved** path and a **derivable** one: the rule for computing the path is generic and already committed, so nothing was missing except a trigger. Shipped as an unconditional step in the session-start checklist, unconditional because a rule that fires on suspicion cannot fire where there is no symptom, and because a matching path costs nothing while a differing one recovers memory that would never have arrived. A follow-up from the same session confirmed the derivation empirically, applying the rule by hand to produce a directory byte-identical to the one it had created by manual redirection, and then found a live instance of a second rule in its own memory. Its `feedback_scope_boundary.md` opened "This is the primary project for this agent", which reads as conferral to any fresh session resolving to that path: the claim-an-entry-by-cwd failure already recorded for the index, occurring in memory instead, and written two days before that session helped catalogue the failure mode. Their observation about which half of the guidance did the work is what got added: not the path derivation, which they had already reached by accident, but the audience rule. **Creating a memory directory does not make it yours**, and deliberate creation is precisely what obscures that, since an agent that noticed the resolution problem and built the correct directory by hand has every reason to feel ownership and none of that effort changes who resolves there next. Their first candidate ships as stated including its honest limit: durable project knowledge prefers `.dev/`, which is repo-keyed and unambiguous, while memory keeps what cannot be committed or is personal, a narrowing rather than a replacement, because in this environment a repository path identifies a project and a memory path does not.

- `2026-08-20 | conventions | minor | yes | never-stamp-a-sha-from-unpushed-work`: two adopting sessions, working independently and correcting each other's premises, found that two rules in this template guarantee each other's failure when read separately. `upgrading-adoption.md` has adopters record `synced: <sha>`; `session-discipline.md`, shipped the same day, says to amend and squash freely until work is pushed. Every amend replaces the SHA, so any marker copied from unpushed work orphans the moment the author touches it again, leaving a value that reads as precise and resolves to nothing. Demonstrated against this repo rather than argued: a commit showed an eight-and-a-half hour gap between author and committer timestamps, the signature of `--amend` where an untouched commit has them seconds apart, and its reflog showed twenty-seven rewrites in one day. The SHA the reporter cited had already orphaned before the report was read, which is the most complete confirmation available. This session was additionally handing SHAs from unpushed work to peers in messages, the same defect one step earlier and the half it was blind to. Fixed from both ends: never stamp an unpublished SHA, and never hand one to a peer who might stamp it; stamp the last released and pushed commit even when newer local work exists. Answering the reporters' further question, whether a deployed copy needs the SHA at all, inverted the marker: `git log --grep '^Release X.Y.Z'` resolves any version to its release commit, and release commits are published and never rewritten, verified across three releases here, so the version is a stable anchor from which the SHA can be **recovered** rather than the approximate half of the pair, and the SHA is a cached lookup that can go bad. Deployed global-context copies are therefore stamped with the version only, since "which release is this" is the whole question for a bootstrap copy and a stored SHA would multiply orphan exposure across every machine while buying nothing a grep cannot recover. Project files keep the existing format, already being in the wild, under the new rule. A further round then found the trap in the fix itself: **an orphaned SHA still resolves locally**, so the obvious validation gives a false pass in exactly the clone where anyone would run it. `git cat-file -t` and `git rev-parse --verify` both succeed on an unreachable commit, since git retains the object until a `gc`; verified here against an amended commit, where `cat-file` reports `commit` while `git branch -a --contains` returns nothing and a live release commit returns five refs. The failure therefore surfaces after a `gc` or in someone else's clone, landing on a different machine than the one that made the mistake, which makes this a write-time discipline rather than something a later check can rescue. It also closes a relaxation the rule's own phrasing invites, "or at least verify it still resolves first", which does not work. Where a check is wanted, reachability is the property that matters and `git branch -a --contains` distinguishes the two cases `cat-file` conflates. The peer-handing half is also reframed as a hazard to the sender rather than a courtesy to the recipient, because it ran both ways: this session handed a peer a SHA from unpushed work, the peer stamped it, and quoted it back as evidence in a report this session then relied on, with neither side noticing until a third session found the defect. The reporters' noise-beats-error framing is kept as the reasoning: stamping the last release means the next upstream check re-reports the unreleased delta every time, which reads as a flaw right up until a SHA orphans and the alternative turns out to have been an error. `breaking: yes`: adopters holding a `synced` SHA from unpushed upstream work should re-stamp to the last release.

- `2026-08-20 | template | minor | no | deployed-global-context-cannot-know-it-is-stale`: reported by an adopting session that had been sent to report a stale template, checked, found the template current, and led with that correction before reporting the finding that survived. A project file carries `agentics-template-version` and `conventions/upstream-check.md` makes reading it a mandatory session-start step, so drift there is both detectable and checked on a schedule. A file deployed into a global context is outside that mechanism entirely, and carried no version marker, so nothing about the artifact could tell a reader it predated the convention it was about to follow. The consequence is not lag but active harm: that session's deployed `agent-index.md` still described the runtime-handle address book, including checking a stored handle against a listing before sending, which `0.18.0` removed after measuring that at least 17% of inter-agent traffic existed only because sessions could not be addressed and more than one message in ten misrouted. A stale deployed copy therefore authoritatively teaches the practice that was deleted for causing measured harm, to any agent reading the file rather than the convention. Checking the claim found it wider than reported: **five of the six files in `global-context/` had no tag**, the mechanism being present in that directory and applied to exactly one file, which reads as deliberate to anyone auditing. The unreported case is the sharper one, since `global-context/security-guidelines.md` is a bootstrap copy the convention requires be kept identical to its canonical version, and a silently-diverged copy of security guidance is worse than a stale index. All six now carry the tag, the directory README states that every file here carries it and is stamped on deployment, and a consistency check fails the build if any lacks one on line one, negative-tested by removing a tag and confirming both the flag and a non-zero exit. The reporter's framing is kept as the argument: the existing instruction not to trust a deployed header reaches only the agent that reads the convention, while the tag reaches the one that does not, which is the same reasoning this template already applies to a value carrying its derivation.

- `2026-08-20 | conventions | minor | no | the-responder-writes-heard-or-the-board-never-clears`: the developer noticed the Requests board was not being cleared, and the cause was a step the convention never assigned. `heard:` existed as a field with nothing saying who fills it, so nobody did: six entries stood open between five and thirty-two hours old without a single `heard:` line, several of them already answered. Underneath that is a structural gap rather than negligence, since most contact succeeds by direct message rather than through the board, so success never propagates back: a poster cannot observe an exchange they were not part of, and their entry outlives the need it recorded. Three of the six were addressed to this session, two of them answered, and it never occurred to this session to say so on the board or in the replies, so it was part of the failure it is now fixing. The responder now writes `heard:` after answering by any route and names the entry in the reply as well, since a poster may not connect a conversation to a post they made a day earlier. Recording it is required even where identity was unconfirmed, because "someone answered, possibly not reaching you" is something a poster can act on while an empty field is not. Clearing stays the poster's job, since only they know whether the need was met rather than merely responded to. Two further defects surfaced while acting on it. First, `heard:` was defined as taking a "handle that answered" in a file whose own rule states that no runtime handles are stored in it in any field, a contradiction shipped in the previous release; a handle is also the wrong value on its merits, since the entry can outlive the answering session and a poster reading a dead handle learns only that something once existed, so the field now carries a label. Second, and the actual reason nothing was being cleared: the live board was still on the pre-release schema. Five of six entries used the old `looking_for:` field and had no `heard:` field at all, so the step being blamed on discipline was in most cases impossible, and the board's own header still stated the withdrawn rules that the release replaced, which is the same stale-summary failure the convention now forbids for rule text living beside data. The stale threshold drops from a week to a day: a week was calibrated for nothing, given contact here resolves in hours or does not resolve at all, and the developer noticed this backlog at between five and thirty-two hours.

- `2026-08-20 | conventions | minor | yes | workspace-replaces-window-and-peer-replaces-sibling`: developer-directed terminology change, applied narrowly rather than by substitution because three unrelated senses of the same words are in the template and a blanket rename would have corrupted them. `window` becomes `workspace`, `sibling` becomes `peer`, and a session's workspace lead replaces what was called the main agent. The rename covers the container sense only: "context window", "migration window", "detection window", "time windows" and `window.opener` are untouched, as are "sibling file", "sibling directory", "sibling case" and "sibling instance", which are ordinary English about files and cases rather than about sessions. One agent-sense "sibling session" outside the index was found and renamed; one filesystem sibling inside it was renamed by the mechanical pass and restored by hand. `family` disappears entirely as a term. The residual-ownership mechanism it named is unchanged and now described functionally, as the owner of the shortest matching prefix owning everything beneath it that no more specific entry claims, since naming it invited the reading that a grouped label owes a family story, which a separate finding this release already withdrew. Product-family uses elsewhere in the template, meaning the Overture product family, are left alone: that is a domain term, not this one. The peer definition gains the boundary the developer asked for: **a session in a different workspace is not a peer**, however closely its work relates, because everything peer status buys (a shared handle prefix, shared memory, a shared working tree, reachability by one announcement) comes from the container and from nothing else. Checked before renaming rather than after: two entries in the live registry sit in the same product family but different containers, so the two groupings are genuinely distinct and collapsing them would have broken the reasoning behind every peer rule. `breaking: yes`: the schema field `window:` is now `workspace:`, so every existing entry flags as missing it until migrated, and `check-agent-index.sh` was updated to match.

- `2026-08-20 | conventions | minor | no | expert-inherits-focus-s-decay-through-the-author`: reported by a session while drafting its own entry migration, and it corrects a claim this release has been repeating. `expert` replaced `focus` because `focus` decayed into recent-work narrative, and the convention treated the replacement as the fix. It is not: the decay was never in the field, it arrives through the author, who writes from what is salient, and what is salient is this week's work. The developer caught it twice in one draft, first as a list of what that session had just finished fixing, then, after generalizing to avoid that, as a service described as "Kafka-driven" when that is its most common deployment rather than its defining mechanism. The second is the instructive one because it was wrong rather than merely narrow, reached by generalizing from personal recent use, and the reporting session confirmed against the service's own provider README that automatic sync and direct indexing are coexisting modes. The consequence reaches further than one field: "every field is stable, so there is no refresh and no staleness check" has been stated all release as though stability were purely a saving, and it is half a saving. A field nothing refreshes is a field nothing corrects. `focus` was accidentally self-healing, since it was rewritten every session and last week's error went with it, while `expert` is written once and read for months, so an authoring error is permanent and the absence of a refresh burden raises the authoring bar rather than lowering it. Stability is now stated as a claim about the fields and not their content. The tell added is the writer-side twin of the reader-side test already in `entry-formats.md`: that one asks whether a reader with no memory of the work would need a sentence in order to act, this one asks whether someone who had not touched the code this week would write it, and where the answer turns on what a service actually does, check the service rather than generalizing from your own experience of it. A further round on the same draft then found a second drift on an independent axis, which is the load-bearing detail: mechanism-level content rather than responsibility-level. "SQON querying", or "via Kafka-driven sync or direct indexing calls", passes the recency test cleanly, being durable and general, and is still wrong because it answers how a service works when the field exists to answer who to ask about it. Since neither test catches the other, both are now stated and run separately: recency asks whether this week produced the sentence, abstraction asks whether the sentence routes a question or explains a machine. Two consequences are recorded beyond what was reported, as extrapolation rather than observation.
