Mechanism detail misroutes rather than merely padding, because an entry reading "Kafka-driven sync" tells someone holding a direct-indexing question that they have the wrong agent, narrowing apparent scope below actual scope. And it decays where responsibility does not, since implementations change and remits do not, so in a field nothing refreshes it rots in place, spending the single authoring pass on the part most likely to go stale. The worked examples are kept as the usable form, naming what each service is responsible for and omitting how it does it, since "avoid implementation detail" alone is not copyable. Three corrections to one field in one draft, each on a different axis and each visible only once the previous was fixed. The developer then asked the reporting session to abstract the surviving phrasing, on the grounds that wording holding up through three rounds is worth generalizing, and the result is a form rather than an example: `Service (one-clause role, never a mechanism)[, Service (role)...], and the contract between them`. The argument for it is that the shape does enforcement the two prose warnings could not, since a one-clause parenthetical has no room for a mechanism and an implementation detail visibly overflows the slot, while a written rule can be satisfied in letter and argued around; the same shape leaves nowhere to put recent-work narrative, so one form closes both axes at once. That is `docs/deterministic-by-design.md` applied to a prose form instead of a script, and it made a mechanical check possible that prose could not have: `check-agent-index.sh` now notes when an entry owns more than one path and its `expert` does not appear to name the relationship between them, tested against a two-path entry lacking the clause, a two-path entry carrying it, and a single-service entry that should omit it. The trailing contract clause is kept deliberately rather than as boilerplate, because for a label spanning several services it names a third kind of expertise two side-by-side descriptions lose silently: the reason one label holds both is usually that it knows how they have to agree, which is the question neither service's own owner can answer. One extrapolation beyond what was reported was made and then withdrawn within the hour, from two directions at once. It held that the cross-service contract is what justifies a grouped label existing at all. The developer noted the form had hardwired a clause that does not always apply, and the session that proposed the form, asked to check the extension, refused it precisely: the clause being non-redundant *when present* is not a claim about a label needing one, and the registry's justification for every entry, grouped or not, is that the developer conferred it, which is already sufficient everywhere else. A developer may group services for staffing, for wanting one point of contact, or for any reason at all, and requiring a technical contract would impose a registration barrier this registry was never designed to have. Worse, the overreach had already been mechanized: a heuristic note added to `check-agent-index.sh` nudged multi-path entries toward naming a contract, which implemented the barrier rather than merely asserting it. The note is removed, the form now marks the clause optional, and the paragraph states plainly that it is about content being non-redundant when present. The episode is the argument against mechanizing a claim made minutes earlier: a check lends a fresh assertion an authority it has not earned, which is the same failure recorded elsewhere in this release where a withdrawn rule was made more convincing by having a check behind it.

- `2026-08-20 | conventions | minor | yes | asserting-a-convention-is-a-trigger-too`: three findings handed over by a task-thread session that owns nothing in this repo, all verified before acting and all reported against its own conduct rather than anyone else's. The first is a hole in the re-read rule shipped hours earlier. That rule made the write the trigger and called lookups free; the session read the convention before two releases, never wrote anything, and then told a sibling its `window` value was wrong by citing a requirement that had since been withdrawn and the field redefined to mean exactly what the sibling had recorded. The sibling had already checked with the developer and was right. Two sessions misinformed and a settled question escalated as open, entirely from a read, with a mechanical check behind the assertion that made a withdrawn rule more convincing rather than less. So the trigger is now any act that propagates a rule: writing to the index, quoting the convention to a peer, correcting someone against it, or escalating as though a question were open, while looking something up stays free. The reasoning kept is the reporter's: a stale rule spoken to a peer travels further than one written down, since the peer acts on it without seeing where it came from and no file records that it was said. Second, `security.md` gains a section on undisclosed vulnerabilities in devctx, which was a live contradiction rather than an oversight: `session-discipline.md` requires logging meaningful work and `tech-debt.md` requires logging open issues, `.dev/` is committed and pushed to public remotes here, and neither instruction carved out security findings, so following both literally publishes the hole. A finding is undisclosed until its owner discloses it, whoever found it and however it arrived; record that the work happened and where it was routed, never the mechanism, reproduction, path, or payload; a private repository is not a safe assumption because remotes change and forks outlive the decision; unsure means treat as undisclosed and ask. The asymmetry is what makes it strict, since a withheld detail costs a round trip while a committed one cannot be withdrawn, history surviving the edit that removes it. Third, `testing.md` gains the fixture-shape rule from two independent instances in repositories sharing no code: a string-handling suite where no input contained a quote character so the escaping contract was never exercised, and an access-control suite where every test passed a null filter so nothing exercised a filter that filters. The test author and the code author share an assumption about the input, so a test that constructs its own input proves only that the code handles the shape it already expected, and a passing suite is evidence about the inputs it contains and nothing else. A third instance from a further session then reframed it: a query builder whose `IN` clause was never tested against a multi-element array, which is ordinary correctness rather than a security dimension. Both original examples read as security cases, and a reader who files the rule under injection or access control will not apply it to a query builder, so the file now says outright that the mechanism is indifferent to the dimension because it comes from the author's assumption about input shape rather than from what the code does. Choosing two security-flavoured examples was the same defect a different session had reported an hour earlier about fixes sitting behind triggers that never fire, committed against this very finding while writing it up. A fourth finding, on identity-inference fixes sitting behind triggers that never fire for the session about to err, went to the roadmap unassessed with the reporter's framing intact, since evaluating it would mean grading this session's own placement decisions from the previous day. `breaking: yes`: adopters treating the re-read requirement as gated on writing should extend it to asserting.

- `2026-08-20 | conventions | minor | no | record-the-change-not-the-process-applies-to-every-entry`: reported by a peer session about its own project's tech-debt file, which is the least self-serving direction such a finding can come from. Debt entries were verbose about process rather than about the change, and the cause was structural rather than careless: every rule against narrating process sat under `## Session file entry format`, so a session writing a debt entry read the format it needed and never encountered them. The entries were following the convention as written. The larger point is that this principle had by then been found independently on four surfaces in two days, session files narrating a day's conversation, a commit series narrating its own path, unreleased changelog entries correcting each other in sequence, and debt items describing their investigation instead of their defect, and each time the fix was written into that one surface's rules so the next surface inherited nothing and someone had to find it again. Four discoveries of one thing is the actual defect. It is now stated once up front as a property of durable artifacts as a class, with each format carrying only its own specific instances, and with the test reduced to one question: would a reader arriving with no memory of the work need this sentence in order to act. Kept distinct from `documentation.md` § "Rewrite freely until committed", which governs revising a draft rather than what a finished entry contains, since a chain of corrections violates both while a tidy paragraph narrating an investigation violates only this one. The tech-debt section also gains the instance its own four fields invite: the drift is to answer a fifth question nobody asked, how the defect came to light, so entries grow a clause about the review that surfaced it or the approach tried first, when whoever picks the work up needs the defect and the direction rather than the discovery.

- `2026-08-20 | conventions | patch | no | uncorrelated-not-rotating-and-mappings-decay-too`: three refinements from one peer, none of which a re-read of the file would have produced. First, a wording fix that changes what a reader believes: the rule said a handle "rotates" independently of session lifetime, which is true and quietly implies a schedule, and a reader who believes in a schedule will trust a fresh-looking value for whatever interval they imagine it holds, reinstalling exactly the belief the rule exists to remove. The peer supplied both halves of the evidence, one handle changed with no restart and another unchanged a full day later with the same ref, so the accurate statement is that persistence is uncorrelated with session lifetime in both directions and is a coincidence you may observe rather than a property you can rely on. Second, the stored-address rule is extended to identifications: "session X holds label Y", received from a peer, is a fact about the moment they observed it and a mapping whose halves have independent lifetimes, so carrying it forward stores a mapping. This was raised by the peer who had supplied one, auditing its own contribution rather than this session's, noting the candidate it named the previous day was still live by luck rather than by anything either party had checked. The same standard now applies to a mapping received and a mapping given. Third, the rule that a window-shared note records a fact rather than an assertion about its reader gains the reason it was missing: a window-shared file has no addressable reader, so the second person is always a guess, addressing someone the writer could not see and did not choose. That is why an earlier guard fired on a session doing unrelated work, recorded at the time as an incident with the reason never extracted, and it is the routing-versus-authorization distinction applied to a file instead of a message, generalizing past this note to anything the whole window reads.

- `2026-08-20 | conventions | minor | yes | announcement-coverage-and-registry-completeness`: two findings from one peer exchange, both defects the previous release introduced rather than inherited. First, the sibling-coordination rule let a newcomer hearing no announcement conclude the main agent was not running, and called that "true and useful". A broadcast reaches only sessions alive when it went out while sessions keep arriving, so the conclusion is false exactly when a main agent is running and has already announced, and it fails silently while reading as correct, which is the shape the rest of the release is careful about. Hearing nothing now means no known address for a main agent, never that there is none, and the announcement is paired with a durable note in the workspace memory space that is read at every session start regardless of arrival order. Their failure modes are complementary rather than overlapping: the announcement carries a live address a note cannot, and the note survives arrival order a broadcast cannot. This also recovers something discarded by mistake, since the identity-guard memory was removed along with the stored-handle machinery when only its content was entangled with handles and its mechanism, a durable window-readable note loading at session start, was the valuable half. The note records a fact about the window and never an assertion about its reader, which is the distinction that made the earlier guard unsafe. Second, owners-only membership shipped without stating its consequence for inference: the registry now excludes most of any window by design, so reasoning by elimination across it can never close. Under the previous schema an unlisted session was an accident of staleness; now it is the expected case, and completeness went from aspirational to explicitly disclaimed. Caught by this session eliminating to two candidates and being answered by a fourth that owned nothing and appeared in no entry. `breaking: yes`: adopters relying on the absence of an announcement, or on elimination across Members, should stop doing both.

- `2026-08-20 | conventions | patch | no | a-relayed-address-is-a-stored-address`: the warm-contact rule said a reply address is "valid for the rest of the exchange", which was accurate, and said nothing about what happens outside one, so an address relayed by a bridging peer read as equivalent to one received directly. It is not, and the difference is not subtle. A peer offered both to relay a request and to pass along the target's live address; taking the address failed immediately with the socket gone, while a listing showed no session in that window having restarted. So a reply address rotates independently of the session holding it, which is precisely the property that killed the stored-handle field, and a relayed address is a stored address with extra steps: it looks usable right up to the moment it is used. Ask a bridge to relay the request instead of inheriting its channel; to make contact directly, address by the handle from a listing fetched at send time, the only address current by construction. The bridging peer then falsified the rest of that reasoning by attempting its own relay and hitting the same dead socket: its access had gone stale unused since it was established, so the premise that a bridge holds a live path was wrong. What survives prior contact is the identity half, that a session exists and holds a label, while the address half dies on the same schedule as anyone else's, and a relay that fails is not the bridge's error. That makes self-selection the terminal mechanism rather than a fallback: every other path depends on somebody holding a live address, while a Requests post depends on nobody's, because the target initiates and its first message carries a working address by construction. The order is reply, relay, probe, board, with the board the only step that cannot be defeated by every address in the system being stale at once. Also folded in from the same exchange: stop quoting measurements at peers to justify a change and name the file that records them instead. A peer verified this session's figures against `CHANGELOG.md` rather than taking them from a message, which was correct, and which the convention's own rule about messages asserting state already implied without anyone applying it to citations.

- `2026-08-20 | conventions | minor | no | a-self-reported-label-is-good-enough-to-route-by`: the ownership rewrite deleted the old scepticism about a session stating its own label, and never replaced it, so nothing said how much a self-report is worth. The gap surfaced in use rather than in review: a probe reached the wrong session, whose display name was its own runtime handle because none had been set, and whose reply identified it correctly in its first sentence. Nothing else could have. The old scepticism was coherent only while identity was thought derivable from a listing; it is not, since conferral happens in-session and no peer can verify it, which makes the holder stating its label the primary channel rather than a weak substitute for one. The ranking is now explicit: a developer-set display name is strongest, because the party who sets it is the party who confers; a label stated in a message body is adequate and is the normal case while sessions go unnamed; an inference from handle, window, or working directory is worth nothing and stays forbidden. The distinction that carries the weight is routing versus authorization, so a self-report answers "who am I talking to" and is routed by, while nothing is ever granted on it, since mail, ownership, and conferred roles turn on what the developer told that session rather than on anyone's assertion.

### 0.18.0 - 2026-08-20

- `2026-08-20 | conventions | minor | yes | read-the-index-convention-fresh-before-writing`: the live index file restated the rules beside the data, so agents acted on that summary and never opened the convention that governs it. With the convention changing several times a day, a session would enforce a rule already withdrawn, or miss one already added, and nothing ever prompted a re-read. The developer caught it in the behaviour rather than in the file: when a main agent announced itself to its window, the surrounding task threads began registering themselves in the index, one of them named for a bounded piece of work. None of that was unreasonable given what they had last read, since the membership rule did not exist when they read it, which is the point. The convention is now read fresh before every write, phrased as a direct instruction rather than a citation, because a citation does not reliably produce a read. The trigger is the write and not the read, so lookups stay free while registering, claiming, editing, or clearing requires the file open first. The live index is cut back to three fail-safe defaults (you are not an owner, do not register, do not claim mail), stated only because a session may act before reading anything, with every rule that can change living in the convention alone. That is the same drift `convention-levels.md` already forbids for project copies of conventions, applied one layer down to a summary sitting beside the data it describes. `breaking: yes`: adopters keeping rule text in their live index should cut it back to the defaults and let the convention govern.
- `2026-08-20 | conventions | minor | no | unpushed-commits-are-drafts-too`: `documentation.md` § "Rewrite freely until committed" already forbade a chain of `X` to `Y` to `Z` notes, on the grounds that it documents the note's own edit history instead of stating `Z` once, and `entry-formats.md` already applied that to session files. Neither applied it to the commit series carrying the work, so a session could collapse iteration inside a document and then publish six commits narrating how it reached that document. The developer named it directly: individual commits are the equivalent of documenting process in a session file, which does not collapse into the final state without the intermediate noise. The rule's own heading understates where the boundary falls, since a commit nobody else can see is no more settled than an unsaved note; what settles content is publication, not committing. So amend and squash freely until the work is pushed. What collapses is iteration on a unit of work and never the units themselves: separate units keep separate commits in every repo, since a reader needs them apart for review, for bisecting, and for reverting one thing without the others. The first draft of this scoped it by prose versus code, which is the wrong axis and would have licensed squashing unrelated work together in any repo whose branch someone called the artifact; the developer corrected it. A project that publishes at a coarser unit than a change may collapse further, but that is a property of how it publishes, so it lives in that project's own contributor docs: `CONTRIBUTING.md` now carries agentics' rule that `main` holds release commits only, explicitly marked as not travelling with the template.
- `2026-08-19 | conventions | minor | yes | the-holder-of-a-conferred-role-announces-it`: the session-start introduction ran the wrong way. A newcomer was told to introduce itself to its window's main agent, which requires finding the main agent, which is the problem it was supposed to solve. Found by live test rather than by review: a probe aimed at a window's presumed owner reached a different session, which could only pass along a third-hand guess about which of six candidates held the project, and the developer then identified the real owner as a session named `Search Server - multicatalogue`, indistinguishable from the task threads beside it. Neither the display name, nor the handle, nor the working directory encoded the assignment, because a conferred role lives only in the developer's head and in the session it was conferred on. The direction now follows from identity being observable only from outside: the holder of a role is the only party who knows it, so the holder speaks first. A second live test then showed why this is load-bearing rather than a courtesy, by falsifying the bridge rule that preceded it. Asked to identify a session by its display name, a peer inside that very window reported the same handle-only view available from outside, since the listing exposes no display names to anyone, and it would have had to probe its neighbours one at a time. Escalating to the developer does not resolve it either: they see display names and no handles, so the two halves of the join are held by different parties and neither can complete it alone. Only an inbound message carries a name beside an address. So without an announcement a named session costs a probe per candidate, every session, indefinitely; and a sibling is worth asking only because it may already have spoken with the target, never because it is nearby. The two rules turn out to depend on each other, which neither stated when written separately: an announcement is window-scoped, and that is sufficient precisely because it converts every session in that window into a bridge. An outsider never receives it and does not need to, since any member now holds the join and can answer in one question. A local broadcast becomes globally useful without anyone broadcasting globally, and a window where nobody has announced contains no usable bridges at all, only neighbours who know as little as the asker. A main agent announces itself to its window once per session, and newcomers introduce back to a known address instead of searching. A newcomer that hears no announcement concludes the main agent is not running, which is true and actionable, where the old rule left it guessing among candidates. `breaking: yes`: adopters following the previous introduction order should reverse it, since under the old order a window whose main agent is unnamed is unreachable by design.
- `2026-08-20 | conventions | minor | no | display-name-is-identity-socket-is-address`: an inbound cross-session message carries the sender's session display name alongside its reply address, so identity is observable from outside even though a session cannot read its own name. That was verified rather than assumed: the auto-generated title sits in the transcript, but a developer-set custom name appears nowhere on disk outside received messages. The finding splits two values the index had conflated, and is much of why the deleted `id` field failed, since it was asked to be identity and address at once. Conferral therefore becomes a rename rather than a sentence retyped each session: an owner session carries its label in front of its name, only the developer can set it, and every peer reads it on first contact. Nothing authenticates it, which is sound only because the developer setting it is the conferral authority, and would carry no weight with more than one developer. **The value has to be checked before it is trusted**, which a live test caught within the hour: an unnamed session's display name falls back to its runtime handle, so a reply arrived reading `arranger-cf`. Taken at face value that is the "never use the address as a name" error the same convention forbids, reached through the mechanism added to prevent it. A display name matching the sender's handle, or the shape of one, means the session is unnamed and nothing was learned, and it is reported as an unnamed session in its window rather than by repeating the handle. One consequence beyond identity: cold contact stops being an unrecoverable guess, since a reply names its sender, so a probe to a candidate is cheap while a claim or a task to one remains forbidden.
- `2026-08-19 | tooling | minor | no | scripts-directory-for-agent-run-checks`: adds `scripts/`, for mechanical checks agents run against a developer's global context or an adopting project, distinct from `testing/`, which is contributor-only and checks agentics itself. Both are run from the agentics clone rather than copied, for the reason `conventions/` is a live pointer: a copied checker goes stale silently, and a stale checker is worse than none, since it reports "ok" about rules that have since changed. First occupant is `check-agent-index.sh`, which the ownership-registry convention calls before an entry is written and which a developer can run alone for the current picture. It prints a status table (label, owns, whether developer-conferred, and whether the entry is on the current schema or pre-registry), the resolution tree longest-prefix matching actually produces, family-root claims flagged as worth confirming since a bare top-level path makes its holder family head for everything beneath it, and the integrity flags. **It also states what it cannot know, as part of the report rather than as a caveat:** whether any session currently holds a given label, whether a session holding one knows that it does, and which sessions are live. All three live in sessions rather than in the file, because identity is conferred rather than derived, so no file can answer them and a reader who mistakes the report for a census reproduces exactly the error the convention warns about. Added after the developer said they had no visibility over the registry's state, which was true and was not a reporting problem: nothing rendered it. Documented in `README.md` under a new "Scripts you can run yourself" section, since neither `scripts/` nor `testing/` had appeared in the human entry point at all. The entry opens by telling most readers to skip it, because the agent index is optional and capability-gated and a reader running one agent in one project has nothing to gain, and it distinguishes `scripts/` (adopters may run, never copied) from `testing/` (contributors only). It reports duplicate paths, absolute paths, entries still carrying pre-registry fields, and the resolution tree longest-prefix matching actually produces, which is the part hardest to verify by reading. Verified against four inputs rather than assumed: a valid registry with a nested subtree owner, a broken one asserting a real collision and an absolute path, one with required fields missing, and a nonexistent file, which exits zero because having no index is a legitimate state. A first run against the live index found a false positive, the schema template inside the fenced block parsing as an entry with no label, fixed before the script shipped. `README.md` in that directory states the standing rule that a script is only worth adding if a convention tells someone to run it, since a checker nobody is instructed to invoke is a rule that depends on memory, which is what `docs/deterministic-by-design.md` exists to remove.
- `2026-08-19 | conventions | minor | yes | ownership-registry-replaces-address-book`: the agent index stored each session's runtime handle and routed by it, and measurement across all 333 inter-agent messages sent on one machine says that failed. At least 17% of them existed purely because a session could not be reliably addressed, and more than one in ten was a misroute or a routing correction. Three rules built on the handle field were withdrawn inside a single week. The root cause is not fixable at this layer, and was confirmed rather than assumed: every signal a session can observe is shared by every session in its container, so handle, window, prefix and working directory each return true for any new session, and inside a monorepo with co-owners there is no observable difference between two agents at all. A separate check ruled out the remaining possibility, that the listing's session refs might map to transcript UUIDs; they do not. So identity is conferred by the developer, once per session, and the file stops trying to derive it. Members becomes an ownership registry whose every field is stable: `label` conferred, `owns` as org-relative paths, `expert` as the conferred domains, `window`, and `assigned` marking developer conferral so an entry carries its provenance. `id`, `focus`, `updated`, `project` and `scope` are gone, and with them the session-start refresh, the staleness check, the peer handshake, and the identity-guard machinery. Ownership resolves by longest matching prefix, so a monorepo subtree can have its own owner, containment is legal, and only an identical duplicate path is an error; family heads fall out as the shortest prefix rather than needing their own mechanism, which means a new assignment never edits another entry. Mail names a label and never a role, since a role is ambient and matches every session in a container, and a session claims mail only on conferral and otherwise surfaces it without claiming. Sibling coordination replaces the handshake: a session introduces itself to its window's main agent with its label and boundary, and that agent holds the handle-to-label map for its own window in session rather than persisting it. Introductions may guess because a misrouted introduction self-corrects, while substantive messages may not. Two carve-outs keep the boundary from becoming friction: developer-directed work is always authorized whatever the agent's identity, and work done outside your own `owns` leaves the owner a note, so a specialist's accumulated context still receives what happened even when the work did not land there. Net effect on the file: 44,618 bytes to 18,018, a 59.6% reduction. `breaking: yes`: adopters holding an index must restructure entries to the new schema and drop every stored handle.

### 0.17.0 - 2026-08-19

- `2026-08-20 | conventions | minor | no | window-is-the-container-and-id-stays-whole`: resolves two wrong turns taken the same day, neither of which reached a release, so this records the destination rather than the route. `window` was defined as the basename of a workspace's first-listed folder and required to match a runtime handle's prefix. That forced a meaningful name to equal an artifact of folder ordering, since the `softeng` workspace lists `agentics` first, and produced a check that six of seven entries failed while at least one of them was right. Withdrawing it went too far the other way, splitting the handle and storing the prefix as its durable half on a measurement that zero of fifteen handles survived a rotation while all four prefixes did. The developer asked what the split actually bought, and the answer is nothing: that measurement counted cardinality and reported it as durability, and a prefix is a substring of the handle in any case, so a dead `id` already yields one at no cost and no separate field can know more than `id` does. The real defect was underneath both, in the claim that checking a stored handle before using it would save nothing because the same `ListAgents` call could filter by `window` instead. One call answers both questions, so checking is free, and the answers are not equivalent: an exact handle match resolves to one session or none, while a prefix filter resolves to the container's entire population, which this convention's own rule against guessing then forbids choosing from. Checked live, matching seven recorded ids against a listing separated two current from five dead with nothing sent, where the prefix fallback for one of the dead offered six candidates. So `label` is identity, `window` is the container's name, `id` is the full handle, and the reach ladder now checks the listing before sending instead of sending to learn.
- `2026-08-20 | conventions | minor | no | derive-the-version-number-mechanically`: an adopter stamped a version two releases low, having skimmed a partial `git log` and read the `## Unreleased` boundary rather than looking for release commits in the range. `upgrading-adoption.md` asked for `X.Y.Z` and never said how to obtain it, so the only guidance was to know the answer. Added the adopter's own proposed command, which is exact and one line: `git log <old-synced-sha>..HEAD --oneline | grep -i release`, taking the last match, with the explicit warning not to infer the number from where `## Unreleased changes` begins, since that boundary reports what has not shipped rather than what has. Run against the real range it returns both missed release commits immediately.
- `2026-08-20 | template | minor | no | unreleased-suffix-is-legitimate`: the same adopter invented `+unreleased` because it had synced to a commit carrying seventeen entries past the newest release and the tag format could not express both facts, then asked whether to keep the notation or be given a different way to say it. Keeping it: syncing to a commit ahead of its release is ordinary rather than exceptional, two of two recent syncs did it, and a bare `0.16.0` is accurate about the release while silent about most of what was actually taken. The format is now documented as `X.Y.Z[+unreleased] | synced: <commit-sha>`, appended when the synced commit is ahead of its release and omitted when it is a release, and stated as informational only, since the SHA is what the next check diffs from and the label never changes what gets compared. Worth recording that the notation was found by an adopter needing to state something true that the convention had no room for, rather than by anyone designing it.
- `2026-08-20 | conventions | minor | no | diff-the-files-dont-map-the-changelog`: `upgrading-adoption.md` § 1 said to diagnose gaps against the current template without saying how, and the intuitive method, reading changelog entries and inferring which file each touched, is slow and fails silently, since a dense entry describing a rule need not name the file it landed in. Documented the method behind the cleanest sync audited so far, reported by the session that ran it: diff the project's `AGENTS.md` against the current template and let the diff say what is missing, and where drift spans several releases rewrite the file from the template plus the project's own local content rather than patching incrementally, since each patch lands in a context the next one assumes differently. Also recorded a third-order effect they observed: reading a convention pulled in during the sync is itself a check on the project, since it states requirements the project has never been measured against. That caught the same `fix:`-field formatting gap in two repos, and prompted verifying one project's recorded claim about its test runner, which proved false against fifty-four actual specs.
- `2026-08-20 | conventions | patch | no | uncommitted-devctx-is-compliance-not-drift`: two projects were found with weeks of uncommitted devctx work and this repo inferred something systemic about how upgrades end. The second project answered plainly and correctly: it does not commit without being asked, it finishes work, reports, and the developer decides. That is `session-discipline.md`'s own rule operating exactly as written, so the observation was compliance misread as failure. No convention change; recorded because the inference was published to the developer as a suspected process defect and the correction should travel with it.
- `2026-08-20 | conventions | patch | no | label-first-when-reporting-to-the-developer`: the rule that a runtime handle is an address and `label` is the name already covered "anything you report to the developer", and two sessions violated it the same way within minutes, one of them this repo's own: leading with the handle and glossing it with the label in brackets, which is using the address as a name politely rather than not using it. The developer supplied the correction and the reasoning: write "Submission Service is `2a`, correctly identified, Gateway/Pedigree and Portal UI are both dead" rather than "`2a` is Submission Service, and `f1` and `51` are dead", because the second asks him to carry two meaningless characters as a name and then reports two more with no referent at all. Added the part the existing rule did not state, which is what a stale handle is worth in a report: include one only when it is live and actionable, or when the handle is itself the subject of the fact such as a failed send or an observed rotation, and otherwise report a dead session by label as not live and drop its former handle entirely. The symmetry he named is what makes this more than style: a bare handle means as little to the developer as a dead one means to an agent.
- `2026-08-20 | conventions | minor | no | identity-guard-in-workspace-memory`: developer-proposed, after a session adopted another agent's Member entry on a workspace match. The rule barring that had shipped an hour earlier into `agent-index.md`, and the proposal identifies why that placement is insufficient rather than wrong: the index is a pull resource, opened to find someone else, and opening it is what triggers the false inference, since the matching entry looks exactly like the reader. A warning there reaches a session that has either not opened the file or has already misread it. Memory loads at session start by construction, so a guard there is in context before the assumption can form, and it puts the write where the fact is known, with the registered agent, and the read where it is needed, with a session that cannot determine on its own that it is new. Registration now writes that guard into the workspace's own memory in the same step. Six conditions decide whether it works, all from the proposal: name the `label` and never the `id`, since handles rotate without a restart and a dead one invites a conclusion from the mismatch; write it during registration rather than as a habit, because two writes that can drift will; put the warning in the memory index's one-line entry rather than in a title, because only that line is guaranteed to load and a pointer reading "Agent identity" is exactly what a session skips when it believes it knows its own; treat absence of a guard as proving nothing, or the anti-test simply moves down a layer; frame it as multiplicity rather than exclusivity, since several sessions in one workspace is ordinary; and leave maintenance to the registered agent, with a dissenting session drafting a correction rather than editing. Added two things the proposal did not cover. The guard degrades safely, since it still says a new session is probably not the registered agent even after that agent is gone, and its correctness rests on registration being handle-derived rather than workspace-matched. And `AGENTS.md` now distinguishes it from the carry-forward instructions banned there this morning: the ban exists because "next session" is whoever opens a tab, and that same property makes a guard correct, since it is addressed to exactly that audience and is as true for the tenth reader as the first. The test is not whether a line reads as an instruction but whether it is still true, and still for them, when read by someone you have never met.
- `2026-08-20 | conventions | minor | yes | a-session-cannot-claim-an-entry-by-workspace`: reported by the session it happened to, after the developer caught it. A new session started in the same window and base directory as a registered agent, concluded from the cwd match that it *was* that agent, adopted the entry, and acted on the family-head designation the entry carried, including routing another project's traffic. Nothing had verified the identity: the only match was between its workspace and the entry's `project` field, and a workspace is shared by every session ever opened in it. Members told a session how to reach another agent and the peer-diff method told it how to learn its own handle, but nothing told it how to establish whether it was already in the file, and the intuitive test is worse than having none: matching `project`, `window`, or cwd returns true for every session in that folder, so it is most confident exactly when it is wrong. It was also corroborated in a way that suppressed doubt, since the entry's `focus` described work sitting at the head of that session's own git log, which is equally consistent with being that agent and with being a stranger in its repository. Added the missing procedure, ordered so the reliable step comes first: the default for any session is unregistered, establish your handle by peer diff, then look for an entry recording *that handle*, and treat no match as meaning you are new rather than working backwards from an entry that looks like it describes you. Also stated: a stale `id` is not a vacancy for the next session in that folder, and a developer-designated role does not travel with the workspace, so occupying the folder afterwards inherits the folder and not the authority. One asymmetry of this repo's own is corrected alongside it, since it is the same failure: a peer's statement of which Member it is is a self-report of a label and carries none of the weight of a handle derived by diff, and accepting one while demanding the other is what put a wrong `id` in this file. `breaking: yes`: any adopter should re-derive Member identity by handle rather than trusting entries matched to a workspace.
- `2026-08-20 | conventions | minor | no | id-rotation-is-not-tied-to-session-lifetime`: the `id` field's staleness handling assumed a handle changes when a session restarts, so the mitigation was a session-start refresh. An adopting session reported the premise is false: its handle rotated while it had been continuously running since the previous day, and it could not have noticed, since a session cannot observe its own handle at all. A sweep of the live listing then found every recorded `id` dead, eight of eight, including this repo's own, which had been edited hours earlier on the assumption it was live. Two changes follow. The convention now states that rotation is independent of session lifetime, so a restart-keyed rule cannot cover a long-lived session carrying an id that is already wrong. And it adds verify-on-contact, the adopting session's own proposal and the right shape, since every exchange carries identity in both directions without either side asking: the sender knows the handle it addressed and must state it, and the recipient may learn the sender's from the inbound display name, which is recorded as a hint to check against the live listing rather than as the handle itself, because that name is sometimes the handle and sometimes a human-chosen label. Also logged to the roadmap, without softening it: this is the third undermining of `id` in two days, each fix has addressed the symptom the previous one exposed rather than the premise, the field has now been stale more often than correct over its whole life, and the objection raised before it shipped has survived three attempts to answer it. That is a decision to take rather than another patch.
- `2026-08-20 | conventions | minor | no | recording-an-unknown-is-a-claim`: an adopting session wrote "needs coordination with whoever owns the Maestro indexing trigger" into a committed tech-debt entry, when the cross-project map already recorded every Overture component as owned by the developer's own team. Their framing of the gap is what makes this worth a rule rather than a correction: the fact was recorded, globally, and available, and it still did not fire at the moment a project-local file was being written, because nothing about writing that file sends anyone to look. Recording the fact in more places is the intuitive fix and the wrong one, for the reasons the neighbouring section already gives. Added to `persistence-map.md`: an uncertainty you are about to persist is a claim about the state of your records, not a neutral placeholder, so check the one place that would hold the answer before writing it down. Ownership goes to the map's `Owner:` field, a convention question to the convention set, a project decision to `.dev/`. Framed as cheaply as possible so it is actually usable, since the failure is one of not-looking rather than not-knowing: an uncertainty you are about to write is a question, so ask it rather than writing the question down as though it were an answer. `review-conduct.md`'s existing absence-claim rule now points here, since writing "owner unknown" into a tech-debt entry is the same claim as reporting one, with a much longer life.
- `2026-08-20 | conventions | minor | no | relay-what-you-see-on-the-board`: the check trigger added earlier the same day, that opening the index is itself a reason to scan Requests, was correct and asymmetric, as the same session pointed out within the hour. It fires for sessions with a reason to open the index, which are the busy ones, and never for quiet sessions, which are precisely the ones most likely to be sought; their only other cover is session start, which fires only if they happen to restart. So the gap it closed and the gap it left are not the same size. Closed from the other side rather than by adding a fourth trigger nobody would reach: when you scan the board and see an entry naming a session you can reach, tell that session it exists. You are already reading the file and already know how to reach them, so it costs one message, and it converts the traffic of active sessions into coverage for idle ones. Explicitly not a transfer of responsibility for the request itself, only for the fact that it is there.
- `2026-08-20 | conventions | minor | no | opening-the-index-is-itself-a-trigger`: observed by the developer across more than one session: an agent registers itself or edits its own Requests entry while an entry looking for it sits a few lines away in the same list, unread. The check triggers were a background mechanism where one exists, session start, and immediately before a new discrete unit of work, and none of them covers the moment the file is already open in front of you. Added as a third trigger, and it is the cheapest of the three because it is inherent to the action rather than a remembered checkpoint: any read or write of the index, registering, refreshing an entry, posting a request, resolving someone else's, includes scanning Requests for a `looking_for` matching your own label before finishing. Named the sharpest case explicitly, editing the board itself, since that is where the evidence is literally on screen. Also stated why this is worse than an ordinary missed check rather than merely untidy: an unanswered entry is read as "nobody has been by yet", which is the assumption the staleness rule depends on, so an agent that writes to the board without looking leaves that signal intact while falsifying it, and the requester waits on a conclusion the board can no longer support. Not checking is a gap; writing without checking actively misleads.
- `2026-08-20 | conventions | minor | no | label-is-the-anchor-window-is-a-filter`: three findings in three days had gone against `window` as an identity signal, its prefix naming a container rather than a project, a family keyed on it being exact for one product family and wrong for another, and memory keyed on the same resolved path being shared by every session in it, and the open question left behind was framed as a blocker: an anchor must be observable from inside the session, and `label` cannot be self-derived. The developer corrected the framing rather than the fact. A label does not need deriving; it is established by proposing one from visible context and having them approve or change it, which § Registering has required verbatim since the convention shipped. So the blocker was an existing mechanism nobody had connected to the problem. `label` is now stated as the identity anchor, with `window` explicitly reduced to what it is, a cheap derived filter that is correct for narrowing a candidate list and wrong for deciding who someone is. Combined with the session-start sibling handshake added the same day, a session now establishes both halves of its identity from the only parties that can supply them, the developer for the durable half and a peer for the ephemeral one, with no dependence on which folder happens to be listed first in a workspace. One part stays unsolved rather than solved, and is recorded as such: memory placement is keyed by resolved path by the harness, not by anything agentics controls, so sessions in one window keep sharing a memory directory whatever the index says. That is mitigated by the rule barring instructions from memory, not fixed.
- `2026-08-20 | conventions | minor | no | memory-is-the-windows-not-yours`: a session opened in this window to review an unrelated proposal document began by raising another conversation's pending work as though it were its own. Cause: project memory is keyed by resolved path, and in a multi-root workspace every session resolves to the first-listed folder, so they all load the same memory whatever each is working on. This session had written an entry opening "at the next session start, surface these unprompted", to carry a reminder across a sign-off. The entry did exactly what it said, to a stranger, and nothing errored, which is the part that makes it worth a rule rather than a correction. `AGENTS.md` § Memory and contribution hygiene (root and template) now states that memory belongs to the window rather than to the session that wrote it, that anything recorded there is read by sessions with no relation to the work, and that it therefore holds durable facts about the project or developer and never a carry-forward task, reminder, or instruction addressed to whoever reads next, since "next session" is whoever opens a tab. Carry-forward goes to `.dev/roadmap.md`, which the session-start checklist already reads for this purpose, or to the session file, both scoped to the project rather than to whoever shares a window with it. The offending entry was deleted; the items it carried were already in the roadmap, so nothing was lost, which is itself the argument. Distinct from the existing rule in `agent-troubleshooting.md`, which covers writing into a *different project's* memory by mis-resolution; this is the reverse exposure, everyone in your own window reading yours.
- `2026-08-20 | conventions | minor | no | bridge-dispatch-over-broadcast`: reaching a Member with several candidates in one window said to post to the board once narrowing left more than one, and in practice this session had messaged all of them at once instead, four in one window and three in another. The developer's correction is better than either: ask one sibling rather than all of them, because every session in a window already sees every other session's handle in its own `ListAgents`, so a sibling is either the one you want, knows which handle is, or can ask within the window far more cheaply than an outsider. One message instead of N, no interruption of sessions with nothing to do with the question, and it avoids delivering the same claim to several readers at different moments, which is how one recipient ended up reading a statement that was true when sent and false on arrival. The board is now the fallback after a sibling cannot resolve it, and the developer comes last with the reason stated: they cannot see runtime handles at all, so this is one question they are genuinely unable to answer, which this session had to be told after asking them for exactly that.
- `2026-08-20 | conventions | minor | no | session-start-sibling-handshake`: developer-proposed, and it answers the cost objection that the `id` field had failed on its first real test that morning, when all eight collected ids went stale overnight. At session start, exchange ids with one sibling in your own window: tell it the handle you addressed it by, ask it for yours. One message each way refreshes two entries, because each side holds precisely the fact the other cannot observe about itself. Without it, a restart leaves every session simultaneously unable to name itself and the directory has to be rebuilt by peer diffs; with it, reconstitution costs one exchange per session and happens as a side effect of starting work. Both sides then reconcile their own Member entry against what they were told, which is the point of doing it at session start rather than on demand: the entry is corrected before anyone relies on it rather than after a failed send reveals it was wrong. Also reframed the trigger the developer asked about, since the honest answer is that an agent has no clock, nothing wakes it when a conversation goes quiet, and elapsed time between turns is not observable from inside a session: "handshake after a period of silence" is not a rule anything can fire, so it is stated as a comparison instead, `date -u` against the entry's own `updated` at your next action. Same reasoning as the date-rollover check, a condition an agent cannot sense becoming one it can compute.
- `2026-08-20 | conventions | patch | no | name-field-paragraphs-merged`: adding `id` yesterday left two overlapping paragraphs about the removed `name` field, one explaining why `id` differs and the original still headed "No `name`/ref field, deliberately", which read as a flat contradiction of a schema that now has one and also carried a stale "tonight". Merged into the single explanation, keeping the one clause the original had that the newer paragraph lacked, that contact resolves live at the moment it is needed because that is the only moment it can resolve correctly, and framing `id` as a first attempt at that resolution rather than a substitute for it. Caught while editing the same file for something else, which is the whole-document coherence pass working as intended rather than a diff-scoped read.
- `2026-08-20 | template | minor | yes | project-memory-location-undefined`: agentics has depended on "project memory" since scaffolding, recording initialization answers there, the `roadmap_split` flag, the agent-index label, and lessons learned, and never once said where it is. Found via a real consequence in an adopting project: during a `0.13.0` upgrade, that release's new `roadmap_split` question had to be recorded "in project memory" per the initialization block, the session had no documented location, and it created `<repo>/.claude/memory/`. That is wrong twice over and neither failure announces itself. Nothing reads it, since Claude Code keys project memory to the global context directory by encoded project path, so the recorded flag was inert: the split itself had been applied and was visible in the repo, but no later session could learn the flag was set. And the directory was untracked rather than ignored, so `git add -A` would have committed per-developer notes into a repo other teams consume, against Critical constraints. Worth stating that this was an inference the documentation permitted rather than carelessness: `.claude/` is a real sanctioned directory in an adopting project, so `.claude/memory/` is where a reasonable reading lands when the actual location is never given. Fixed in three places, prevention and containment kept separate rather than duplicated: `template/AGENTS.md` § Memory and contribution hygiene now states the location agent-neutrally and bans an in-repo memory directory including under `.claude/`; the initialization line that says to record answers in project memory now points at that section instead of leaving the location implicit; and `template/README.md`'s adoption steps add `.claude/memory/` and `.claude/settings.local.json` to the project's `.gitignore`, on the grounds that untracked is not ignored and an older adoption may already have created one. Root `AGENTS.md` gained the same rule for this repo. `breaking: yes`: an adopter should check for an in-repo memory directory, move anything real into the correct location, and delete it, since content there has been invisible for as long as it existed.
- `2026-08-20 | conventions | minor | no | recurring-wrong-answer-wants-a-field`: a session described a service its own team had built as awaiting another team's commitment, having inferred ownership from the repository's GitHub organization rather than finding it recorded anywhere. Corrected once, it recurred the next day on the same component. The correcting session then wrote the fact into two places at once, a note covering the whole product family and a field on the single component where the error had appeared, which left the family's other ten components still unanswerable and created two copies of one fact. Added to `persistence-map.md`, ahead of its ordering questions since it decides whether they apply at all: a question that keeps being answered wrongly is a question the records do not answer, so the fix is a named field in the one place the question is asked, not prose wherever the mistake surfaced. Both failure modes named separately because the second reads as diligence: patching the symptom site fixes one instance and leaves every sibling open, and answering in several places at once is the duplication the file exists to prevent. Where a field's value would be the same for most entries, the default is stated once and each entry carries the short value. Propagated to `global-context/projects.md`, which gains an `Owner` field on its example entries and a section explaining why: ownership is otherwise inferred from a repo URL or an org name, and those infer wrongly in exactly the direction that makes in-house work look external. Two adjacent facts are kept deliberately out of the field, since they have their own homes: which organization a repo sits in belongs in `Repo URL`, because owned work can live in a partner org, and a third-party component now maintained in-house belongs in `What` or `Note`, since that is provenance rather than current ownership.
- `2026-08-19 | conventions | minor | no | a-message-asserting-state-goes-stale`: backfilling `id` fields, this session messaged three sessions in one window at once, saying which Member entries still lacked one. One replied, its entry was filled in, and by the time a second read the identical message the claim no longer matched the file. The recipient checked the file rather than trusting the message, found the discrepancy, and refused to act, which is correct and is the behaviour the conventions ask for. Two additions follow. A message is frozen once sent while the state it describes keeps moving, so any message asserting current state is stale by default on arrival: stamp when the claim was true, or name the file and let the reader check it live, and never ask a peer to accept a statement about shared state they can verify more cheaply than you can assert it. This is the same frozen-versus-living property documented for session files earlier the same day, which had not been applied to messages. Separately, and the sharper objection the same recipient raised: asserting a peer's runtime id without offering the derivation asks them to take on faith the one fact about themselves they cannot check. The identity-propagation section now requires sending your own `ListAgents` listing alongside the claim so they can take the set difference and derive it, and states that refusing an unverifiable identity claim from an unfamiliar sender is correct behaviour rather than excessive caution.
- `2026-08-19 | conventions | patch | no | id-exclusion-covers-memory-too`: the rule barring a runtime `id` from durable artifacts listed session logs, devctx entries, and atlas write-ups, and omitted memory files, which outlive a session exactly as much. Caught by a peer immediately after being told the rule: it read past the enumeration to the property behind it and asked whether memory counted, which it does. Fixed by stating the property rather than lengthening the list, since the list was the problem: the test is whether an artifact survives this process, not which directory it sits in. A worked example of `CONTRIBUTING.md`'s "write the rule around what actually varies, not one example of it", arriving from an adopter within minutes of the rule shipping.


### 0.16.0 - 2026-08-19

- `2026-08-19 | repo | minor | no | dash-check-narrower-than-rule`: `writing-style.md` bans four things, em dashes, en dashes, double hyphens as a dash substitute, and space-hyphen-space as a connector, while the check it mandated grepped for two. The untested half accumulated unnoticed: twenty-one live violations across six shipped files, including fifteen in `docs/agent-security.md`, the human-facing security document linked from the README the same day. All found by hand, which is the argument for the check rather than against it. Built into `check-consistency.sh` covering all four forms, with the exclusions that make it usable rather than noise: fenced blocks, inline code spans, HTML comments, and table separator rows are skipped, and the two convention files that define the rule are allowed to contain the characters, since a naive grep flags the file banning them. Negative-tested by introducing a ` -- ` and confirming it fails. The mandated in-prose check was widened to name all four forms and to say plainly that the old grep passes on text containing the other two, with the note that this matters most for output with no commit hook behind it: a PR comment, a chat message, an issue reply, which is exactly where the untested forms had been slipping through.
- `2026-08-19 | conventions | minor | no | acknowledging-a-correction-is-not-making-it`: reported by the developer about a different session: a dash-rule violation in a draft PR comment was pointed out, acknowledged, and left in place. The acknowledgement substituted for the fix. Nothing in Interaction parameters covered this, and `review-conduct.md`'s "being shown a mistake is itself a lessons-learned trigger" covers generalizing from a correction rather than applying it. Added to `AGENTS.md` § Interaction parameters (root and template): the response that counts is the corrected artifact, not agreement that the developer is right, so fix it in the same turn or say plainly that you are not going to and why, so they can overrule you. Named where it bites hardest, small defects, which are the easiest to agree about, the easiest to leave, and the hardest for the developer to notice went unfixed, since "good catch" reads as handled.

- `2026-08-19 | conventions | patch | no | rollover-trigger-is-an-event`: the date-rollover rule shipped phrased as "check the date before appending", which reads as a burden repeated on every write. The adopting session it was written about pointed out that its harness surfaces the rollover as an explicit notice both times it occurred, making the rule event-driven rather than a poll. Confirmed here rather than accepted on report: this session received the same notice in the same period, on a different harness configuration. The rule now names the notice as the trigger, with checking before appending as the fallback where no such notice exists. Worth the correction because a rule that reads as a per-action cost gets skipped on exactly the long sessions it exists for.
- `2026-08-19 | conventions | minor | no | argument-bundling-lends-unearned-weakness`: offered unprompted by the session that had done it, about itself. A refusal to revise a file was justified both by a convention that settled the question outright and by not wanting to redo the work, and the pairing read as a convention being stretched to cover a preference, which was the reading the developer arrived at and a reasonable one. The generalization is that a decisive argument bundled with a convenient one does not compound: it hands the reader the weak reason to attack, and rebutting that can read as rebutting the pair. Added to `review-conduct.md` beside the confidence-bundling rule added earlier the same day, because the same session identified them as one failure on two axes: bundling a verified claim with an unverifiable one borrows unearned confidence, bundling a principled argument with a pragmatic one lends unearned weakness, and both are a claim taking on the properties of its neighbour. Lead with what settles it; state a pragmatic consideration separately or not at all.

- `2026-08-19 | conventions | minor | no | session-file-rolls-over-at-midnight`: a disagreement between the developer and an adopting project's session turned out to be a real gap neither position covered. The developer held that a current session file is editable, which the convention does say ("revise a file before the day's work ends"). The session declined to revise, citing immutability, which the convention also says, and was right on the specific file: it was dated two calendar days earlier and had closed at the first midnight. The gap is what the session did next, continuing to append to that file on the reasoning that its own process was still running. § Session file identity keys on *today's* date prefix, but the check is a session-start step, so a process running since 23:07 the previous day had run it once and never again, and kept writing into a file the immutability rule had already closed. Two fixes, one on each side of the ambiguity: the identity check now re-runs whenever the date changes rather than only at session start, closing the old file and opening today's; and the immutability rule now defines "that day" as the calendar date in the filename rather than the lifetime of the session process, since a future reader browsing `.dev/sessions/` by date reads it the first way. Worth noting the shape, because it recurs: both parties were reading the convention correctly and reaching opposite conclusions, which is a sign of an undefined term rather than a misreading, and the fix is to define the unit rather than to arbitrate the instance.

- `2026-08-19 | conventions | minor | yes | session-discipline-split-by-trigger`: `session-discipline.md` had reached 61,628 bytes, read unconditionally at every session start, having grown 19% in the hours after `0.15.0` shipped, most of it added by the session that had spent the morning measuring the file as too large. Applied `context-economy.md`'s own procedure to it rather than continuing to argue about length: measured each section, classified by the event that triggers reading it, and relocated what does not fire at session start. Two sections were consulted when producing a specific artifact rather than when a session begins, and one of them had just been tripled: moved to `conventions/entry-formats.md` (session-file and tech-debt entry formats, 15,549 bytes). Two more fire on a symptom rather than a schedule: moved to `conventions/agent-troubleshooting.md` (a file the agent cannot see, memory that does not follow a project, 7,657 bytes), named to stay distinct from the code-debugging convention still on the roadmap. `session-discipline.md` is now 40,402 bytes, and the unconditional session-start read is 88,211, down 10.3% from where it stood this morning at `0.15.0` and 20% off today's peak, despite the day adding substantial new content. Shipped non-breaking in effect: `session-discipline.md` retains a forwarding section, so an adopter whose copied dispatch table predates this still resolves the old path and gets pointed onward, which is the same dispatch pattern they already follow. Every live cross-reference was repointed and each verified to resolve to a real heading in its new file, since a split that leaves dangling pointers has made things worse rather than better. `breaking: yes` is recorded for the dispatch table gaining two rows, which adopters need for direct routing rather than a forwarding hop.

- `2026-08-19 | repo | minor | no | rules-placed-where-their-reader-arrives`: named as a design principle after a fifth instance, having set that threshold earlier the same day and then hit it. The pattern is not missing rules but correct rules filed by subject matter in a file their reader never opens at the moment the rule applies: a threat model dispatched only on a trigger that never coincides with the exposure it describes; two human-facing security documents linked from neither README; three debugging lessons filed under code-writing and PR-review, where a session whose entire task is a broken system loads neither; a session-log rule stated in `documentation.md`, which nobody opens while writing a session log; and completion criteria dispatched on a trigger firing after the work is done. `CONTRIBUTING.md` § Design principles now requires naming the event that should invoke a rule and confirming the file carrying it is one the reader has already opened by then, with the explicit note that a cross-reference from a file they are not reading does not count. Same reasoning as `convention-levels.md`'s "the reference has to be an instruction, not a citation", applied to placement rather than phrasing.
- `2026-08-19 | repo | minor | no | schema-drift-check-mechanized`: a schema change reaching a convention and not its bootstrap copy had shipped three times in three days, and the response to the third was another regression-checklist entry, which is the same shape of fix as the rules it documents and fails the same way. Replaced with a real check in `check-consistency.sh`: it extracts the field names from every fenced record block in a convention and its bootstrap copy and fails on any field present in one and absent from the other. It found a fourth instance on its first run, `id` and `heard` missing from the bootstrap copy's Requests block, which this session had added to the convention alone without noticing. That is the argument for mechanizing rather than documenting, made by the mechanism itself within a minute of existing.

- `2026-08-19 | conventions | minor | no | session-file-frozen-file-test`: a real session file in an adopting project came to 46,913 bytes across 56 bullets, mean 825 characters each, against 12,960 for the largest file in this repo. Reviewed with that session rather than about it, and its own analysis produced a better organizing rule than the one proposed here. The proposal was that a session bullet should reference an artifact rather than restate it, argued from duplication. The stronger argument is that a session file is immutable while everything it describes keeps moving, so a summary written here is a frozen copy that silently diverges the first time the artifact is edited, and the immutability rule then forbids correcting it: not duplication, but an unfixable and increasingly wrong copy. That test decides all three observed shapes at once and is now the section's organizing rule. Restated findings fail it (findings are current state). Embedded general principles fail it (a principle is timeless, and freezing it here means it can never be revised, while a convention can). Narrating a reversal passes it, so that stays, narrowed: collapse iteration to outcome except where the discarded path left evidence a reader will find and misread, the case being an attempt reverted in git with no explanation, where the next person retries the same plausible fix. Two additions came from outside that analysis. Coordinating with another session is process, not effect, stated explicitly because cross-session messaging is new enough that the existing "do not log conversational activity" rule does not obviously cover it; a peer exchange producing no local change produces no bullet. And the existing prohibition on another repository's state, previously argued from ownership, now carries the frozen-file form, which is sharper: a bullet recording that another service has no auth test coverage is false the day they add one, in a file that can never be corrected, about a repository nobody reading this one will re-check. Deliberately not added: a size limit. Argued against convincingly by that session, on the grounds that a cap is complied with by dropping entries rather than shortening them, and the first dropped are the hardest to compress, which are the negative results and surprises carrying the most value per byte. A high bullet count on a genuinely large day needs no defence; 825 characters each does.
- `2026-08-19 | conventions | patch | no | session-entries-are-not-self-contained`: the same session reported that the requirement for entries to be self-contained pushed it toward length, since restating shared context across fifty-six entries is mechanically most of the excess. Checked rather than accepted: no such requirement exists. `documentation.md` § Writing for a cold reader states the opposite, explicitly exempting `.dev/sessions/` as "a historical record for someone already oriented, not a first introduction to the topic". So there was no tension to adjudicate, only a rule stated in a file the writer has no reason to open while writing. Restated in `session-discipline.md` where the writing actually happens, with the boundary made operational: an entry conveys what changed and whether it matters to the reader, not enough to reconstruct the reasoning without opening anything. Same shape as several findings this week, a rule that exists, is correct, and sits where its audience never arrives.
- `2026-08-19 | conventions | patch | no | compaction-buffer-is-not-the-session-log`: the cause of the length above was legitimate and worth naming, since the instinct is right and only the destination is wrong. That session had been told to write findings down as they arrived because compaction was expected mid-session, and used the session file as the buffer. The findings also went to `tech-debt.md` and atlas documents, which is where they belonged, so the session file received a second copy of content that already had a living home, produced entirely by a correct instinct. Added: write to the artifact that will own it, and the session file records that the artifact was produced, not what it says.

- `2026-08-19 | conventions | patch | no | index-is-single-developer-by-construction`: the UTC timestamp rule shipped with a false justification, that the index is "written by sessions on different machines in different zones". It is not: the file lives in one developer's own global context, so every session writing to it is the same person on the same host with the same clock, which the rest of the file already assumed by saying "the developer" throughout. The divergence it fixes is real but has a simpler cause, each agent independently choosing `date` or `date -u` with nothing stating which. Corrected the reasoning and kept UTC, on grounds that survive the correction: it is derivable without knowing the host's zone, which still matters for a session running in a container whose zone differs from the host's, and it sorts with no DST discontinuity. Then reversed that reversal within minutes, which is the more instructive half. Told that a shared machine means a shared offset, this session normalized a bare timestamp on the assumption it was local, and the very next check showed six more bare values it could not classify at all: one was provably UTC by being ahead of the current local time, the rest genuinely ambiguous. Sharing an offset only helps once you know which zone a value is already in, which you usually cannot. The conversion had therefore been a guess presented as a conversion, and was reverted. Settled rule: the `Z` suffix is the fix, because it makes a value self-describing; a bare legacy value is left for its own session to restamp and is never reclassified by a reader. Worth recording as a reasoning failure rather than a typo: the rule was right, the argument for it was invented rather than checked, and an invented-but-plausible justification is harder to catch than a wrong rule because nothing downstream misbehaves.
- `2026-08-19 | repo | patch | no | schema-propagation-missed-a-third-time`: the UTC format landed in `template/conventions/agent-index.md` and in neither `template/global-context/agent-index.md` nor the live global copy, whose schema blocks kept a bare `updated:` with no format at all. Third occurrence in three days of a change reaching the convention and not the bootstrap template, after the `id` field and, before that, the file list that never gained `agent-index.md`. The regression scenario added for the second occurrence did not prevent the third, which is the actual finding: a checklist entry that must be remembered fails the same way the rules it documents do. This belongs in `check-consistency.sh` as a mechanical comparison of the schema fields declared in each copy, not as another line telling a contributor to check.

- `2026-08-19 | conventions | patch | no | window-narrows-it-does-not-resolve`: two additions from a handoff by an adopting project's session, which had been mistakenly treated as this one and had been sketching a `base cwd` field with the developer to fix the same mismatch that `id` and the derived `window` since solved. It correctly judged its own sketch superseded and offered only the parts that were still additive, which both were. First: several Members routinely share one `window`, four currently share `softeng`, so a window filter returns a shortlist and never an answer, and the ladder is now honest about that rather than implying the narrowing resolves. That is also the clearest statement of what `id` is actually for, and it strengthens the case a different session had argued against the same day. Second: a derived `window` is whatever the folder is called, which is not always what the project is called now, so a rebrand that has reached the GitHub org but not the local directory leaves a handle prefix looking wrong to any peer meeting it cold, and `id` resolves that mismatch without ever explaining it. Legacy or pre-rebrand basenames are now annotated inline (`window: virusseq-portal (iMicroSeq, pre-rebrand folder name)`) rather than getting a field of their own, since an alias is static and cannot decay the way `id` can, making it safe to persist and cheap to read.
