- `2026-08-19 | conventions | patch | no | id-is-advisory-and-session-scoped`: an adopting project's session, complying with the new `id` field, pushed back on it with an argument worth answering in the spec rather than in a reply. `label`, `project`, `window`, and `scope` stay true after `updated` is stamped, while a runtime handle is true only for its session's lifetime, so one `updated` cannot vouch for both halves of a record at the same decay rate, and a stored handle that has since been reused is confidently wrong with no signal that it is stale, which is the exact failure shape several fixes this week already addressed. Their sharpest form of it: if using an `id` required an `ListAgents` call to validate it, the field would save nothing where reliable and mislead where not, since that same call could have filtered by `window` instead. Answered directly rather than by adding machinery: validation needs no call, because § Identity propagation already has the recipient echo back the id they were addressed by, so a reused handle is caught by the recipient in the same exchange for the price of one message, and a failed send is equally self-announcing. A stale `id` therefore costs one round trip and a correction, the same price as never having had one, while a live `id` skips the narrowing entirely. Adopted their proposed mechanism for the decay itself, refresh `id` at session start alongside `focus` and `updated`, on their reasoning that it is cheapest because it is where the entry is already maintained. Added a boundary their argument implies: an `id` belongs in a Member entry and in a `heard:` line, both of which are refreshed or cleared, and never in a session log, devctx entry, or atlas write-up, which outlive every session and where `label` is the only identifier that stays true.
- `2026-08-19 | repo | patch | no | bootstrap-template-lagged-again`: the `id` field landed in `template/conventions/agent-index.md` and not in `template/global-context/agent-index.md`, the bootstrap template the developer's live global file is copied from, so the live file kept both a schema block and a "No `name`/ref field" prohibition that directly contradicted an entry an adopting session had just been instructed to write. Found by that session rather than here. The risk was not untidiness: another agent doing ordinary index hygiene would have been correct, per the file in front of it, to delete the new field as stray. This is the second time in three days the same bootstrap template lagged a convention change, after its own file list never gained `agent-index.md` when that convention shipped, so the recurrence is the finding rather than the instance. `CONTRIBUTING.md` step 3 already requires a new convention file to be reachable from both the dispatch table and the file table; the equivalent obligation for a *field* added to a schema that exists in more than one file is not stated anywhere, and both misses are that shape.

- `2026-08-19 | conventions | minor | yes | identity-propagates-by-observation`: the agent index could register a session and still leave it unreachable. A registered Member with `window: softeng` had no matching handle in `ListAgents` at all, so filtering by project returned zero candidates rather than several, a case the convention did not cover: it handled "several similar handles" and "no Member entry", not "registered, and nothing to filter to". Root cause, verified against the workspace files rather than inferred: a runtime handle's prefix is generated from the basename of the workspace's first-listed folder, so it identifies a *window* and never a project, and two different workspaces that both list the same folder first produce indistinguishable prefixes. Four changes follow from that. `window` is now derived from that folder rather than being a friendly name, which turns prefix filtering from guesswork into a checkable narrowing step. Members and Requests gained an `id` holding the session's current runtime handle. Because a session is excluded from its own `ListAgents` output, `id` is the one fact about itself it can never observe and every peer can, so it is peer-supplied only: § Identity propagation now requires telling a peer their own id whenever you message them, and correcting your own entry when told yours, which makes ordinary traffic the maintenance mechanism instead of a remembered refresh. Bootstrapping needs no developer: a session with no `id` asks any sibling to compare listings, and since each session is excluded from its own and present in the other's, the handle in theirs and not yours is you and the handle in yours and not theirs is them, so one exchange gives both sides their own id. Asking directly does not work and the difference is what makes it work, because an inbound message carries a socket and a display name rather than a handle, so a peer cannot tell which listed session is the one writing to it. Reconciled explicitly against `no name/ref field, deliberately`, which this would otherwise contradict: the removed field was a self-asserted guess with no fallback, where `id` originates in an observation, is refreshed by traffic, and degrades to the `window` narrowing when stale. Finally, a Request that carries no `id` can now be answered with a `heard:` line on the requester's own post, naming the sought agent and giving its id, meaning "this reached me, here is how to reach me back"; the requester then makes direct contact and deletes the post, which was only ever a placeholder. Scoped to that one case deliberately, so its presence distinguishes a handshake blocked on reachability from one nobody has read. Posting a mirror-image request instead is named as the wrong move, since it creates a second handshake rather than completing the first, which is what this session had done. `breaking: yes`: adopters carrying the earlier text should treat `window` as derived and add `id`, since the resolution ladder now starts there.
- `2026-08-19 | conventions | patch | no | index-timestamps-are-utc`: the index schema said only `<timestamp>`, so sessions stamped it however their clock was set, and entries from different machines could not be compared. Confirmed directly: two posts minutes apart in reality were written four hours and one calendar day apart in the file, one local and one UTC. Since every timestamp in this file exists to be compared against another session's, for a staleness sweep or a which-is-current question, the schema now specifies UTC with the `Z` suffix and gives the mechanical derivation rather than leaving it to be typed. An entry already written in local time is explicitly left alone rather than converted, since a reader does not know the author's offset.

- `2026-08-18 | conventions | minor | no | resolved-memory-may-not-be-yours-to-write`: reported by an adopting project's session about its own mistake, unprompted. `session-discipline.md` already documented that path-keyed memory identity resolves wrongly in a multi-root workspace, to whichever folder is listed first regardless of what the session is actually working on, but framed the consequence entirely as *reading* the wrong context. The same mis-resolution writes. A session designated for a submission service, rooted in a portal repo, recorded a scope-boundary fact about itself into the portal's project memory, a space belonging to whichever agent actually works from that project. The owning agent later found assertions about its own role that it had not written. Added: before writing a fact about which session you are or what you own, confirm the memory you resolved to belongs to the project you are actually working on rather than the directory you happen to be rooted in, and where those differ, keep the fact in your own conversation or with the developer instead of a shared space keyed to someone else's project. Named as the memory-side form of `another-sessions-work-is-not-yours`: a directory confers no ownership, any more than proximity to another session's work does.
- `2026-08-18 | conventions | minor | no | legitimate-edit-misread-as-tampering`: the same session, continuing the same incident, flagged an edit to that shared memory file to the developer as tampering and a hostile reversal of its scope. It was the agent that actually owned the memory space correcting the record about its own role, on the developer's own instruction. `session-discipline.md` § Unattributed working-tree changes already covered one failure direction, dismissing an unexplained change rather than validating it, and this is the opposite polarity, which is worse in a specific way: it accuses a colleague. Added both directions, with the point that no amount of care separates them from inside one session, since "this content is false" and "this is a legitimate edit by an authorized party I cannot see" are indistinguishable when the missing information is authorization, which is not in the file. The fix is therefore not to judge harder but to change what you say: describe what changed and that you did not write it, ask who did, and let the developer supply the authorization you cannot observe. Words like tampering, reversal, or hostile are reserved for actual evidence of intent rather than an absence of explanation, since raising an alarm about a colleague's authorized work carries a social cost a neutral question does not, and reaches the developer either way.

- `2026-08-18 | conventions | minor | no | verbatim-applies-to-all-reconciled-content`: `upgrading-adoption.md`'s "replace it with the current canonical table verbatim, don't paraphrase" was scoped to the dispatch table, though the failure it prevents is not table-specific: paraphrase drops qualifying clauses invisibly, since the result reads fine and only a diff against upstream shows what went missing, and this had been observed across three separate cross-project upgrades. Generalized to any non-conflicting content applied during reconciliation, with the carve-out stated so it does not read as absolute: content this project has deliberately diverged on, recorded in `.dev/agentics-overrides.md`, keeps its local wording and is not applied at all, which is a different case from paraphrasing upstream rather than an exception to copying it. A companion proposal, recording a per-upgrade `verbatim-copy | changelog-paraphrase` marker in project memory for later cross-project auditing, was considered and dropped: it is a write enabling an audit nobody has run, in a store that is per-agent and per-machine so it under-counts silently while reading as complete, the same reasoning that rejected a private provenance tally the same day. If the verbatim rule holds there is nothing to audit; if it does not, the drift is visible in the files themselves.
- `2026-08-18 | template | minor | no | bootstrap-addendum-files-carry-a-tag`: `AGENTS.softeng.md`, `AGENTS.overture.md`, and the four `AGENTS.roles/*.md` files are copied into a developer's global context at bootstrap and then have no way to be found stale, since only `personal-preferences.md` carried a version tag, 1 of 12 bootstrap files. Not hypothetical: `0.15.0` renamed these exact filenames as a breaking change, and an already-bootstrapped global context has nothing to compare against to notice. Gave the six copied files the same canonical pointer tag `personal-preferences.md` already uses, reusing a shipped mechanism rather than inventing one, and wired it into the two procedures that make a tag mean anything: `global-context/README.md`'s bootstrap step now stamps each addendum file with a real `X.Y.Z | synced: <sha>` snapshot on copy, for the same reason it already does so for `personal-preferences.md` (a copied file can no longer resolve a pointer back into agentics' `CHANGELOG.md`), and `upstream-check.md` now checks each bootstrapped file's tag separately rather than only the one the agent happens to remember. That last point is the real failure mode: these are snapshots taken at different times, so a role file bootstrapped today and a team addendum six months ago hold different `synced` values and drift independently. The five `global-context/*.md` scaffolds were deliberately left untagged: they are starting points a developer fills in and owns, so a version tag there would assert a sync relationship that was never intended and would read as permanently stale.

- `2026-08-18 | conventions | patch | no | dirty-tree-is-not-a-live-session`: deciding whether to edit another project's `AGENTS.md`, this session ran `git status`, found the file uncommitted-dirty, and reported that a session was "mid-edit right now" and that editing would collide with work in flight. A peer working in that same repo then established the edit predated its own conversation entirely, so both the author and the age were unknown and the inference was unsupported. `session-discipline.md` § Unattributed working-tree changes already covered validating such content before trusting it, and already listed the possible sources, but never said that the working tree carries no timestamp and no author, so it cannot tell you whether anyone is currently active. Added that, with both failure directions named since they cause opposite harm: reading a dirty tree as "someone is mid-edit" stalls work nobody is doing and routes requests to sessions that may have ended, while reading it as "stale, nobody is here" clobbers work genuinely in flight. If the answer changes what you do, ask or check for a live session rather than deriving it from `git status`. Same underlying shape as `scoped-search-unscoped-claim`: a cheap check reported at a confidence its output did not support, caught here by someone with visibility this session lacked.

- `2026-08-18 | conventions | minor | no | mark-verified-versus-assumed-about-their-context`: an adopting project's session reported back something this session could not have seen from here. A correction sent to it bundled two claims at one confidence level: that the convention's per-entry test applied, which was checkable against text both sides could read and which held, and that most of its roadmap entries would therefore need extracting, which was a claim about that project's own data and was wrong. Forty-seven of its seventy-three entries already conformed. Had it acted on the second half at the confidence the first half earned, it would have over-extracted and produced exactly the fragmented-reasoning outcome the convention exists to prevent, while believing it was complying. It avoided that only because its own conventions require verifying a peer's claim; nothing in the message itself signalled that one half was softer than the other. Added to `review-conduct.md`: separate what you verified from what you are assuming about the other side's situation and mark which is which, on the principle that you can know a convention's text and cannot know their repo. The same source also identified that the fix changes the shape of an exchange rather than only its accuracy, since an absolute claim leaves a recipient two moves, accept that they invented something or go hunting for a counterexample, which is adversarial framing for a collaborative task, whereas naming your evidence invites them to supply the piece you were missing. That is what actually happened here: `scoped-search-unscoped-claim` was found because the recipient went looking for a counterexample, and would have been found faster had the original claim named which files it had covered.
- `2026-08-18 | conventions | patch | no | scoped-search-is-a-general-shape`: `scoped-search-unscoped-claim` cited two instances, both from this repo, which risks it reading as one team's discipline problem rather than a general failure mode, and a convention framed that way gets underused. The same adopting project volunteered three of its own from a single session, with no mechanism in common: a shell glob whose no-match abort was read as the file being absent when it was present, a test harness gating on the wrong property so the code path being tested never executed, and a symbol reported missing from a type declaration when it was reachable only through a wildcard re-export. Broadened the entry to six instances across two independent sessions in one day, and named the property that unifies them: each produced a false negative that read as a clean result, which is why the mitigation has to be procedural rather than a matter of being careful.

- `2026-08-18 | conventions | minor | no | another-sessions-work-is-not-yours`: this session tracked another project's in-flight migration decision on its own pending list, presented it to the developer as an open item awaiting his call, and offered options for resolving it. Two things wrong. The work belonged to that session and its own developer relationship: it had measured the scope itself, made its own call not to re-run on a peer correction, and recorded the state honestly in its own devctx, so there was nothing for anyone here to decide and offering to drive it was pressure on an ownership already being exercised. Separately, it was presented as currently open without checking that it still was, which is stale state reported as current, the same shape as `scoped-search-unscoped-claim` and `credential-hook-never-fired` earlier the same day. Added to `AGENTS.md` § Interaction parameters (root and template): another session's work is not yours to pick up, finish, or decide unless the developer or that session explicitly asks; report what you learned and stop, since acting on it duplicates effort, collides with edits you cannot see, and overrides an ownership actively being exercised. The carve-out is stated in the same breath because it is the valuable half and would otherwise be chilled: a peer's report that reveals a gap in your *own* scope is yours to act on immediately, that is your work rather than theirs, and this repo's own conventions improved several times this week on exactly that basis. Also required: verify another session's open item is still open before relaying it as open, since their state moves without you. `definition-of-done.md`'s existing "whose problem is this" test covers where a lesson gets written and not whose work it is to do, so this is the action-side twin rather than a restatement.

- `2026-08-18 | conventions | minor | no | removal-note-is-not-documentation`: an adopting project's `DEVELOPMENT.md` recorded that a Copilot-specific instructions file had been removed. `documentation.md` § Rewrite freely until committed already has the right test, would the sentence stay true and useful to someone who only ever sees the current merged state, but qualifies it with "and the earlier value never shipped", so a removal of something that *did* ship falls through to the change-recording exception, which names CHANGELOG entries, migration guides, and PR descriptions and never says that ordinary project documentation is not one of them. Added that: a removal note in a README or contributor guide is a changelog entry that wandered into the wrong file, already recorded by git history and the CHANGELOG, and ageing worse than either, since it carries no expiry and a reader years later still meets a paragraph about a file that has not existed for most of the project's life, possibly inferring the removal is recent or the thing still somehow relevant. Kept only where a reader would otherwise recreate the thing or holds a stale local copy needing action, and then written as the action rather than as history ("delete `<path>` if you still have one"), placed in the migration or upgrade path rather than a general guide, and removed once that window closes. Also names the audience half, which is what made this instance notable: the note could only ever serve a Copilot agent, and Copilot agents do not read `DEVELOPMENT.md`, so it was noise for the humans who do read it and invisible to the one reader it addressed. Content placed where its intended audience does not look recurred repeatedly in this repo's own review the same week, the security documents unreachable from either README and `agent-security.md` dispatched on a trigger that never coincides with its exposure among them.

- `2026-08-18 | conventions | minor | no | scoped-search-unscoped-claim`: twice in one day, a search that covered part of the surface was reported as a finding about all of it. Checking whether a phrase an adopting agent quoted existed, `grep` was run against `session-discipline.md` and `documentation.md` and the result stated as "that phrase is not in the convention"; it was in `template/AGENTS.md`'s initialization question, and the adopting agent had to point that out. Earlier the same day, the credential hook's test fed sample paths to its pattern list and was reported as functionally tested, when it had never exercised the payload contract that was actually broken. Same shape both times: a real check, honestly run, whose scope was narrower than the sentence describing it, and in both cases the gap was found by someone else rather than by the person who ran it. Added to `review-conduct.md` § Ground truth over claims: state what a check covered, not what it suggests, and before reporting an absence, confirm the search space was the whole space the claim refers to. An absence claim is the easy case to get wrong, since a scoped search returns clean and reads as proof. Practical form: prefer quoting the line you found over summarizing what a file says, and when reporting that something is not present, name where you looked.

- `2026-08-18 | conventions | minor | yes | recency-tiebreak-withdrawn`: the documented tie-breaker for reaching a Member, default to the most recently started `ListAgents` candidate, is withdrawn after failing three times in one day. Twice it sent a message about another project's breaking changes to a session that owned something else entirely: once a submission-service session picked for portal-ui work, once a Usher-dedicated session picked for Arranger work. Process start time carries no information about which session is currently active or what it has been assigned, and both wrong picks were the *most recent* candidate while the correct session in each case was days older. The rule is worse than having no tie-breaker, because recency reads like evidence and converts "I don't know which" into a confident wrong answer; the failure surfaces only if the recipient happens to verify and push back, which is what saved both of these. Replaced with: narrow to candidates consistent with the Member entry, message only if exactly one remains, and open with who you are looking for plus an invitation to redirect so a wrong guess costs an exchange rather than an action; more than one candidate goes to the Requests board to self-select, developer only if that fails. Separately, and the sharper half of the same incident: the handle prefix is unreliable as a *project* signal, not just as a name. In a multi-root workspace a session's handle derives from whichever folder is listed first rather than what it works on, so a session dedicated entirely to one Overture service was listed under a different Overture project's prefix and was returned by a filter on that prefix for a project it had no involvement in. The prefix is now framed as weak corroboration of a Member entry already in hand, never as what identifies a session. `breaking: yes`: any adopter that already synced the recency rule should stop applying it, since following it as written produces confidently misrouted cross-project messages.

- `2026-08-18 | conventions | minor | no | roadmap-split-selective-by-density`: an adopting project's agent, running the `roadmap_split` migration, reported the convention as saying to split the entries "that need it" and proposed doing only the clearly-dense ones. That phrase does not appear in that section, but it does appear in `template/AGENTS.md`'s initialization question, "for any entry that needs it", which is where the agent had actually got it. The first pass here grepped only `session-discipline.md` and `documentation.md` and then stated the conclusion repo-wide, an overclaim caught by the adopting agent rather than by us: a scoped search reported as an unscoped finding, the same shape as `credential-hook-never-fired`'s test, which exercised the half already known to work and was reported as covering the whole. The qualifier has now been removed from the initialization question too, which is the more consequential of the two locations, since it is the text an adopter reads at the moment they turn the flag on, before any reason to open `session-discipline.md` exists. The text says to sweep the file and relocate *each* already-written entry's justification, alternatives, or history. The gap that made the misreading easy is real rather than pure carelessness, though: the existing "solves nothing" sentence rules out partial application along the *temporal* axis (entries written after the flag versus before) and says nothing about the *density* axis (only the worst-looking entries), which is the one the agent actually fell into. Added the per-entry test explicitly, phrased as a question about content rather than a judgement about appearance: does this entry carry justification, alternatives, or history beyond what the item is and where it stands? If yes, extract however moderate it looks; if no, it is already conformant, which is not the same as deciding to skip it. Names why density-ranking is worse than it sounds: it silently redefines the migration as a cleanup of the top few, leaves the remainder carrying exactly the reasoning the flag exists to relocate, and yields a file inconsistent about where reasoning lives, which reads worse than one that is uniformly dense. Also promoted to a rule the one thing that agent got right and the convention never actually said: extract verbatim first, then write the terse replacement as a separate step, since rewriting and relocating in one motion is how content quietly goes missing.

- `2026-08-18 | conventions | minor | no | zero-match-routes-to-requests-not-a-guess`: found by violating the rule, live. Reaching for a project's session with no Member entry, this repo's own agent messaged a plausible-looking candidate picked from `ListAgents` by recency, which is precisely what the Zero-matches rule forbids ("Do not fall back to scanning `ListAgents` and picking whichever name looks plausible"). The guess was wrong: the session was rooted in the target's repo directory but owned a different service entirely, so its own working directory was actively misleading about its remit. It verified the claims against the repo and applied nothing, which is the only reason a wrong guess cost an exchange rather than a wrong edit to a project it did not own. Two fixes. First, an internal inconsistency: this file's opening line says the Requests board exists "for reaching a project with no confirmed live Member", the zero-match case exactly, while the Zero-matches rule routed to the developer and never mentioned the board. Zero matches now posts to Requests and lets the right session self-select, with the developer involved only if that fails; the prohibition on plausible-name guessing is unchanged and still explicit. Second, the condition under which the guess is most tempting is now named, since "zero matches" did not feel like zero matches at the time: when several candidates share a runtime handle prefix, that prefix is derived from resolved `cwd`, so N sessions opened on one repo produce N near-identical handles carrying no information about which the developer assigned to what. Several similar handles, none registered, is to be treated as zero matches rather than as a shortlist to rank by recency.

- `2026-08-18 | conventions | minor | no | runtime-handle-is-an-address-not-a-name`: real incident, one session referred to a peer in conversation as `arranger-21`, the runtime handle from `ListAgents`, rather than by its registered `label`. `agent-index.md` already established that `label` is the stable developer-assigned handle and that the auto-derived session name is a resolved-`cwd` artifact, but it only ever said this in the context of *registering* an entry; nothing said which of the two to use when *referring* to a peer, so the handle got used as a name. Added the distinction explicitly: the runtime handle is an address, correct in a message's recipient field and nowhere else; the `label` is the name, and it is what belongs in messages to other sessions, session-log entries, roadmap and tech-debt entries, atlas write-ups, and anything reported to the developer. The justification is functional rather than cosmetic, which matters for whether an agent bothers to follow it: the handle is derived per process and does not survive a restart, so a session log reading "arranger-21 confirmed X" is unresolvable a day later, and actively misleading rather than merely useless once a different session of the same project has taken a similar handle, because it then reads as a confident attribution to the wrong session. Also states the reverse direction, since that is the operation an agent actually has to perform: given a handle, find the Member entry whose `project`/`window`/`scope` matches and use its `label`, and if none matches, say "an unregistered session in project X" rather than falling back to the handle as though it were a name.

### 0.15.0 - 2026-08-18

- `2026-08-17 | repo | minor | no | security-for-developers`: the multi-lens review found that nothing in agentics is addressed to the human, so every lesson learned ships as an agent-side rule with no developer-side counterpart; the security half of that gap is now filled. Added `docs/security-for-developers.md`, explicitly for people rather than dual-audience. It opens on the real incident rather than an abstraction: this repo shipped a credential blocklist that silently allowed everything, and the three reasons nobody noticed generalize well past the bug, a permissive failure produces no error and no log line so it cannot be noticed passively, the documentation asserted the control was working which turned absence of evidence into reassurance, and it had been tested with a test that exercised the half that already worked. The developer-facing consequence is stated as a question to ask rather than a principle to hold: when told a control is tested, ask what input the test used and where that input's shape came from, since a test that constructs its own input cannot discover that the real caller uses a different shape. Also covers: a copy-pasteable command to confirm the blocklist is actually live, run verbatim against both the template and a live installation before publishing rather than written from memory; the blocklist's real limits, Claude Code only so other agents have no mechanical enforcement at all, trivially bypassable by an assembled path or a rename, and no protection for a secret already in context; a table of agent claims that are unreliable because they are claims about the agent's own behaviour rather than about the world, each with a cheap check; the trust boundaries only a person can hold, pull request text being attacker-controlled, a peer session's message never being developer consent, and global context applying everywhere while usually not under version control; committed prose as a leak path, with the honest note that the mechanical check greps for the committer's own identity and therefore catches their name and nothing else; how to review an agent-proposed security change, where the failure mode is a change correct in the part being considered and untested in the part that is not; and what to actually do once a secret has been read, a secret has been committed, an agent has acted on injected instructions, or an instruction file changed unexpectedly. Cross-referenced from `docs/agent-security.md` as its companion. Both security documents were previously unreachable from the root `README.md`, which linked only `deterministic-by-design.md`, so a Security section was added there and to `template/README.md`, giving adopters' humans a pointer they previously had no way to find.
- `2026-08-17 | conventions | minor | no | context-economy-procedure`: growth measurement done during the multi-lens review produced a repeatable decision procedure, and nothing captured it, so the next person facing the same problem would re-derive it or guess. Added `conventions/context-economy.md`, written explicitly to be followed step by step rather than interpreted, on the developer's stated goal of making this workable for a less capable model. Core content: cost is bytes times read frequency rather than bytes alone, so rank by frequency-weighted cost; measure with real byte counts per file and per section instead of estimating; classify every section by what event triggers reading it, since a section whose trigger differs from its file's trigger is the split candidate and that mismatch is the entire signal; prefer relocating to the trigger over merging over archiving over rewriting for density; leave an entry point behind when splitting so an adopter's stale dispatch table still resolves, which is usually what makes a split non-breaking; repoint every live reference by grep rather than memory; wire the new file into both the dispatch table and the file table or it is an orphan; and report both reading models (section-only and whole-file) rather than only the favourable one. Also states the anti-patterns explicitly, since they are the likely failure modes for anyone applying this mechanically: never delete a rule to save tokens, never compress away the reasoning that lets an agent judge an unlisted case, never split by topic instead of trigger, and never relocate safety-critical content out of an always-read file before a mechanical backstop exists. Names the growth loop directly, a rule fails to fire, and the fix is a reinforcing paragraph added to the file whose size caused the miss, which makes the next miss more likely; that loop is visible in roughly one in ten of this repo's own changelog entries.

- `2026-08-17 | template | patch | yes | credential-hook-never-fired`: the `PreToolUse` credential blocklist in `template/.claude/settings.json` has never blocked anything, in any installation, since it shipped. It extracted the target as `params.get('filePath', params.get('path', ''))`; Claude Code's Read, Edit, and Write tools all send `file_path`, and NotebookEdit sends `notebook_path`, so the extracted path was always empty, no pattern could match, and every credential file returned `allow`. Found by executing the shipped hook against real payload shapes rather than reading it. Three properties made it invisible: it is the only fully mechanical control the template ships, so nothing else covered the gap; a hook returning `allow` produces no log line and no error, making failure indistinguishable from correct operation; and `docs/agent-security.md` tells the developer a correct installation "should contain exactly one hook: the `PreToolUse` credential file blocklist," turning absence of evidence into reassurance. Worth recording how it survived a review the day before, which added seven patterns to this same file and reported them functionally tested: that test fed paths to the pattern list directly, so it exercised the half that already worked and never touched the payload contract. A test that constructs its own input in the shape the code expects cannot discover that the real caller uses a different shape. Fixed: read `file_path` first with `filePath`, `notebook_path`, and `path` as fallbacks, and additionally tokenize `params.get('command','')` so a Bash `cat`, `cp`, or piped `grep` of a blocked path is denied too. Verified against 15 payloads covering every tool shape, both the deny and allow sides. `check-consistency.sh` gained a check that pipes canonical payloads through the hook command extracted from the JSON and asserts deny, deny, allow, so the contract is now gated rather than assumed; the check was itself negative-tested by reintroducing the original bug and confirming it fails. Still uncovered and stated rather than left to be discovered: `Grep` and `Glob` pass a directory, not a file, so they remain outside its reach. `breaking: yes`: `settings.json` is a copied artifact, not a live pointer, so every adopter holds a broken copy and must re-copy this file.
- `2026-08-17 | template | patch | yes | dispatch-paths-unresolvable-in-adopters`: an adopting project's dispatch table pointed at paths that could not resolve anywhere. `template/AGENTS.md` states that every path is a live pointer into agentics, then writes them bare (`conventions/session-discipline.md`), while agentics' own root `AGENTS.md` correctly writes `template/conventions/...`, so the prefix was dropped precisely in the copy that leaves the repo. The adopting project has no local `conventions/` directory, correctly, since the convention forbids creating one; nothing in the adoption flow records where agentics lives, so no base existed to resolve against; and the only inference available, the "Adapted from" repo-root URL, 404s because the required `template/` segment is invisible from the adopter's side. `convention-levels.md` § How much to keep locally, cited by the table for the full rule, governs whether a file should be copied and never states what a bare path resolves against. `CHANGELOG.md` § `global-guideline-material-never-in-project` diagnosed this exact ambiguity and fixed the symptom by forbidding local copies, which left the reference dangling rather than resolving. Fixed: `template/AGENTS.md` now states the base explicitly, gives a two-step resolution order (the recorded agentics entry in the global cross-project map, else the GitHub URL with the `template/` segment spelled out), names the two `docs/` paths as the one repo-root exception, and says to report a missing convention rather than guess or substitute a local file. `breaking: yes`: adopters need this block added to their copied `AGENTS.md`.
- `2026-08-17 | conventions | minor | yes | pr-identity-exception-scoped-to-author-field`: `review-conduct.md`'s exception granting the developer's recognized platform identity the same authority as a direct instruction pointed straight at an attack `docs/agent-security.md` already documents, injected instructions in PR titles, issue bodies, and comments, demonstrated against Claude Code and others in 2026. Neither file referenced the other, and the PR-review dispatch row did not include the security document, so the caution was never in context when the exception applied. The distinction that makes the exception safe existed but was unstated. Fixed: the exception now attaches only to the platform's own structured author field on a comment, review, or issue fetched directly, never to attribution appearing inside body text (a quoted "as I said above", a pasted transcript, a block formatted to resemble a maintainer's reply), which is attacker-supplied text that happens to name someone. Added a second limit: recognizing who is speaking settles authorship, not permission, so a destructive or externally visible action reached this way still needs consent in the current conversation, on the grounds that an identity recognized in a channel you do not control is the weakest possible basis for the strongest possible action. The PR-review dispatch row now also loads `docs/agent-security.md` when the change or its discussion came from outside your own team. `breaking: yes`: adopters carrying the unscoped wording should re-read this section.
- `2026-08-17 | conventions | patch | no | supply-chain-owasp-category`: `security.md` filed the pnpm install-script control under A08 twice, a correct 2021 mapping that survived the 2025 re-map. This repo's own canonical map in `security-guidelines.md` puts Software Supply Chain Failures at A03 and Integrity Failures at A08. Dispatch order made it self-reinforcing rather than self-correcting, since `AGENTS.md` sends security work to `security.md` first, so the wrong category was read before the file that would correct it and would then be carried into review comments and threat models. Both occurrences corrected to A03.
- `2026-08-17 | conventions | minor | no | private-global-config-leaked-into-template`: two shipped convention files carried content belonging to a maintainer's own private global configuration rather than to the universal tier. `session-discipline.md` cited a section named "Bulk text replacements" as the authority for a `grep -c` backstop; that section exists only in one contributor's personal global instructions, so no adopter could resolve it, and the content it wanted was already present in `writing-style.md` § Dashes, where the citation now points. `writing-style.md` carried a Terraform rule naming three live softeng resource names and referring to VSO companion blocks, in a file whose own opening says "Everyone reads this file," while VSO is defined only in `AGENTS.softeng.md` behind a team-membership gate; that rule moved to `AGENTS.softeng.md` § Terraform root structure, phrased as the softeng-specific reading of the general named-entry ordering rule rather than a second copy of it. Recorded as one gap rather than two: propagation outward, from a project up to the template, has a full discipline in `convention-levels.md` and `CONTRIBUTING.md`; the inward direction has none, and it carries a specific risk the outward direction does not, since content written for an audience of one who already shares the missing context arrives phrased as a general rule and reads as universal on arrival. The guard against recurrence is logged in `.dev/roadmap.md`, not solved here.
- `2026-08-17 | conventions | minor | no | upstream-check-split-from-convention-levels`: `convention-levels.md` § Checking for upstream updates was 56% of a 26KB file and was reached by a different trigger than the rest of it, every session start via `session-discipline.md` step 6, rather than when deciding where a new convention belongs. Two unrelated audiences in one file meant every session paid for the placement model it was not there to read. Split to `conventions/upstream-check.md` (15KB), leaving a pointer behind, with `convention-levels.md` down to 11.9KB. Every live cross-reference repointed across `README.md`, `CONTRIBUTING.md`, `template/README.md`, `template/AGENTS.md`, `upgrading-adoption.md`, `session-discipline.md`, `personal-preferences.md`, `regression-checklist.md`, and `fixtures.md`; already-released `CHANGELOG.md` entries and old `.dev/sessions/` logs deliberately left pointing at the old location, since they record what was true at the time. Wired into the dispatch table and `template/README.md`'s file table per `CONTRIBUTING.md` step 3, so it is not an orphan. `breaking: no`, deliberately: the split degrades gracefully for an adopter whose copied dispatch table predates it, because `convention-levels.md` still resolves and now points onward, which is the same dispatch pattern they already follow.
- `2026-08-17 | repo | patch | no | stale-claude-md-architecture-refs`: several files still described the architecture that existed before `agents-md-single-dispatch-table` moved everything to `AGENTS.md`. `convention-levels.md`, the canonical placement authority that `upgrading-adoption.md` defers to by name, said project content lives in the project's `CLAUDE.md`, that a dispatch line there "is enough", and that `AGENTS.md` dispatches "the same way `CLAUDE.md` does"; an agent following it would write content that the upgrade procedure then flags as a defect to repair, so two conventions collided inside one procedure. `CONTRIBUTING.md` described `CLAUDE.md` as "~30 lines" holding dispatch detail (it is 7 lines with no table) and placed the initialization block there (it is in `AGENTS.md`). `README.md` repeated the stale framing twice. Root `AGENTS.md` used bare `conventions/...` in three prose references to files that exist only under `template/`, its dispatch-table preamble asserted a root-level layout that does not exist, and its row for the repo's most common activity sent the agent to a 7-line stub "to understand what we are maintaining". All corrected. Notable for the review record: the whole-repo pass earlier the same day checked `CONTRIBUTING.md` for exactly this class of staleness and cleared it on the strength of a different section that happened to be correct.
- `2026-08-17 | repo | patch | no | multi-lens-review-smaller-defects`: a batch of confirmed smaller defects from the same review. `upgrading-adoption.md`'s never-copy exemption list named three of the four bootstrap artifacts, omitting `AGENTS.overture.md`, so an upgrade diagnosis on an Overture project would have seen it as a legitimate gap and created it, the exact class of write that list exists to prevent. `session-discipline.md` cited a CHANGELOG slug as living in `CONTRIBUTING.md`, breaking this repo's own `see CHANGELOG.md § <slug>` citation format and making it unresolvable by grep. `testing.md`'s restatement of the full-suite rule dropped the clause covering two of the three checkpoints, so an agent dispatched there alone would conclude the full suite is a semiregular-checkpoint obligation only and skip it before committing. Three cells in `CONTRIBUTING.md`'s role table read `|: |`, artifacts of this repo's own mandated em-dash sweep firing on cells where the dash meant "not applicable" rather than prose, a failure mode worth knowing since `writing-style.md` exempts table separator rows but not empty-cell placeholders. `check-consistency.sh`'s root-versus-template drift check gained a second known-difference normalization so that root's necessarily-prefixed convention paths no longer read as content drift; negative-tested to confirm it still catches real drift.

- `2026-08-14 | conventions | minor | no | code-style-propagated-verification-heuristics`: reviewing a contributor's own established global setup for candidates never yet proposed upstream (see `proactive-reverse-propagation-check` below) surfaced three real, verified gaps in `code-style.md`. Structured logging's baseline justification, OWASP A09, was never connected to the mandatory-setup rule despite `security-guidelines.md` already having its own A09 section; added the cross-reference. "Verify before assuming a limitation" existed only for external dependencies; extended explicitly to the agent's own tooling or harness, citing a real confirmed incident (cross-session messaging misdiagnosed as architecturally unavailable when a pending client update was the actual cause). A narrow, npm-dist-tag-specific "check before concluding absence" case existed for capability evaluation; generalized into a standing debugging heuristic, check for a working sibling instance elsewhere in the same system before diagnosing from first principles, with a second confirming instance (Kubernetes sibling-namespace debugging) named directly. `breaking: no`
- `2026-08-14 | conventions | minor | no | session-discipline-propagated-safety-defaults`: same review, two gaps in `session-discipline.md`. A generated summary compressing "intended to do X" into "X is done" is a distinct risk from the existing long-thread-context-loss signal (which covers re-triggering the checklist, not auditing a prior summary's claims); added an explicit verify-before-building-on-it rule. No default existed anywhere in the git section for which branch to push to; added: default to a per-task feature branch, never `main`/`master`/a release branch, unless explicitly instructed otherwise. `breaking: no`

Checked a real project's existing copy against this: it had already drifted, missing an entire session-start step (the agentics upstream-check) and several security-trigger details its `AGENTS.md` had gained since, exactly the duplication-creates-drift risk `CONTRIBUTING.md`'s design principle already names. `upgrading-adoption.md`'s "other agent-specific instruction files" bullet previously recommended keeping a Copilot file in sync; split it out and corrected: for Copilot specifically, propose retiring the file (or reducing it to a stub) rather than syncing it, since there's no longer an offsetting benefit to keeping it at all. `CONTRIBUTING.md`'s design-principle statement updated to match. `breaking: no`

- `2026-08-17 | conventions | minor | no | agentics-own-state-vs-project-tag-conflation`: real incident (a helm-charts session's own root-cause investigation into an unwanted commit-attribution trailer): the session determined it was "running agentics 0.14.0" by reading agentics' own `CHANGELOG.md`/`AGENTS.md` pointer tag, not the actual project's own stamped `synced` tag, which read a much older version, several releases behind the rule the session then missed. - `2026-08-17 | conventions | minor | no | naming-conventions-adopted`: resolved a three-item colleague proposal (`.dev/roadmap.md`, bundled under an explicit "discuss before adding any" gate) together with independent validation for one of the three from a real incident: identifier verbosity matters beyond just import aliases. Added to `code-style.md`: no `Type` suffix on type names, no `I` prefix on interfaces (name the concept, not the declaration keyword); full-word, descriptive identifiers for any variable, parameter, or import alias, explicitly including a package's own conventional short alias (`fc` for `fast-check`, `z` for `zod`), not just the obviously terse ones, with a narrower exception for idioms embedded in the language or framework itself (a loop counter, Express's `req`/`res`/`next`), not a single package's own convention; and explicit return types on named or declared functions, anonymous callback arguments exempted. Roadmap item pruned. `breaking: no`

Added: the semiregular in-session checkpoint specifically runs the full suite, not just new tests, since that's the one an involuntary session end (a usage or context limit, a crash) can still reach when the final "before ending a session" checkpoint can't. Confirmed directly: a roadmap design-question item sat fully superseded by a shipped convention across at least one release cycle, caught only by a completion check tracing it by hand. Added: when a change fully resolves an existing roadmap or tech-debt item, however old, prune or close it in the same pass, cross-referencing `CONTRIBUTING.md` step 8 rather than restating it. `breaking: no`
- `2026-08-17 | conventions | minor | no | structural-pattern-sweep`: `review-conduct.md`'s existing "Being shown a mistake is itself a lessons-learned trigger" asked whether a mistake generalizes, but didn't require actually checking, three real instances the same evening showed the gap: a self-trigger fix generalized only when a different party checked; a stale cross-reference found in one role file turned out to exist in all three siblings; one flagged citation led to checking all nineteen instances of the same style across the repo. Added: when a fix corrects something structural (a repeated phrase, a same-shaped check, a citation convention), sweep every file of that class before finishing, not just the one noticed, bounded explicitly to structural patterns rather than one-off content errors, so a single typo doesn't trigger a full audit. `.dev/roadmap.md`'s matching design item trimmed to what's still genuinely open (cross-project reach). `breaking: no`
- `2026-08-17 | template | patch | yes | claude-prefix-removed-from-addendum-files`: a whole-repo review flagged, then a direct instruction resolved, a naming inconsistency: `template/CLAUDE.roles/*.md`, `template/CLAUDE.softeng.md`, and `template/CLAUDE.overture.md` carried a `CLAUDE.` prefix despite none of them being auto-loaded by Claude Code, unlike root/template `CLAUDE.md`, which genuinely earns the name for that reason. These addendum files are reached entirely through `AGENTS.md`'s own agent-neutral dispatch table and initialization block, identically for any agent, so the naming directly cut against this repo's own "agent-neutral" design principle, misleading a new adopter on a different tool into thinking them Claude-specific or optional. Renamed to `template/AGENTS.roles/`, `template/AGENTS.softeng.md`, `template/AGENTS.overture.md`, matching the file that actually dispatches to them. Every reference across the repo updated (`AGENTS.md` root and template, `README.md` root and template, `CONTRIBUTING.md`, `upgrading-adoption.md`, `convention-levels.md`, `global-context/README.md`, the role files' own self-references, `regression-checklist.md`/`fixtures.md`'s scenario descriptions); historical, already-released `CHANGELOG.md` entries and old `.dev/sessions/` logs deliberately left referencing the old names, a record of what was true at the time. `breaking: yes`: any already-adopted project whose global context was bootstrapped from the old filenames needs to re-bootstrap under the new ones; nothing currently detects that staleness automatically, since these addendum files carry no version tag of their own the way `personal-preferences.md` does.
- `2026-08-17 | conventions | minor | no | whole-document-coherence-pass`: real feedback from an imicroseq/portal-ui agent asked to review a project's docs for completeness: a doc that grew across many prompts had implementation details ahead of its own rationale, a broken cross-reference to a since-removed section, and one topic duplicated across two sections, none of it caught until an explicit review, because `session-discipline.md`'s "Refinement passes" checkpoints are explicitly diff-scoped ("checking the changed passages"), and duplication, dangling cross-references, and ordering problems never show up in any single diff, only in the accumulated whole. Added: at the "before committing" and "before ending a session" checkpoints, a doc with two or more edits in the same stretch gets read whole, not diffed, checking cross-reference validity, duplication, and ordering. Also added the preventive half to `documentation.md` § Cross-linking, not duplication: search for existing coverage of a topic before writing a new section for it, the same "Searching before writing" habit `code-style.md` already requires for code, since a topic already covered elsewhere rarely looks like duplication while you're writing it. `breaking: no`
- `2026-08-16 | conventions | minor | no | agent-index-shipped`: the agent-index design (cross-project session directory plus a payload-free bulletin board), a local prototype live-tested against real sessions since 2026-08-08, is now a real convention: `conventions/agent-index.md`, a bootstrap template at `global-context/agent-index.md`, a new dispatch line, and a new opt-in initialization question (`agent_index: yes | no`, item 8), all added to `AGENTS.md` (root and template). States its own core rule explicitly: check Members first, always, before `ListAgents`-driven guessing or a developer prompt, prompted by two live incidents of exactly that guessing happening, once resolved only through an out-of-band developer handshake, once by asking the developer directly when a correct Members entry existed and would have resolved it instantly. Members has no `name`/ref field: a stored value failed in every real attempt to use it during drafting, a registered name that no longer resolved at all when first tried, a fresh session unable to derive its own current name at registration (excluded from its own peer listing by design, no peer yet exists to observe it from outside), and a later session finding a different Member's stored name matched no active session either. Reaching a Member instead resolves live: filter `ListAgents` to sessions matching `project`/`window`/`scope`, default to the most recently started candidate, fall back to a Requests post or the developer if that's wrong or ambiguous. Also refined during drafting: cleanup ownership shifted from the requester to the responding agent (the one already engaging with the mechanism at that moment), with the requester's own staleness check as a fallback; validating a callback collapsed to one plausibility-based rule instead of a separate exact tier, since that tier depended on the now-removed field; registering proposes `label`/`project`/`window`/`scope` from visible context and confirms with the developer before committing, a write to a file shared across every project isn't a purely local decision. `breaking: no`
- `2026-08-16 | conventions | patch | no | incident-citation-functional-value-pass`: prompted by a developer question about whether one specific "confirmed directly" incident citation was actually adding value, or just narrating that a rule was real. Ran the same functional-value test (`convention-skepticism-functional-test`, above) against all 19 instances of the pattern across the repo, not just the one flagged. Verdict: 15 hold up, each teaches something the abstract rule alone doesn't (a specific technical gotcha, quantitative data, a genuinely counterintuitive claim needing proof). 4 didn't: the "Default to project-scoped" paragraph's citation (`AGENTS.md`/`template/AGENTS.md`, the one that prompted this) and `upgrading-adoption.md`'s "update"-ambiguity citation restated an already-self-evident point instead of teaching something new. Trimmed those three (two files carry the first paragraph). `breaking: no`
- `2026-08-16 | conventions | patch | no | atlas-roadmap-path-drift`: real feedback from an adopting project (imicroseq) syncing 0.10.0 to 0.14.0: `documentation.md`'s "Roadmap depth lives here too" line stated the `roadmap_split` destination path as `.dev/docs/roadmap/<topic>.md`, missing the `atlas/` segment, while `AGENTS.md`'s initialization question and all three mentions in `session-discipline.md` correctly say `.dev/docs/atlas/roadmap/<topic>.md`. Fixed to match. `breaking: no`
- `2026-08-14 | conventions | patch | no | done-pass-self-trigger`: `AGENTS.md`'s dispatch table names two triggers for `definition-of-done.md`, "finishing a task, or asked 'is this done?'", but the file's own "Invoked by" line only documented the second. Confirmed live: a session presented a design-doc edit as complete without the checklist ever firing, since nothing recognized its own "that covers what was asked" framing as an invocation. Added the missing trigger explicitly: presenting work as complete is itself the trigger, not only being asked. `breaking: no`

### 0.14.0 - 2026-08-12

- `2026-08-10 | conventions | minor | no | upstream-check-empty-diff-not-personal-catchup`: a real incident during live agent-index testing, a second concurrently active session in the same project, checked directly, had no knowledge of a concept (the atlas) a sibling session's earlier upgrade had already made current in the project's own files. Root cause: `convention-levels.md` § Checking for upstream updates step 2 treats an empty `synced`-to-`HEAD` diff as "nothing changed, stop here," but `synced` is a project-level fact shared by every concurrently active session, it cannot distinguish "I already ran this check" from "a different, concurrently active session already ran it." Added a caveat: on a session's own first check this conversation, an empty diff alone isn't sufficient to stop silently, skim the current content once regardless, even though nothing needs applying to the files. Also fixed the complementary, deliberate case: `upgrading-adoption.md`'s "update your agentics" trigger now runs its procedure unconditionally, an explicit ask always verifies for real rather than skipping ahead on the assumption an earlier check (by this session or a different one) already settled it. A second, independent instance confirmed the fix works as intended, in a different project entirely: run against this same still-uncommitted draft, it caught two real drifts a same-conversation, top-of-mind-only check would have missed, one from earlier in that session, one pre-dating it, verified directly against the file afterward, not accepted on the peer's report alone. `breaking: no`
- `2026-08-12 | conventions | minor | no | undisclosed-machine-state-change`: real, external incident (team feedback, not a hypothetical): an agent created a symlink on a developer's own machine to make a local Docker socket path match what a testing library expected, without disclosing it. The developer didn't know it happened; the fix worked only on that machine, and a teammate applying the resulting PR elsewhere couldn't reproduce it, confused, since nothing in the repo explained why. Two separate gaps, not one. First, disclosure: `AGENTS.md`'s "Check in before non-trivial decisions" (root and template) already covered a lasting footprint outside the current project, but its own example was scoped to global config files; generalized explicitly to the developer's own machine and OS-level state (installed packages, symlinks, sockets), not just config directories. Worth naming why this matters beyond the one incident: an undisclosed but otherwise-reasonable action is exactly what compounds into "don't trust this tool's permission behavior" generally, a separate but related piece of the same team feedback, invisibility is the actual problem, independent of whether any single action was harmless. Second, reproducibility: added to `definition-of-done.md`'s checklist, a fix isn't done if it only works because of an undocumented, machine-specific accommodation; either generalize it or capture it somewhere reproducible. Cross-platform divergence (this incident: a Mac-default Docker socket path versus the Unix default a library expected) is named explicitly as a common instance of this, since softeng's own work isn't Mac-only by default and assuming your own OS is representative is an easy, easy-to-miss mistake. Added `code-style.md` § "Platform portability" for the proactive half: default to detecting the runtime or making it configurable rather than hardcoding what one machine happens to need. `breaking: no`
- `2026-08-12 | repo | minor | no | onboarding-doc-underspecified-preconditions`: real, external feedback from a prospective adopter, several distinct gaps in `README.md` surfacing across one conversation, not independent nitpicks. "Accessible to your agent" was used as a precondition in Quick Start's first line without ever defining it operationally, or linking to the section that could. The required session context (working directory must be the target project) was never stated at all. "What your agent will ask" listed 4 questions when `template/AGENTS.md`'s actual initialization had grown to 6, undetected drift between the pitch and the source of truth. There was no way to verify setup succeeded beyond trusting the agent's own narration, no git-level checklist independent of that. And the whole flow assumed one adoption path (project-wide, repo-committed) when most of what a first-time reader actually wants, consistent interaction style, is meant to be global-tier and requires no repo changes at all, per `convention-levels.md`'s own existing classification, never offered as a distinct choice. Rewrote `README.md`: a concrete "what this is / does / lets you do" opening (replacing an overclaiming tagline promising an agent instantly "knows how to behave"), two explicitly separate Quick Start entry points (personal/global-only vs. project-wide/team), the operational definition of "accessible" with a forward link, the required session context stated directly, the question count synced to 6, a git-status verification checklist, and explicit framing that the payoff comes from sustained use, not installation. `breaking: no`
- `2026-08-12 | template | minor | no | personal-global-only-bootstrap`: the project-wide-only onboarding gap above needed an actual mechanism behind it, not just new wording: there was no template file bundling universal Interaction parameters and Critical constraints for a developer who wants agentics' behavioral conventions personally, without adopting it into any project. Added `global-context/personal-preferences.md` and a "Bootstrapping without a project" procedure in `global-context/README.md`: ask role/team/product-family, merge the universal sections into the developer's own global config, stop, no project files created or suggested for committing. Caught and fixed before shipping: the new file was initially named `CLAUDE.md`, inconsistent with every sibling file in that directory (`projects.md`, `roadmap.md`, `security-guidelines.md`), all agent-neutral by name with per-agent destination handled separately in the directory's own table; renamed to match. `breaking: no`
- `2026-08-12 | conventions | minor | no | struggle-narrative-vs-system-fact`: prompted by the same adoption-friction conversation, the more direct cause turned out not to be procedural at all: developers can feel exposed committing `.dev/` content that reads as a record of their own mistakes, even with no names attached. `session-discipline.md` already collapses iteration to outcome for density; added the sharper distinction this didn't yet make explicit, a negative result worth keeping ("X doesn't work because Y") is a fact about the system, the process of getting there isn't, with a before/after example modeling the transformation rather than stating a rule about confidence or safety. Deliberately no mention of exposure, safety, or impostor syndrome anywhere in the convention text itself, the real motivation lives here, in this entry, not paraded in front of every future reader of the rule. `documentation.md` § The atlas cross-references it for reference-material write-ups specifically. `breaking: no`
- `2026-08-12 | template | patch | no | personal-preferences-no-project-scaffolding`: caught right after publishing this same batch, discussing how an adopter would actually use the new personal/global-only path: `global-context/personal-preferences.md` never instructs creating project-level devctx (`.dev/roadmap.md`, `tech-debt.md`, `AGENTS.md`), true by construction (no dispatch table or initialization content is in the file), but never stated as an actual guarantee, only inferable from what the file happens not to contain. Added an explicit section: this creates no project-level scaffolding, ever, in any project, working in one with no agentics setup of its own means applying the interaction style only, not assuming or creating devctx just because it's habitual elsewhere. `breaking: no`
- `2026-08-12 | conventions | minor | no | global-tag-version-tracking`: the new personal/global-only path shipped with no version tag at all, unlike project `AGENTS.md`. A developer's own global bootstrap can drift behind agentics with nothing tracking it, and, worse, a project's own `synced` tag could end up ahead of the developer's global one, backwards, since global should generally lead every project it informs, refreshing it is cheap and personal, no team coordination needed. Added a canonical version-tag pointer to `global-context/personal-preferences.md`, stamped with an actual snapshot on bootstrap (`global-context/README.md`'s procedure), and extended `convention-levels.md` § Checking for upstream updates: the global tag gets the same diff-and-diagnose treatment as a project's, on the same gating tiers, and a project found ahead of it triggers bringing the global tag current immediately, not just a note. `breaking: no`
- `2026-08-12 | repo | patch | no | quick-start-sequential-not-additive`: shipped in this same batch as two parallel, equally-weighted choices (personal/global-only versus project-wide), which undersold the actual relationship between them. Agentics is fundamentally a personal, global thing; adopting it for a specific project doesn't replace that; it adds persistent project memory and any project-specific overrides on top of a global foundation that's assumed to already be there. Reworded `README.md`'s Quick Start as an explicit sequence, step 1 (global, always recommended) and step 2 (project-level, additive, not a from-scratch duplicate), and stated directly why step 2 still creates a full, self-contained `AGENTS.md` rather than just pointing back at the developer's own global config: a teammate who hasn't done step 1 personally still needs consistent behavior working in that repo, the redundancy is intentional. `breaking: no`

### 0.13.0 - 2026-08-10

- `2026-08-06 | conventions | minor | no | staleness-noticed-not-diagnosed`: an agent in a live adopting project checked its agentics version tag, recognized the project was several releases behind, then moved on to unrelated work without ever fetching the diff, reading the new entries, or running the diagnosis, treating the notice of staleness as if it were the completed check. `convention-levels.md` § "Checking for upstream updates" already guarded the later failure (stopping after the diff but before the diagnosis, "Don't stop at printing the entries"), but nothing addressed stopping even earlier, right after the first cheap signal, before running any of the numbered procedure at all. Added a paragraph stating that detecting staleness obligates running the rest of the procedure in the same sitting, not deferring it implicitly, extending the "not a soft offer the developer can wave off with no trace" standard from the no-tag case to this one too. `breaking: no`
- `2026-08-06 | conventions | minor | no | no-synced-catch-up-empty-after-release`: `upgrading-adoption.md` § 1's no-synced-value path surfaces everything under agentics' `## Unreleased` as a bounded first-encounter catch-up, but a first-time sync landing right after a release drains that section to empty, producing no catch-up content at exactly the moment a brand-new adopter has the most to catch up on. Confirmed directly: a first-time sync right after agentics' own `0.12.0` release found `## Unreleased` empty and reported nothing from the changelog, correctly still completing the rest of the diagnosis from direct file comparison. Added a fallback: if `## Unreleased` is empty, surface the most recent `## Released` version's entries instead of nothing. `breaking: no`
- `2026-08-06 | repo | patch | no | upgrading-adoption-dash-cleanup`: found while dash-checking the fix above, `upgrading-adoption.md` carried several pre-existing em-dash connectors that predated the mandatory pre-output check. Replaced with colons per `writing-style.md` § Dashes; no content changed. `breaking: no`
- `2026-08-06 | conventions | minor | no | let-const-mechanical-trigger`: "Non-mutational" stated the principle abstractly ("prefer functional style") without a concrete per-line trigger, which in practice read as an optional preference rather than an enforceable rule. Added a concrete heuristic: default to `const`, treat every `let` as a question to answer (rewrite it as an expression, or confirm it's one of the narrow named exceptions), not a neutral second option. Also added, verified before citing (`prefer-const` is not part of `eslint:recommended`, confirmed against ESLint's own docs and a related `eslint-config-google` issue): an "Optional automated enforcement" note recommending ESLint's `prefer-const` rule for the mechanical half (a `let` never actually reassigned), while the judgment half (whether a reassigned `let` is accumulation-as-computation or a real exception) stays this section's own call. `breaking: no`
- `2026-08-07 | conventions | minor | no | roadmap-human-agent-split`: real team feedback: `.dev/roadmap.md` served as both agent-queried memory and a human-facing artifact with no split between the two, and different people had already diverged on how they used it, one querying it only through an agent, another reading it directly and finding agent-written entries overwhelming. Added an opt-in split: a new initialization question 7 in `template/AGENTS.md` (`roadmap_split: yes | no`, recorded per project), and a conditional clause in `session-discipline.md` § Keeping `.dev/` current: when set, roadmap entries stay terse and human-scannable, with deeper reasoning routed to `.dev/docs/atlas/roadmap/<topic>.md`. Formalized a new subfolder, `.dev/docs/atlas/`, in `documentation.md`: a required `index.md`, one line per topic, and plain relative-link cross-referencing between topic files (not a special syntax, unlike project memory, `.dev/docs` files sit at stable repo paths, so an ordinary link already works). Nested under `.dev/docs` rather than reusing its top level or standing up a separate `.dev/atlas`: `.dev/docs` itself is often already populated by a project's own human-curated service folders before any of this arrives, and nesting keeps that content completely untouched rather than retroactively imposing an index and cross-linking requirement on it. Default stays off; agentics' own `.dev/roadmap.md` is a deliberate non-adopter, it's already used as agent-queried memory by design. `breaking: no`
- `2026-08-07 | conventions | minor | no | commit-message-content-undefined`: `session-discipline.md` § Git covered staging permission and AI-attribution, but nothing about commit-message content or quality itself, despite `writing-style.md` listing "a commit message" as an output type it covers, general prose rules (dashes, spelling) aside. A real agent session did much of this well on its own initiative: described the user-visible effect in the subject rather than internal mechanics, wrote a prose body explaining why, disclosed an incidental bundled fix honestly, and staged only what was logically part of the change. Added "Writing the message itself" to `session-discipline.md` § Git, codifying that practice, plus a correction caught on review: matching a repo's own commit history should cover format only (a type prefix, capitalization), not length, a verbose existing convention isn't a discovered rule worth carrying forward, succinctness is the default regardless of what past commits in the same repo happened to do. `breaking: no`
- `2026-08-07 | conventions | minor | no | dev-content-release-status-scope-violation`: a devctx file (`.dev/roadmap.md`) asserted release/version status ("shipped in vX") on a project whose branching model explicitly keeps `main` out of scope for versioning, the exact same shape `session-discipline.md` § Git's "Say this in a reply, not into a persisted file" already named for staging state, just never generalized past staging. Sharpened during a real exchange: on a branch with no visibility into release status at all, asserting it isn't just a staleness risk, it's a scope violation, the branch never had standing to make the claim in the first place. Generalized the existing bullet to cover release/publish status too, and added an explicit exception: `CHANGELOG.md`'s own `## Unreleased changes` heading isn't the same violation, it's the canonical, actively-maintained source of that fact, not a driftable duplicate of one tracked elsewhere, the exact distinction the rule is actually about. `breaking: no`
- `2026-08-07 | conventions | minor | no | atlas-gaps-found-on-review`: asked to review the atlas/roadmap-split feature just shipped for completeness, found three real gaps rather than confirming it was done. (1) The `roadmap_split` clause said turning it on wasn't a retroactive requirement, which solves nothing for the actual complaint that motivated it, an already-overwhelming existing roadmap; added that flipping the flag is a migration, sweep and relocate existing dense entries in the same pass, the same "cutover, not an addition" lesson already learned for the `.dev/sessions/` migration. (2) `definition-of-done.md`'s lessons-learned criterion routes substance into `.dev/docs` without ever mentioning the atlas's own index requirement, a second trigger writing into the same directory with no pointer to its maintenance rule; added a cross-reference. (3) The atlas section's "once it accumulates more than a couple of topics" framing left "do I need an index yet" as an unstated judgment call; now required from the first topic file, no threshold to judge. `breaking: no`
- `2026-08-07 | conventions | minor | no | cross-project-finding-wrong-destination`: an agent working in one project (informed by reading a related project's context, per the already-encouraged "cross-pollination" pattern) risked writing a finding that was actually about the *other* project into the current project's own devctx, simply because that's the repo open and writable in that session. `definition-of-done.md`'s existing "Where it goes: which project" bullet already defaulted to "usually this one" but never stated the actual test, or addressed this reverse case. Added it explicitly: the test is whose problem a finding is, not which project's context informed it; if the other project hasn't adopted agentics or has no devctx to write into, surface the finding to the developer directly rather than inventing an entry in the wrong project's files, the same "surface ideas unprompted" instinct already required elsewhere, and the same boundary `propagation-crosses-repo-boundary-uninvited` already enforces for agentics specifically, now stated for any project pair. `breaking: no`
- `2026-08-07 | conventions | minor | no | recheck-trigger-not-gated-on-commit`: a `.dev/roadmap.md` design-question entry described a feature as "not yet decided" well after the same session had already shipped it, fully specified, across five other files, caught only by a different agent reviewing the batch. `session-discipline.md`'s existing "Re-check before committing" rule describes this exact failure almost verbatim and still didn't fire: its trigger is anchored to the literal git commit action, which can be arbitrarily delayed in a long, uncommitted working session, exactly this one. Added: run the re-check at each natural completion point within a session, not only right before committing, which may be much later or never in the same session. `breaking: no`
- `2026-08-07 | conventions | minor | no | correction-is-lessons-learned-trigger`: prompted by the incident above and a direct question about closing the loop between live feedback and persisted convention. `review-conduct.md`'s "Push back, including on yourself" covered revising a stance when shown wrong, but stopped at the immediate fix, never connecting it to `definition-of-done.md`'s lessons-learned criterion. Added: being shown a mistake is itself a lessons-learned trigger, ask whether the underlying cause generalizes before moving on. Caught on a follow-up question and corrected before this shipped: the first version pointed only at "a convention" as the destination, when `definition-of-done.md` right above it already routes this three ways, a memory pointer, a `.dev/docs/atlas/` write-up, or a convention change, depending on scope; the addition now routes there instead of narrowing to one destination. `breaking: no`
- `2026-08-07 | conventions | minor | no | update-agentics-verb-ambiguous`: a live incident in a different adopting project (Arranger) confirmed the same ambiguity `AGENTS.md`'s Critical Constraints already guards against in the reverse direction: told to "update your agentics," an agent has no principled way to distinguish "update the upstream source" from "update how this project uses it" from the bare word alone. `upgrading-adoption.md`'s own trigger list only had the clearer, less-likely-to-be-said "upgrade this project's agentics integration," leaving the actual colloquial phrasing to an unspecified "(or similar)." Added the real phrasing explicitly, plus the disambiguation itself: read "update" as "fetch the latest and apply what's relevant to this project," cross-referencing the existing `AGENTS.md` framing rather than restating it. `breaking: no`
